                                                            3                               Economic and fiscal outlook
Economic and fiscal outlook   4
Foreword


This Economic and fiscal outlook (EFO) sets out our central forecast, alongside the risks and
uncertainties that surround it, for the five years to 2031-32. It takes account of recent data and other
developments, alongside government policies announced since our last forecast – up to and
including the October 2026 Budget. The forecasts presented in this document represent our view as
the three independent members of the OBR’s Budget Responsibility Committee (BRC). We take full
responsibility for the judgements that underpin them and for the conclusions we have reached.

As always, we have been greatly supported in our work by the staff of the OBR. We are very grateful
for their hard work and expertise. We have also drawn on the work and expertise of officials across
government in preparing these forecasts. We are grateful for their engagement and insight.

We present the economy forecast in calendar years and produce quarterly paths. The fiscal forecast
is typically presented on a financial year basis which runs from April to March. For the current fiscal
year, 2026-27, outturn data is incomplete and is, therefore, presented as an OBR forecast. Our
online detailed forecast tables, and charts and tables data files, detail the points where individual
forecast variables are outturn and forecasts.

On 20 July, we provided an assessment of the outlook for the economy and public finances to the
incoming Chancellor of the Exchequer, to support his initial preparations ahead of the Budget. This
assessment was subsequently used for the initial round of our pre-measures forecast. The date for
the Budget was then announced on 31 July, which provided more than the ten weeks’ notice
required by the Memorandum of understanding between the Office for Budget Responsibility, HM
Treasury, the Department for Work and Pensions and HM Revenue and Customs (MoU). After this, the
timetable for the production of the forecast proceeded as follows:

     •    Over the remainder of the summer, the OBR developed the pre-measures economic
          forecast, drawing on new data releases and other information available during this
          period. At various points, using the economic determinants from these forecasts (such as
          the components of nominal income and spending, unemployment, inflation, and interest
          rates), we commissioned updated forecasts from the relevant government departments for
          the various tax and spending items that in aggregate determine the position of the public
          finances. We discussed these in detail with the officials producing them, which allowed us
          to investigate proposed changes in forecasting methodology and to assess the
          significance of recent tax and spending outturn data.

     •    The pre-measures economy forecast was completed on 8 September and was based on
          financial and energy market data averaged over the 10 working days to 19 August. This is
          a longer gap between the closure of the pre-measures economy forecast and publication
          of the final forecast than is set out within the MoU, and was requested by the Treasury to


                                                   5                       Economic and fiscal outlook
Foreword




           allow for greater policy decision-making time after receiving the final pre-measures
           forecast. We agreed to this change on the basis that it would also provide the OBR with
           greater time to effectively scrutinise the economic and fiscal impact of policy decisions,
           and to finalise and quality assure our analysis. We will review the effectiveness of this
           change with the Treasury ahead of our next forecast.

    •      Once completed, the pre-measures economy forecast was sent to government
           departments to produce individual tax and spending forecasts and they returned them on
           the same day. These fed into a final pre-measures fiscal forecast that was completed on
           18 September. The fiscal forecast is consistent with the ONS Public sector finances (PSF)
           release on 21 August, including ONS estimates of revisions that would arise in the 22
           September release, as well as management information on tax receipts for August. In line
           with recent practice, we took a later window for interest rates in the fiscal forecast, over
           the five working days to 15 September. This results in very minor discrepancies between the
           economy and fiscal forecast which we judge to be immaterial.

    •      The final pre-measures economic and fiscal forecast was sent to the Chancellor on 18
           September. At the request of the Treasury, this was the only pre-measures economic and
           fiscal forecast round that was shared with them during this forecast. This represented a
           change from the process followed in recent years, when we shared interim rounds of the
           pre-measures economic and fiscal forecasts with the Treasury as they were completed. No
           further material adjustments were made to our economy or fiscal forecasts after this, other
           than to take account of the impact of policy measures provided by the Treasury. We
           always reserve the right to reopen our pre-measures forecasts in the event of exceptional
           circumstances which lead to a significant and structural change in the economic and fiscal
           outlook. We did not judge that to be the case at this event.

    •      In parallel to the production of the forecast, we scrutinised the costing of a number of
           individual tax and spending measures announced since the March 2026 forecast. As
           usual, OBR staff and the BRC requested further information and/or changes to many of
           the draft costings prepared by HMRC, DWP and other departments.

    •      We made an initial assessment of the economic and fiscal effects of the emerging Budget
           policy package that was provided by the Treasury on 25 September. We incorporated this
           package into an initial post-measures forecast, to provide an early view on the effect of
           policy measures on the economy and public finances, which we sent to the Chancellor on
           2 October. This forecast round was produced using our internal ready-reckoner models
           rather than being sent to departmental forecasters.

    •      In line with the agreed timetable, on 9 October the Treasury provided the final package of
           measures that could cause movements in our economy forecast. We sent the resulting
           final economy forecast to the Treasury on 16 October and a near-final fiscal forecast on
           20 October. Final policy decisions, that would only affect the fiscal forecast, were returned
           by the Treasury on 20 October, and our forecast was then finalised and sent to the
           Treasury on 22 October.



Economic and fiscal outlook                        6
                                                                                                  Foreword




The Treasury made a written request, as provided for in the MoU between us, that we provide the
Chancellor and an agreed list of special advisers and officials with a near-final draft of the EFO on
22 October. This allowed the Treasury to prepare the Chancellor’s statement and accompanying
documents. We also provided pre-release access to the full and final EFO on 26 October.

The agreed forecast timetable was largely adhered to. Small changes were made to the parameters
of one policy measure following the costings certification deadline, meaning that, although we have
no concerns about the methodology used, we have not been able to certify this costing. We have
used the Government’s estimates of its fiscal impact and will return to it at the next forecast. In
addition, two policy measures with direct impacts on our economy forecast were altered after it had
been finalised. Had we been able to incorporate these, they would have only made a small
difference to our final economy forecast.

During the forecasting period, the BRC held dozens of scrutiny and challenge meetings with officials
from other departments, in addition to numerous further meetings at staff level and with external
stakeholders. We have been provided with all the information and analysis that we requested and
have come under no pressure from Ministers, advisers, or officials to change any of our conclusions.
A full log of our substantive contact with Ministers, their offices, and special advisers since we
published our previous forecast in March 2026 can be found on our website. This includes the list of
special advisers and officials who received the near-final draft of the EFO on 22 October.

In this EFO, we present a detailed central forecast in line with the responsibility in our primary
legislation to assess the extent to which the fiscal mandate has been, or is likely to be, achieved. The
fiscal mandate is currently defined as a target in the third year of our forecast. This requires us to
produce a precise forecast for a set of very uncertain outcomes. For example, the borrowing forecast
represents the difference between over £1.5 trillion each of receipts and expenditure by the end of
the forecast period. We therefore also present a range of different indicators of uncertainty to
illustrate the potential effects of alternative scenarios, sensitivities and risks that inevitably lie around
the central forecast. For fiscal policy-makers, and those who scrutinise fiscal policy, we see this
analysis as being as important as the central forecast. The central forecast remains our best estimate
of the central outlook for the economy and public finances, within the constraints in which we are
required to make it.

We would be pleased to receive feedback on any aspect of the content or presentation of our
analysis. This can be sent to feedback@obr.uk.




  Professor Jonathan Haskel CBE          Professor David Miles CBE                 Tom Josephs

                                  The Budget Responsibility Committee




                                                     7                        Economic and fiscal outlook
Economic and fiscal outlook   8
1     Executive summary


Overview
1.1   The UK’s economic and fiscal position remains very challenging. Since 2008, repeated
      adverse shocks have contributed to persistently weak GDP growth and elevated levels of
      government borrowing. This has resulted in a sharp increase in government debt as a share
      of GDP, to a level that is high by both recent historical and international standards.

1.2   There have been further adverse developments since our March forecast, with both interest
      rates and energy prices rising significantly. As a net importer of energy, the rise in energy
      prices constitutes a negative terms-of-trade shock for the UK, which pushes up on inflation
      and lowers real household disposable incomes. In the central forecast – the mid-point of a
      wide range of possible outcomes – CPI inflation is 3½ per cent in 2027, compared to 2 per
      cent expected in March. Real GDP growth slows from around 1¼ per cent in 2026 to 1 per
      cent in 2027 – the latter around a ½ percentage point lower than the March forecast.
      Assuming energy prices subsequently decline in line with market pricing, inflation returns to
      2 per cent by 2028 and GDP returns to the level expected in March by 2030. After falling
      slightly in the near term, per person real household disposable income in 2030-31 is over
      ¾ per cent below our March forecast and only 2 per cent above its pre-pandemic level.

1.3   The rise in interest rates and increase in the inflation forecast leaves pre-measures
      borrowing an average of £11 billion per year higher than in March. The direct effects of
      policy measures further increase borrowing by £8 billion in 2026-27, £4 billion in 2027-28,
      and £0.4 billion in 2028-29, and then reduce borrowing by £7 billion, on average, in the
      years thereafter. Overall, while borrowing as a share of GDP is still forecast to decline over
      the forecast to just under 2 per cent of GDP, these changes slow the near-term pace of
      consolidation compared to previous plans. Public sector net debt peaks around 97 per cent
      of GDP in 2028-29, before falling slightly to 96 per cent in 2031-32. In the central forecast,
      the fiscal mandate for the current budget to be in surplus in 2029-30 is met by a margin of
      £15 billion. The supplementary target for public sector net financial liabilities (PSNFL) to be
      falling as a share of GDP in 2029-30 is met by a margin of £24 billion.

1.4   The outlook for the economy and public finances is uncertain, with significantly better or
      worse outcomes of broadly equal probability. If energy prices reach a higher and more
      sustained peak, borrowing could be £17 billion above the central forecast in 2027-28,
      while a lower energy price path could reduce borrowing by £12 billion. A one per cent
      change in interest rates could raise or reduce government debt interest costs by £16 billion.
      If productivity growth were 0.5 percentage points higher or lower than forecast, borrowing
      could reach £50 billion lower or £40 billion higher. Significant pressures on departmental
      spending include higher inflation, public sector pay, and policy ambitions such as defence.


                                                9                       Economic and fiscal outlook
Executive summary




Economic and fiscal context
1.5                        Adverse shocks have continued to hit the UK since the 2008 global financial crisis, including
                           rising barriers to trade after EU exit, the pandemic, and the 2022 energy price spike. These
                           shocks, alongside other structural trends, have resulted in weak growth in GDP per person,
                           periods of elevated inflation, and low growth in real household disposable incomes. On top
                           of these, since we finalised our March forecast, there has been another spike in energy
                           prices and a significant rise in interest rates. Gilt yields have returned closer to their long-run
                           averages following a period of exceptionally low rates after the financial crisis.

1.6                        These adverse shocks and low economic growth have resulted in elevated levels of
                           government borrowing and debt in the UK. Chart 1.1 shows that UK borrowing has
                           followed a similar trend to the G7 and advanced economy average over the past two
                           decades but at a consistently higher level. As a result, UK government debt as a share of
                           GDP has increased by more than in any other G7 economy, rising from 38 per cent in
                           2006 to reach 93 per cent in 2026. The cost of servicing this debt has also increased
                           sharply recently – from 1.7 per cent of GDP (£39 billion) in 2019-20 to 3.5 per cent of GDP
                           (£109 billion) in 2025-26. In the IMF's forecasts, UK borrowing falls to close to the
                           advanced economy average by 2031, which would lead to debt stabilising at around the
                           G7 average.

                                                                                                                               Executive summary




Economic outlook
1.7                     Energy prices have increased sharply since our last forecast in March. The left panel of
                        Chart 1.2 shows that market pricing, at the time that our forecast was conditioned, implied
                        that oil prices would fall from their peak of 95 dollars a barrel in the second quarter of this
                        year to just above 70 dollars a barrel by late 2028 and remain around that level thereafter.
                        The right panel shows that gas prices were expected to peak at around 150 pence a therm
                        at the end of this year before falling back to around 70 pence a therm from mid-2028.
                        Compared with the March forecast, market pricing for oil and gas is significantly higher in
                        the near term, by 30 and 80 per cent respectively, but in our central forecast they are only
                        around 10 per cent higher in 2030. Oil and gas futures have increased further since we
                        took market expectations – at the time of finalising this report, they were close to the top of
                        the range since the beginning of the Middle East conflict. Given the uncertainty around the
                        outlook for energy prices, we have produced scenarios assessing the potential economic
                        and fiscal implications of alternative paths for these in Chapter 7.

1.8                     The future market-implied path of Bank Rate and gilt yields has also increased significantly
                        since our March forecast (Chart 1.3). Our central economy forecast is conditioned on Bank
                        Rate reaching just over 4¼ per cent by mid-2027 and just over 4½ per cent by the end of
                        the forecast period, around ¾ and ½ a percentage point higher than our March forecast,
                        respectively. In our fiscal forecast, which was finalised later than the economy forecast, Bank
                        Rate is a further ¼ percentage point higher on average. Gilt yields have risen substantially
                        across all maturities since our March forecast.

1.9                As Chart 1.4 shows, in our central forecast, higher energy prices contribute to CPI inflation
                   increasing to a quarterly peak of 3¾ per cent in early 2027. It is then forecast to fall to 2
                   per cent in 2028 as energy prices drop back. Inflation is about ¾ and 1½ percentage
                   points higher in 2026 and 2027, respectively, than the March forecast and returns to target
                   around one year later. This reflects the direct effect of higher energy prices on fuel prices
                   and utilities, the indirect effect of energy prices as an input into the cost of other products,
                   and some second-round effects of higher inflation through wage growth. But we judge that
                   these second-round effects will be less inflationary than those following the previous energy
                   price spike in 2022, as a significantly looser labour market restricts workers’ capacity to
                   bargain for higher wages. This means that nominal wage growth in the central forecast is
                   only around ½ and ¾ percentage points higher in 2026 and 2027, respectively, than in
                   March.

1.10               The outlook for inflation is highly uncertain, including the extent to which energy prices
                   generate second-round effects through wages. This is important for the fiscal forecast given
                   the high effective tax rate on labour income and frozen income tax thresholds. Based purely
                   on past forecast errors, Chart 1.4 suggests that there is a one-in-five chance that CPI
                   inflation in 2027 will be above 4½ per cent and a similar probability that it will be below
                   2½ per cent.

1.11               In the central forecast, limited second-round effects of externally driven inflation on nominal
                   wages – and labour market looseness – means we expect no real earnings growth in 2027.
                   Real wage growth then recovers to around ½ a per cent a year by 2030, as energy prices
                   drop back. That medium-term growth rate is slightly below growth in output per worker as
                   we expect firms to rebuild profit margins and the rate of return on capital to recover.
                   Subdued real wage growth, alongside the combined effect of frozen income tax thresholds
                   and higher nominal earnings growth, means we expect RHDI per person – an indicator of
                   living standards – to fall slightly in the near term (Chart 1.5). RHDI per person then partially
                   recovers as the energy price shock subsides. But, in 2030-31, it is only around 2 per cent
                   above pre-pandemic levels and is about ¾ per cent below the level from our March forecast.

                                                                          13                                           Economic and fiscal outlook
Executive summary




1.12   Our central estimate of the level of potential output in the medium term is broadly
       unchanged from March. Recent GDP growth has been stronger than we expected – and we
       judge this to be structural – raising the starting level of potential output. We also have a
       slightly higher forecast for business investment – which raises capital deepening and
       productivity growth a little. Offsetting this, we expect net migration to be 70,000 lower on
       average per year, which has a small negative impact on our potential output forecast. But
       we judge that these migrants would have had broadly the same labour market
       characteristics as the domestic population, so the migration change has no impact on
       potential output on a per person basis. The higher starting point for potential output and the
       increased capital deepening forecast means that our central forecast for GDP per person
       has been revised up slightly in the medium term relative to March.

1.13   Our forecast for growth in total factor productivity in the medium term is unchanged relative
       to our March forecast. This is a highly uncertain judgement – and there is even uncertainty
       around the historical growth rates, which we explore further in Box 2.2. We have also
       assessed the impact of the Employment Rights Act 2025 in this forecast. We judge that while
       it will likely have a negative impact on potential output, the size of the effect is too uncertain
       to make an explicit adjustment at this forecast.

1.14   We expect real GDP growth of around 1¼ per cent this year – slightly higher than in March
       as the UK economy has, so far, been resilient to higher energy prices. We anticipate growth
       of around 1 per cent in 2027 – down about ½ a percentage point from March – as it takes
       time for higher global energy prices and increased interest rates to weigh on activity. Based
       on energy prices falling back, as suggested by the market pricing that underpins our
       forecast, we expect GDP growth to then recover and for the level of GDP to be broadly
       unchanged from our March forecast by 2030. Over the forecast, average annual real GDP
       growth is largely unchanged from March at around 1½ per cent. We judge the risks around
       our central forecast are broadly balanced. Looking at the distribution of historical forecast
       errors shown in Chart 1.6, in 2027, there is a one-in-five chance that real GDP growth
       could be higher than 2¼ per cent and a similar chance that it contracts.

1.15            In the central forecast, net additions to the housing stock fall from around 230,000 in
                2025-26 to 220,000 in 2026-27, before increasing to 260,000 in 2030-31. Compared to
                March, we expect 190,000 fewer cumulative net additions over the forecast period. We
                have not revised our judgement that the 2024 planning reforms will have a positive impact
                on housebuilding. Instead, this change is driven by wider pressures in the construction sector
                because of weak demand, rising material costs and the impact of other regulatory changes.

1.16            We expect cumulative growth in the GDP deflator over the forecast to be around 1
                percentage point higher than in March. This is due to higher energy prices received by
                domestic producers of oil and gas, and the second-round effects that higher inflation has
                through wage growth. This is the main driver of our upward revision to our central forecast
                for cumulative growth in nominal GDP over the forecast period – which is 1¼ percentage
                points higher than March. This drives higher growth in all of the main tax bases – labour
                income, corporate profits and nominal consumption.

Fiscal outlook
1.17            Relative to March, there is a moderate deterioration in the medium-term fiscal outlook,
                primarily due to the impact of higher debt interest costs. Public sector net borrowing is
                expected to be higher compared to March in every year of the forecast – by around £18
                billion this year, £16 billion in 2027-28, and an average of £11 billion a year thereafter. As
                shown in Chart 1.7, the main drivers of the change in borrowing since March are:

                •      Debt interest spending in the pre-measures forecast is expected to be £15 billion
                       higher this year, driven by increased inflation raising the cost of index-linked gilts. Debt


                                                                     15                               Economic and fiscal outlook

                         interest is then forecast to be £12 billion higher on average in the medium term,
                         reflecting increased forecasts for gilt yields and Bank Rate.

                  •      Other areas of current public spending are £8 billion higher on average in the pre-
                         measures forecast, including from higher inflation raising welfare spending and the
                         cost of public service pensions. Capital spending items that are higher than forecast in
                         March include departmental spending in 2026-27, and over the medium term local
                         government and public corporations’ capital expenditure.

                  •      Upwards revisions to the pre-measures receipts forecast leave it £17 billion higher on
                         average compared to March, largely driven by increased income tax and NICs, and
                         corporation tax. This reflects higher inflation leading to upward revisions to the
                         forecasts for nominal earnings and profits, respectively, alongside higher-than-
                         expected receipts outturn so far this year.

                  •      The direct effects of policy measures increase borrowing by £8 billion in 2026-27, £4
                         billion in 2027-28, and £0.4 billion in 2028-29, and then reduce borrowing by £4
                         billion in 2029-30, £5 billion in 2030-31, and £12 billion in 2031-32. In the near

                         reduction in borrowing is from a combination of tax increases and a reduction to the
                         pre-measures spending assumption in 2031-32. The indirect effects of policy
                         measures increase borrowing by a further £2 billion on average per year.

                  •      The changes to the forecast for the current deficit compared to March are very similar
                         to the changes to borrowing, particularly in the medium term (Chart 1.7). The largest
                         difference is in 2026-27 where a £12 billion forecast increase in capital spending, in
                         large part due to departments spending more of their capital budgets than expected,
                         does not affect the current budget. As a result, the current deficit is forecast to be only
                         £7 billion higher than in March in 2026-27.

1.18                  As a result of these changes, our central forecast is for borrowing to fall from 4.4 per cent
                      of GDP (£134 billion) in 2025-26 to 4.2 per cent of GDP (£133 billion) this year. It is then
                      expected to fall steadily over the medium term to reach 1.8 per cent of GDP (£67 billion) in
                      2030-31 and remain broadly unchanged thereafter (Chart 1.8). The outlook for borrowing
                      is particularly uncertain as it represents the difference between around £1.5 trillion of both
                      tax and spending by the end of the forecast period. Based on the distribution of historical
                      shocks, there is a 60 per cent chance of PSNB being between a surplus of 1.0 per cent of
                      GDP (£38 billion) and a deficit of 4.4 per cent of GDP (£168 billion) in 2031-32.

1.19                  The right panel of Chart 1.8 shows that since 2022 plans to consolidate borrowing have
                      been repeatedly delayed. This is due to a combination of subsequent unexpected shocks,
                      lower-than-expected GDP growth, and discretionary policy choices to delay fiscal
                      consolidation. Borrowing has now declined from around 5 per cent of GDP between 2022-
                      23 and 2024-25, to 4.4 per cent of GDP in 2025-26. It is forecast to decline further to 4.2
                      per cent of GDP this year, and in the current central forecast is set to decline to just under 2
                      per cent of GDP in 2030-31. However, in the near-term the pace of this consolidation has
                      again slowed, with borrowing between 2026-27 and 2028-29 now forecast to be around
                      0.5 per cent of GDP a year higher on average than forecast in November 2025. If the
                      current forecast for borrowing to fall to around 2 per cent of GDP in 2029-30 is achieved, it
                      would be 2 years later than forecast in November 2022.

1.21                   In the pre-measures forecast, borrowing as a share of GDP starts to rise slightly from 1.9
                       per cent in 2030-31 to 2 per cent in 2031-32. This unusual feature is because some of the
                       long-term pressures that we identified in the 2026 Fiscal risks and sustainability report (FRS)
                       are already starting to bite. The increase reflects a combination of an increased pensioner
                       population placing upward pressure on welfare spending, declining receipts from fuel duty
                       due to the transition to electric vehicles, and increased spending on debt interest driven by
                       significant redemptions of gilts at higher interest rates. This rise has been fully offset by
                       policy measures announced by the Government at this Budget so that in the post-measures
                       forecast borrowing as a share of GDP is flat between 2030-31 and 2031-32.

1.22                   In the post-measures forecast, public sector net debt rises from 96 per cent of GDP in this
                       year to peak at 97 per cent of GDP in 2028-29. It then falls back to 96 per cent in 2031-
                       32, the final year of the forecast. This would be 0.6 per cent of GDP higher than in the final
                       year of the March 2026 forecast. Net debt excluding the Bank of England is forecast to rise
                       from 92 per cent of GDP this year to stabilise around 94 per cent in the final three years of
                       the forecast. Debt on either measure is forecast to be no lower in 2031-32 than in the
                       current year. Public sector net financial liabilities (PSNFL) are forecast to rise from 83 per

Performance against the Government’s fiscal targets
1.23                   In the central forecast, the current budget deficit declines from 1.5 per cent of GDP (£47
                       billion) in 2025-26 to 1.3 per cent (£41 billion) this year (Chart 1.11). It is then forecast to
                       improve gradually to reach a surplus of 0.4 per cent of GDP (£15 billion) in 2029-30, and
                       then to stabilise at around 0.6 per cent on average in 2030-31 and 2031-32 (at £22 and
                       £21 billion, respectively).

1.24                   As a result, the Government’s fiscal mandate for the current budget to be in surplus in
                       2029-30 is met by a margin of £15 billion. This is £7 billion lower than when the fiscal
                       rules were last formally assessed at Budget 2025. The probability of meeting the fiscal
                       mandate is now estimated at 56 per cent, which compares to a probability of 59 per cent at
                       Budget 2025. For the probability of meeting the fiscal mandate to be 60 or 70 per cent, it
                       would require the current budget to be in surplus by around £25 and £50 billion
                       respectively, in 2029-30.

1.25                   The supplementary target, for public sector net financial liabilities to be falling as a share of
                       GDP in 2029-30, is forecast to be met by a margin of £24 billion. This is unchanged
                       relative to Budget 2025, when the fiscal targets were last assessed. For the probability of
                       meeting the supplementary target to be 60 or 70 per cent, it would require PSNFL to be
                       falling by 1.8 or 3.9 per cent of GDP in 2029-30 respectively, instead of the current forecast
                       margin of a fall of 0.7 per cent of GDP.

Risks and uncertainties
1.26                   These margins against the fiscal targets are very small compared to the inherent risk and
                       uncertainty around any medium-term fiscal forecast. The central forecast is an estimate of
                       the middle of a range of possible outcomes, which means outturns substantially below and
                       above our central forecast are likely and of broadly equal probability. Small percentage
                       changes in the forecast for either tax or spending can lead to large percentage changes in
                       borrowing. Since the pandemic, the average revision to pre-measures borrowing in the third
                       year of the forecast has been £18 billion. The median three-year-ahead difference between
                       forecast and outturn, since 2010, has been £34 billion. The margins against the fiscal
                       mandate set out above are below the average margin of close to £30 billion held by
                       Chancellors since 2010.

1.27                   Key risks to our economy forecast include:

                       •      The evolution of the conflict in the Middle East, and its implications for energy prices,
                              interest rates and inflation. Energy markets remain volatile – at the time of finalising
                              this report, oil and gas futures pricing was close to the top of the range observed since
                              the beginning of the Middle East conflict. Scenario analysis in Chapter 7 illustrates that
                              if energy prices were to peak at a higher level and remain elevated for longer, we
                              would expect increased inflation, weaker near-term GDP growth and a potential
                              tightening of financial conditions. This could raise borrowing by as much as £17
                              billion in 2027-28, and by £6 billion in 2031-32, alongside an erosion of real-terms
                              departmental spending power. If energy prices return to our March forecast path, we
                              would expect more rapid disinflation, stronger near-term GDP growth and a potential
                              easing of financial conditions – which could result in a decrease in borrowing of
                              around £7 billion by 2031-32. The fiscal impacts would be particularly dependent on


Economic and fiscal outlook                                          20
                                                                                  Executive summary




            changes in financial conditions, the extent of pass-through of an inflation shock to
            wages given tax thresholds are currently frozen, and any policy response to inflationary
            pressures on departmental spending.

       •    The outlook for productivity growth is a key risk to the economic and fiscal forecasts.
            The development and adoption of artificial intelligence could have a significant
            positive effect – our central forecast is for it to boost productivity growth by 0.2
            percentage points a year by the medium term – but the scale and timing of any effect
            is highly uncertain. Revised ONS data on average hours, discussed in Box 2.2, suggest
            that UK productivity growth slowed after the financial crisis, but that the magnitude of
            the slowdown was closer to international comparators than previously suggested. In
            the November 2025 EFO, we illustrated that if annual productivity growth were 0.5
            percentage points higher or lower than our central forecast in the medium term,
            borrowing could be around £50 billion lower or £40 billion higher by 2030-31.

       •    There are several sources of uncertainty around the labour market outlook. There is
            significant uncertainty around the size and composition of future migration flows, given
            the post-Brexit change in the migration regime and the recent spike and subsequent
            fall in net migration. And there are risks around the outlook for unemployment. In the
            March EFO, we produced scenarios on the fiscal implications of alternative paths for
            the labour market. These suggested that if unemployment returned to its estimated
            equilibrium rate two years earlier than the central forecast, borrowing could be £16
            billion a year lower on average, or if it instead peaked at around 7 per cent,
            borrowing could be an average of £20 billion a year higher than our central forecast.

1.28   Key additional risks to the fiscal forecast include:

       •    The cost of servicing government debt – which had already increased sharply since
            2021, before rising further in recent months following the start of the current Middle
            East conflict. Debt interest spending has risen from £39 billion (1.7 per cent of GDP) in
            2019-20 to £109 billion (3.5 per cent of GDP) in 2025-26. It is now forecast to rise to
            £160 billion (4.2 per cent of GDP) in 2031-32. Gilt yields have recently been volatile,
            with the ten-year yield ranging from around 4.5 per cent to 5.5 per cent since March.
            But yields within this range are not unusual by historical standards. A 1 percentage
            point change in gilt yields and Bank Rate expectations across the forecast could lead to
            a medium-term change in borrowing of £16 billion.

       •    There are a number of pressures on departmental spending. Since the Spending
            Review envelope was set, higher inflation has reduced the real value of the original
            spending limits by £7 billion each year. There are further pressures in the later years of
            the forecast for which departmental spending limits have not been set. Based on the
            Government’s assumption for total spending over this period – after taking into
            account policy commitments in areas including the NHS, defence and schools –
            spending would have to be £11 billion higher in 2031-32 to prevent ‘unprotected’
            areas of day-to-day spending from falling in real terms from 2028-29.


                                                  21                     Economic and fiscal outlook
Executive summary




             In the longer term, there are further pressures on public spending plans, including
             from the Government’s commitment to reform the social care system and to spend 3.5
             per cent of GDP on defence by 2035.

       •     There is also uncertainty around the outlook for welfare spending, especially related to
             health and disability benefits which have seen very significant rises in caseloads since
             the pandemic. In the central forecast, we assume that caseloads will continue to rise
             but at a slower rate. If they were to continue to rise at the post-pandemic growth rate,
             this could add around £25 billion to welfare spending by 2031-32. Or if caseload
             growth were to slow to pre-pandemic rates, then spending could be around £13
             billion lower by 2031-32.

       •     The tax-to-GDP ratio is expected to surpass its historical high of 37.2 per cent in 2027-
             28 and continue increasing in every year of the forecast. A higher level of the tax take
             increases the risk that incentives within the tax system constrain economic activity by
             more than expected. The single largest driver of the increase in the tax-GDP-ratio is
             from rising income tax and NICs which are highly sensitive to growth in nominal
             earnings, especially with personal tax thresholds frozen. Increased revenue from
             capital taxes is also a key component of the increase but the expected yield is highly
             sensitive to both equity prices and uncertain behavioural responses from affected
             taxpayers. The forecast also assumes the tax gap – a measure of tax compliance – will
             decline over the forecast period following a recent period where it has remained
             broadly flat.

1.29   The Government has taken some steps to address previously identified fiscal risks. The
       recently-announced reforms to the triple lock should significantly reduce the projected
       increase in the cost of state pension uprating over the long term relative to the previous
       policy. This is because the reforms remove the ‘ratchet’ effect whereby any increase in the
       value of pensions relative to earnings are permanently embedded and compound over
       time. However, alongside this reform, the Government also announced an intention to
       expand state provision of social care. The overall impact of these announcements on the
       public finances over the long term, therefore, depends on the additional cost of the social
       care reforms which is dependent on the final policy design and the timing of its introduction.
       In addition, the fiscal mandate now targets the third year of the forecast. This means that,
       from the next spending review, detailed spending allocations will be required for the year in
       which the fiscal targets bind which should reduce the risks around meeting these targets.

1.30   In practice, uncertainties around the central forecast are likely to be different to those in the
       past and go beyond the ones described above. As recent years show, there is a significant
       risk of unforeseeable adverse shocks having a large negative impact on the economy and
       public finances. Any such shocks over the forecast period, during which debt is forecast to
       remain elevated at approximately 95 per cent of GDP, would place further pressure on the
       UK’s fiscal sustainability.




Economic and fiscal outlook                       22
                                                                                                              Executive summary




1.31                    Our 2026 FRS showed that fiscal pressures are likely to build over the long term. The drivers
                        of this included an ageing population, wider health cost pressures, commitments to increase
                        defence spending, and the fiscal costs of the transition to net zero. Long-term projections
                        such as these are highly uncertain. However, in nearly all the scenarios we produced,
                        including with different assumptions for long-term policy, debt would move on to an
                        unsustainable upward trajectory within the next couple of decades unless policy action was
                        taken to prevent this.

1.32                    In the baseline long-term scenario in the FRS, debt moved to an unsustainable upward
                        trajectory in the 2040s (Chart 1.12). This was based on the plans for fiscal consolidation in
                        the March 2026 forecast, in which the primary balance – a measure that excludes debt
                        interest spending – was projected to be in surplus by 1.5 per cent of GDP by 2030-31. If
                        achieved, this would be the largest primary surplus since 2000-01. As set out above, since
                        2022, successive governments have set out similar plans for medium-term consolidation
                        which have not subsequently been achieved. If we assume that the primary deficit in 2030-
                        31 was at the same level as in 2025-26, then the unsustainable upward trajectory of debt
                        would start in the 2030s. This underlines the importance for fiscal sustainability of delivering
                        the current planned medium-term consolidation.

2     Economic outlook


Summary of the economy forecast
2.1   The UK’s economic position remains challenging. A series of major adverse shocks since the
      global financial crisis, interacting with underlying structural trends, have contributed to
      persistently weak growth in per person GDP, episodes of sustained high inflation and
      subdued growth in real household disposable incomes. A broad range of factors, many of
      which originate outside the UK, will influence the UK’s future economic performance.
      Further adverse shocks may prolong or worsen recent weak growth, while stronger
      productivity growth, for example from advances in artificial intelligence (AI), may offset or
      reverse recent trends.

2.2   We finalised our March 2026 forecast just before the start of the current conflict in the
      Middle East. That forecast was conditioned on market pricing for Bank Rate to be cut twice
      by the end of this year. We expected inflation to fall back to around 2 per cent by the end of
      this year and GDP growth to rise to around 1½ per cent in 2027. Since then, energy prices
      have risen substantially – though by less than following the Russian invasion of Ukraine. As
      the UK is a net importer of energy, this constitutes a negative terms-of-trade shock and has
      already raised CPI inflation. While real GDP growth over the year to date has been more
      resilient than expected, the labour market remains weak, with job vacancies continuing to
      fall and some data suggesting an ongoing drop in the number of payrolled employees.
      Market pricing suggests interest rates will be significantly higher than anticipated in March.

2.3   Table 2.1 summarises the key conditioning assumptions and judgements which underpin
      our latest central forecast. Our central forecast for real GDP growth in 2027 is 1 per cent –
      ½ a percentage point below our March forecast, as a result of higher energy prices and
      interest rates. We expect CPI inflation to peak at close to 4 per cent in early 2027, driven
      largely by the direct and indirect effects of higher energy prices, with relatively modest
      second-round effects through earnings. We expect no growth in real earnings in 2027 and
      2028, and a slight fall in real household disposable income (RHDI) per person in the near
      term. Market pricing suggests energy prices will fall sharply from mid-2027. This would
      contribute to inflation falling back to its 2 per cent target in our central forecast by 2028,
      and to the level of real GDP in 2030 being broadly unchanged from our March forecast.
      But we expect RHDI per person to continue its recent trend of weak growth such that in
      2029-30, it is only expected to be 1 per cent higher than its pre-pandemic level.

2.4   Our central economy forecast is a point estimate within a wide range of possible outcomes.
      It is also a conditional forecast based on market pricing in mid-August for current and future
      energy prices and interest rates. Energy prices have been highly volatile and, at the point at
      which we finalised this Economic and fiscal outlook (EFO) publication, had increased


                                                25                      Economic and fiscal outlook
Economic outlook




          significantly compared to those levels. Therefore, we set out alternative energy price
          scenarios in Chapter 7 to assess the impact that higher or lower energy prices could have
          for the economic and fiscal outlook. We also discuss a wide range of other risks around our
          central forecast throughout this EFO, including through the use of fan charts of probability
          ranges and sensitivity analysis. One of our most important and most uncertain judgements
          is around the outlook for productivity growth. Box 2.2 explores the recent ONS revisions to
          historical productivity, which show that even the recent evolution of productivity growth is
          highly uncertain.

Conditioning assumptions
2.5       We conditioned our economy forecast on market pricing over the 10 working days to 19
          August. The final pre-measures fiscal forecast is produced after the pre-measures economy
          forecast, enabling us to use a later reading for interest rates in the fiscal forecast – based on
          the five working days to 15 September. Below we describe the conditioning assumptions
          used in the economy forecast, consistent with the earlier window, and briefly explain where
          the fiscal assumptions differ.

Energy prices
2.6       As Chart 2.1 illustrates, wholesale energy prices have increased significantly since our
          March forecast, largely as a result of the conflict in the Middle East. Energy prices have also
          been volatile over this period, with Brent crude spot prices ranging between 72 and 114

Economic and fiscal outlook                                    26
                                                                                                                              Economic outlook




                        dollars a barrel (with the highs close to the 2022 peak) and UK natural gas spot prices
                        ranging between 97 and 216 pence a therm (still significantly below the 2022 peak).

2.7                     Our central forecast, shown in Chart 2.1, is based on market pricing over the 10 working
                        days to 19 August. Based on those prices, over the forecast period:

                        •     Oil prices fall from a peak of 95 dollars a barrel in the second quarter of this year to
                              between 70 and 75 dollars a barrel by 2028, and remain around that level thereafter.
                              Compared to our March forecast, oil prices are 30 per cent higher in 2026 and 2027
                              and around 8 per cent higher in 2030.

                        •     Natural gas prices reach around 150 pence a therm at the end of this year, more than
                              double our March forecast but significantly below the 2022 peak. They then fall to
                              around 95 pence a therm by late 2027 and average just under 70 pence a therm
                              from mid-2028 (12 per cent higher than the March forecast).

                        •     Electricity prices peak at around 125 pounds a megawatt hour at the end of this year
                              (around 65 per cent higher than in the March forecast) before falling to under 70
                              pounds per megawatt hour from mid-2028 (5 per cent below our March forecast). The
                              reduction in medium-term electricity prices since March, despite the rise in medium-
                              term gas prices, illustrates their continued gradual decoupling, discussed in Box 2.1.

2.8                     Oil and gas prices have increased further since we took market expectations on the 10
                        working days to 19 August, and remain volatile. Since the start of the conflict in the Middle
                        East, futures prices for 2027 have ranged between 70 and 95 dollars a barrel for oil and
                        75 and 180 pence a therm for gas. At the time of finalising this report, oil and gas futures
                        were close to the top end of this range. We set out scenarios which assess the impact of
                        higher and lower oil and gas prices for the economic and fiscal outlook in Chapter 7.

 Box 2.1: The impact of energy price shocks on economic activity
 Oil and gas prices have risen significantly since the start of the current conflict in the Middle East.
 This comes four years after the spike in oil and particularly gas prices following the Russian
 invasion of Ukraine in 2022. The impact of an energy price shock on UK economic activity
 depends on a range of factors beyond the change in wholesale prices – including longer-term
 trends in the UK’s energy mix and energy intensity, the UK’s dependence on energy imports, the
 pass-through of wholesale prices to those paid by firms and households, and underlying
 economic conditions.
 Chart A shows the annual nominal change in fossil fuel prices weighted by the UK’s changing
 relative total consumption of coal, oil, and natural gas over time. Fossil fuel prices rose by 83
 per cent in the year to September 2026, just under a third of the peak rate seen in 2022, and
 less than half of that seen in the 1973 shock.a There is some evidence that ample pre-shock
 supply and inventories, supply responses elsewhere, and reduced global demand – especially
 from China – have helped cushion the initial price shock in 2026.b
 Chart A: Fossil fuel price inflation
                              350

 of global energy and food supply chains, Bank of England, June 2026. The last data point on the chart is for September 2026.
 Source: Bloomberg, DESNZ, World Bank, OBR

 Structural changes in the UK’s energy usage influence the impact of energy price shocks on
 economic activity.c Since the 1970s:
                               •    The UK’s energy mix has become more diverse, with the share of energy consumption
                                    from fossil fuels falling from 96 per cent in 1970 to 75 per cent in 2025 (Chart B, left
                                    panel). Coal has been nearly eliminated as an energy source and replaced by a
                                    combination of renewables and gas, with the latter’s supply disrupted by both the 2022
                                    and 2026 shocks. In the long term, greater electrification of transport and heating is
                                    expected to shift direct energy demand further from oil and gas, and towards the
                                    increasingly renewable inputs used to produce electricity.d



Economic and fiscal outlook                                                28
                                                                                                                                                            Economic outlook




                                        •    The UK’s energy intensity has fallen significantly, with energy consumption per unit of
                                             real GDP now one quarter of its value in 1970 (Chart B, right panel). All else equal, this
                                             reduces the economy-wide impact of a given energy price shock. Reduced energy
                                             intensity reflects improvements in energy efficiency and changes in the composition of
                                             economic activity.
                                        •    The UK’s reliance on energy imports has varied significantly. The right panel of Chart B
                                             illustrates how North Sea production turned the UK from a substantial net importer in the
                                             1970s into a net exporter, before declining production reversed this from the mid-2000s.
                                             This drives the size and sign of the terms-of-trade shock when international prices vary,
                                             and therefore the scale of the effect on real incomes.

energy imports are measured as a percentage of inland energy consumption of primary fuels plus marine bunkers. The latest data in

The pass-through of fossil fuel prices to electricity prices depends in part on marginal pricing,
because the wholesale electricity price is set by the last and most expensive generator needed to
meet demand. In the UK, this is often relatively expensive gas-fired generation, so higher gas
prices can feed through to wholesale electricity prices even when most generation does not
depend on gas. This link has weakened recently as the share of renewable generation has
increased.e Furthermore, wholesale electricity and gas costs were only expected to make up 47
per cent of the average domestic consumer’s bill in the fourth quarter of 2026,f with the
remainder from a combination of taxes, levies, and other costs. Consumers have been shielded
from higher prices in recent years by bespoke government support and fuel duty not being
uprated. Government support in 2022-23 totalled £52.2 billion.g
Underlying economic conditions also play a significant role in the ultimate economic impact of
an energy price shock. Chart C illustrates that the labour market was considerably looser at the
start of 2026 than at the start of 2022, with the vacancies-to-unemployment ratio at less than
half of its 2022 level. Our estimated negative output gap of -1.0 per cent of potential output at



                                                                                     29                                                          Economic and fiscal outlook
Economic outlook




 the start of 2026, compared to a positive output gap of 2.1 per cent at the start of 2022, implies
 a much greater degree of spare capacity in the economy. Monetary policy was also looser in
 2022, when Bank Rate was initially 0.5 per cent compared to 3.75 per cent. The 2022 shock
 also came when inflation was already high and rising, amid severe global supply chain
 disruption as the global economy rebalanced after the pandemic. Relative to 2022, these factors
 should reduce the risk of the current shock propagating strongly through wages and creating
 significant domestically generated inflation. However, the scale of second-round effects remains
 uncertain: for example, inflation expectations may now be more responsive to energy and food
 price movements after several years of above-target inflation.h

 Note: Shaded areas represent the historical energy price shocks from Dhingra, S., Running on empty: Climate risks and the fragility of

 average.
 Source: ONS, OBR

 As the UK is a net importer of energy, the recent rise in global energy prices represents a
 negative terms-of-trade shock that will lower real incomes. But this latest shock is smaller than
 the 2022 episode and there is more spare capacity in the economy, which should reduce the
 peak and persistence of inflation. The size and limited duration of the shock, declining fossil-fuel
 exposure, and the decoupling of electricity and gas prices together mean we do not expect a
 permanent hit to potential output from the energy price paths conditioning our forecast.

Interest rates
2.9                Chart 2.2 shows that market pricing for Bank Rate and gilt yields has increased significantly
                   since our March forecast:1

                   •      Our central economy forecast is conditioned on Bank Rate reaching just over 4¼ per
                          cent by mid-2027 and just over 4½ per cent by the end of the forecast. On average,
                          this is just under ¾ of a percentage point higher than our March forecast. In our fiscal
                          forecast, Bank Rate is on average just over a further ¼ of a percentage point higher.

                   •      Gilt yields have also risen substantially across all maturities since our March forecast.
                          On average, yields are just over ½ a percentage point higher in our economy forecast,
                          and a further ¼ of a percentage point higher in our fiscal forecast.

2.10               Since the start of the conflict in the Middle East, interest rates have fluctuated significantly.
                   Between the onset of the conflict and 20 October, market pricing for Bank Rate in late-2027
                   has ranged from 3½ per cent to 5 per cent. Gilt yields at the 10-year mark have ranged
                   from around 4½ per cent to 5½ per cent over the same period. The assumptions in our
                   fiscal forecast and the latest market pricing at the time we finalised this report sit close to the
                   top of these ranges.

                     on inflation and on monetary policy expectations, and rises in real yields. Some

2.12                 However, interest rates at all maturities in the UK have been consistently among the highest
                     in the G7 since 2022, having been toward the middle of the pack in the decade prior.
                     Drivers of this likely include:

                     •         Recent episodes of higher and more persistent inflation than in other advanced
                               economies. CPI inflation in the UK averaged 9.1 per cent in 2022, the highest in the
                               G7 versus an average of 6.4 per cent across the rest of the group. UK inflation has
                               remained among the highest in the G7 since then.

                     •         The UK's higher level of borrowing resulting in a larger increase in net debt than other
                               advanced economies. Between 2019 and 2025, the UK's net debt-to-GDP ratio on an
                               internationally comparable basis increased by 19 percentage points, significantly
                               above the 5 percentage point average for other G7 economies.

                     •         Structural changes in the composition of demand for gilts, as set out in our 2025 Fiscal
                               risks and sustainability report (FRS). The decline of defined benefit pension schemes
                               and the Bank of England’s continued quantitative tightening have reduced gilt holdings
                               of pension funds and of the central bank. We found that expected shifts in gilt demand
                               toward more price-sensitive foreign investors could increase the overall interest rate on
                               UK government debt by close to 1 percentage point over the next 50 years, though
                               markets may already have priced in some of this impact.

Economic and fiscal outlook                                            32
                                                                                   Economic outlook




2.13   Despite these substantial rises over recent years, interest rates are not high by historical
       standards. The 2010s, in which Bank Rate was close to the zero-lower bound and
       quantitative easing reduced longer term gilt rates to multi-decade lows, was historically
       unprecedented. Current levels are close to historical experience – since 1935, 10-year gilt
       yields have averaged around 6 per cent.

Equity prices
2.14   Equity prices, measured by the FTSE All-Share index, are around 1½ per cent higher on
       average than in our March forecast. We assume that equity prices grow in line with nominal
       GDP over the forecast. Valuations, measured by price-to-earnings (P/E) ratios, remain
       around historical averages in the UK, while US equity valuations remain elevated. However,
       as global equity markets are highly correlated, a potential correction in equity valuations
       elsewhere poses a downside risk to our forecast. We explored the potential impacts of a
       negative shock to equity prices in our Autumn 2025 EFO.

World economy, trade and the exchange rate
2.15   Our central forecast, based on the IMF’s July 2026 World Economic Outlook Update and
       April 2026 World Economic Outlook (WEO), is for global GDP to increase by 3.0 per cent
       this year, down from 3.4 per cent in 2025. This is unchanged from our March forecast. The
       IMF July WEO Update noted that the impact of tariffs has been more muted than expected,
       while the energy shock has been partly offset by momentum in the technology sector due to
       the growth and adoption of AI. Global GDP growth thereafter averages around 3 per cent a
       year. Global trade is expected to increase by 3½ per cent in 2026 and 4¼ per cent in 2027
       before averaging around 3 per cent a year over the rest of the forecast.

2.16   US tariff policy continues to be restrictive compared to recent experience. Changes in US
       tariff policy since July have reduced the relative advantage previously held by UK exporters.
       Chapter 3 discusses the potential impacts of recent changes to UK trade policy.

2.17   Since our March forecast, the pound has appreciated ½ per cent against the dollar (to 1.35
       dollars per pound) and 2 per cent against the euro (to 1.17 euros per pound). We assume
       that exchange rates are unchanged over our forecast. As a result, the trade-weighted
       sterling effective exchange rate remains about 1¼ per cent higher across the forecast period
       compared to our March forecast.

Fiscal policy
2.18   In the central forecast, public sector net borrowing falls from 4.4 per cent of GDP in
       2025-26 to 1.8 per cent of GDP in 2031-32, but is higher than in our March forecast in
       every year. The primary balance (public sector non-interest revenue minus non-interest
       expenditure) moves from a deficit of 1.5 per cent of GDP in 2025-26 to a surplus by
       2028-29, and reaches a surplus of 1.7 per cent of GDP in 2031-32. The drivers of reduced
       borrowing over the forecast are discussed in Chapter 6.



                                                33                      Economic and fiscal outlook
Economic outlook




2.19       Policy measures increase borrowing modestly as a share of GDP from 2026-27 to
           2028-29, and slightly reduce it thereafter. We judge that these changes in borrowing will
           not have a material impact on real GDP growth that would justify an explicit adjustment to
           the forecast. This is due to the relatively moderate size and profile of the changes in
           borrowing, and because some measures were announced prior to the Budget (see
           paragraph 3.9). The wider impacts of policy measures are discussed in more detail in
           Chapter 3.

Inflation
2.20       CPI inflation has fallen by less than we expected in March, reaching 3.1 per cent in the third
           quarter of 2026, 1 percentage point above our March forecast. In the 12 months to
           September 2026, CPI inflation was 3.5 per cent. Higher-than-expected inflation has largely
           been driven by the direct effects of higher utilities and fuel prices.

2.21       As illustrated in Chart 2.4, we expect that the recent rise in energy prices will keep CPI
           inflation above the Bank of England’s 2 per cent target in 2026 and 2027. In our central
           forecast, CPI inflation increases from around 3.1 per cent in 2026 to 3.4 per cent in 2027,
           with a quarterly peak of 3.8 per cent in early 2027.2 Policy measures reduce CPI inflation by
           0.1 percentage points in 2027 and increase it by around the same amount in 2028 – this is
           discussed in more detail in Chapter 3. We expect inflation to fall back to target halfway
           through 2028 in our central forecast, as energy prices fall based on the market pricing that
           our economy forecast is conditioned on.

2.22       The near-term outlook for inflation is significantly higher than in our March forecast,
           primarily driven by:

           •      the direct effect of higher energy prices on fuel prices and utilities – in our forecast the
                  Ofgem energy price cap peaks at around £1,875 in Q1 2027;3

           •      the indirect effects of higher energy prices on the production and transportation costs
                  of other goods and services, compounded by broader commodity price pressures –
                  including from elevated fertiliser prices and increases in cereal and metals prices –
                  which could feed through to the price of food and other tradable goods; and

           •      some second-round effects from higher inflation through earnings, as workers attempt
                  to protect their real wages – but we assume that these will be smaller than for the
                  2022 energy shock because the labour market is significantly looser, the rise in energy
                  prices is smaller and market pricing suggests it will be less persistent.



2
  We recently published more information on our CPI inflation modelling framework, in line with the recommendations from our 2025
external review to share more transparently our analytical infrastructure. See Cavallero, J., The OBR’s CPI inflation modelling, OBR
articles, August 2026.
3
  External forecasts based on more recent readings of energy market pricing suggest that the Ofgem energy price cap could rise to
around £2,150 in Q1 2027. This figure represents the simple average of estimates from the forecasting services of EDF, British Gas, and
E.ON Next available as of 21 October 2026.


Economic and fiscal outlook                                       34
                                                                                                                     Economic outlook




2.23            There is significant uncertainty around our inflation forecast, mainly due to the evolving
                situation in the Middle East. Our central forecast is conditioned on market pricing for oil
                and gas from mid-August. We explore scenarios for different energy price paths in Chapter
                7. If energy prices are significantly higher than the path assumed in our forecast, we would
                expect larger direct and indirect effects on CPI inflation, and potentially larger second-round
                effects. Conversely, an orderly resolution of the conflict could mean that inflation falls more
                quickly than expected. The extent to which higher energy prices generate second-round
                effects through wages is also highly uncertain and particularly important for the fiscal
                forecast, given the high effective tax rate on labour income and that income tax thresholds
                are currently frozen.

2.24            Other risks to the CPI inflation forecast include the degree to which a particularly strong El
                Niño phenomenon disrupts global food production and puts upward pressure on food
                prices. Based purely on past forecast errors, Chart 2.4 suggests there is a one-in-five
                chance that CPI inflation in 2027 will be above 4½ per cent and a similar probability it will
                be below 2½ per cent.

2.25            In our central forecast, RPI inflation increases from around 3½ per cent this year to around
                4¼ per cent in 2027, before falling back to 3 per cent in 2028, broadly in line with the
                shape of the CPI inflation path. We then expect RPI inflation to drop to 2¼ per cent in 2030
                as the ONS converges the RPI measure to CPIH (see Box 2.3 of our October 2024 EFO for
                a more detailed explanation of this change).

2.26            We expect the GDP deflator, which measures the price of all domestically produced goods
                and services, to grow by 3 per cent in 2026. The GDP deflator is affected by energy prices


                                                                     35                               Economic and fiscal outlook
Economic outlook




             directly to the extent that energy is produced in the UK and indirectly to the extent that
             energy prices create second-round effects on the price of other goods and services through
             increased average earnings. We expect the growth in the deflator to fall to around 2¼ per
             cent in 2027 and around 2 per cent on average from 2028 onward, as energy prices –
             and, in particular, the second-round effects of those price rises – fall back. Compared to our
             March forecast, we project that cumulative growth in the GDP deflator between 2025-26
             and 2030-31, an important determinant of our receipts forecast, will be around 1
             percentage point higher.

Earnings and income growth
Average earnings
2.27         Nominal and real average earnings growth in 2025 has been revised down by around a
             percentage point by the ONS since our March forecast. In our central forecast, we anticipate
             that nominal earnings growth will continue to slow in the near term, partly due to looser
             labour market conditions (left panel of Chart 2.5). But we expect a more gradual slowdown
             than in March, reflecting a judgement that there will be some second-round effects of higher
             inflation through wage growth, as workers attempt to protect their real wages. We judge
             that these effects will be modest relative to the previous energy price spike in 2022, as a
             significantly looser labour market restricts workers’ capacity to bargain for higher wages.
             This is reflected in recent survey evidence, with firms increasingly expecting limited upward
             pressure on wages.4 The market pricing underpinning our forecast also suggests that the
             increase in energy prices will be smaller and less persistent than in 2022, which should
             further limit the size of second-round effects.

2.28         Second-round effects on wage growth have particularly important fiscal implications, given
             that income tax thresholds are currently frozen and labour income has the highest effective
             tax rate of any tax base. The size of these effects is uncertain and will depend on a number
             of factors including: the size of the energy price shock relative to the market pricing that our
             forecast is conditioned on; whether workers’ inflation expectations respond more or less
             than we assume to rising inflation; the monetary and fiscal policy response to the shock;
             and the degree to which a relatively loose labour market restricts workers’ capacity to
             bargain for higher wages. We explore the impact of stronger second-round effects in our
             energy price scenarios in Chapter 7.

2.29         Taken together with our forecast for inflation, this means that we expect lower near-term
             growth in real earnings than in March. We expect no growth in real earnings in 2027 and
             2028. Over the medium term, real wage growth recovers to around ½ a per cent a year by
             2030 in our central forecast (right panel of Chart 2.5). This is slightly weaker than growth in
             output per worker as we expect firms to rebuild profit margins, which allows the rate of
             return on capital to recover. This, in part, reflects our assessment in our October 2024
             forecast – we expect firms to pass on an increasing share of the Autumn Budget 2024
             employer NICs increase to workers, rather than absorbing it in lower profits.
