Real household disposable income
2.30                  In our central forecast, real household disposable income (RHDI) per person, a useful
                      though incomplete measure of living standards,5 falls slightly in the near term. The left
                      panel of Chart 2.6 shows the contributions to growth in RHDI per person, with labour
                      income, non-labour income, and social benefits all boosting income. But across last year
                      and this year, these factors are outweighed by higher prices and taxes on income and
                      wealth. In terms of economic drivers, this near-term weakness is driven by the negative
                      terms-of-trade shock, which pushes down on real wage growth, but up on nominal wage
                      growth. The latter then weighs on RHDI because frozen income tax thresholds increase the
                      degree to which effective tax rates rise as nominal earnings increase.

2.31                  In the medium term, the expected fall-back in energy prices means RHDI per person partly
                      recovers. Growth over the last four years of the forecast averages around ¾ of a per cent a
                      year, broadly in line with the average since 2010. But the right panel of Chart 2.6 shows
                      that the level of RHDI per person is forecast to remain more than ¾ of a per cent below the
                      March profile in 2030-31.




5
 RHDI measures the purchasing power of households’ monetary income. It does not account for changes in household wealth due to
asset prices, changes in the quantity and quality of the provision of goods and services by the Government, the state of the environment,
or factors that may change quality of life such as leisure time. For details, see ONS, Understanding real household disposable income per
head, November 2025.

2.32             Our central forecast represents a continuation of the historically weak growth in RHDI per
                 person since the global financial crisis. Chart 2.7 shows the rolling 10-year average annual
                 growth in RHDI per person. This slowed significantly in the aftermath of the financial crisis,
                 due to lower growth in productivity and real wages. Growth reached a historic low over the
                 10 years to 2025-26, averaging just ¼ per cent a year, and we expect it to fall further in the
                 near term. By 2029-30, our central forecast sees growth over the prior 10 years averaging
                 less than 0.1 per cent a year. As a result, 10 years on from the onset of the pandemic, RHDI
                 per person would be just 1 per cent higher than its pre-pandemic level.

Potential output, productivity and labour supply
2.33              In our central forecast, we expect potential output growth to fall from around 1½ per cent
                  this year to about 1 per cent next year, as higher energy prices weigh on productive
                  potential. We also expect lower net migration to slow population and labour supply growth,
                  but to have little impact on GDP or real incomes per person. Chart 2.8 shows that we
                  expect potential output growth to then rise to around 1½ per cent in the medium term as
                  market pricing implies a fall back in energy prices.

2.34              Relative to March, our central forecast is for the level of potential output to be broadly
                  unchanged in the medium term. A downward revision to net migration is offset by a higher
                  starting level of potential output driven by strong recent GDP outturns, which we judge to be
                  structural; and a higher forecast for business investment which slightly raises capital
                  deepening. In per adult terms, potential output is expected to be slightly higher in the
                  medium term than in March as the net migration revision has no impact.

Labour supply
2.35              Labour supply growth is expected to slow from around ¾ of a per cent in 2026 to around
                  1
                    /3 of a per cent a year over the medium term, marginally lower than in March. The
                  slowdown over the forecast reflects net migration falling this year and then remaining more
                  in line with the previous decade’s average over the next few years, alongside the impact of
                  the ageing population on average hours and participation.

                  Adult population
2.36              Our forecast for the adult population is based on historical data from the Labour Force
                  Survey (LFS), births and deaths from the latest ONS National Population Projections (NPPs),


                                                           39                      Economic and fiscal outlook
Economic outlook




                and our own forecast for net migration. 6 Based on these, the size of the adult population is
                forecast to rise by around 320,000 people a year (around 150,000 via net migration), from
                56.8 million in 2026 to 58.4 million in 2031. The adult population in 2030 is now expected
                to be around 50,000 lower than in our March forecast, as shown in the left panel of Chart
                2.9, reflecting the downward revision to our net migration forecast partly offset by higher-
                than-expected outturn population data.

                Net migration
2.37            Data published since we finalised the March forecast suggest that net migration will be
                lower than we previously assumed. The ONS estimates that net migration fell from 331,000
                in 2024 to 171,000 in 2025, while visa data up to June 2026 continue to show declines
                across most visa types. Reflecting these latest data, and informed by our migration model,
                we expect net migration to fall to 120,000 in the year to mid-2026 (right panel of Chart
                2.9).7 The near-term decline reflects visa restrictions introduced since 2023 alongside higher
                emigration following past high levels of immigration, especially among students. Net
                migration is then forecast to rise gradually to 230,000 by the year to mid-2031, in line with
                the ‘principal variant’ of the latest ONS population projections. This rise largely reflects our
                assumption that the net outflow of British nationals and EU nationals who arrived under the
                pre-Brexit immigration regime will gradually lessen. We judge our net migration forecast to
                be a reasonable central assumption based on recent outturn data, our modelling
                infrastructure, and current government policy.

2.38            Our net migration forecast is around 70,000 a year lower than in March. This has a small
                negative impact on the level of GDP and potential output in the medium term – but has no
6
  Current LFS data are based on an older vintage of National Population Projections (NPPs), which assumed a higher level of net
migration. Since March, we have taken on the April 2026 ONS NPPs, which imply higher inflows into the adult population and marginally
fewer deaths than the previous NPPs. As such, LFS outturn for the adult population in the second quarter of 2026 was 145,000 higher
than we expected in March.
7
  We recently published more information on how we forecast net migration, including our migration model, in line with the
recommendations from our 2025 external review to share more transparently our analytical infrastructure. See Robson, E., Forecasting net
migration, OBR articles, September 2026.


Economic and fiscal outlook                                        40
                                                                                                               Economic outlook




           impact on per person incomes. The composition of migration is also important for economic
           and fiscal outcomes, with the following being important factors:

           •      Demographics: on arrival, migrants are generally more likely to be of working age
                  with around 80 per cent of migrants being aged 16-64 – compared to around 60 per
                  cent for the wider UK population – in the principal variant of the ONS 2024-based
                  NPPs.

           •      Participation: on arrival, migrants tend to have a lower rate of participation at a given
                  age than the domestic population. This generally offsets the effect of demographics
                  and means that the average migrant tends to have a similar overall participation rate.
                  See Box 2.3 of the March 2024 EFO and Box 2.3 of the November 2023 EFO for
                  more detail on these assumptions.

           •      Productivity: we assume migrants have broadly the same average rate of productivity
                  as the domestic population. This judgement is highly uncertain as it depends on a
                  range of factors related to the characteristics of migrants which we explored in more
                  detail in Box 2.1 of the March 2026 EFO.

2.39       We consider all of these factors when assessing the impact on the labour market of revisions
           to our overall net migration forecast, as the composition of the migrant population will vary
           significantly across different entry routes.8 The downward revision to net migration in this
           forecast is broad-based across various visa routes so we judge that this revision has no
           impact on the overall participation rate or level of productivity. This means that we assume
           that lower net migration will leave GDP per person, an indicator of average living
           standards, unchanged by the end of the forecast.

2.40       There remains considerable uncertainty around our net migration forecast, including the
           impact of previous policy changes on the outlook for student visas, the share of
           humanitarian arrivals from Ukraine that remain in the UK, and the future level of net
           migration of EU nationals. Potential changes to indefinite leave to remain policy, which are
           not currently reflected in our forecast as the policy details are not sufficiently certain, and
           changes to visa salary requirements, the details of which were confirmed after the deadline
           for inclusion in the economy forecast, may also affect net migration in future. We explored
           the economic impact of two alternative scenarios of higher and lower medium-term net
           migration in Box 2.3 of the March 2024 EFO. In these scenarios, we also demonstrated the
           impacts of using alternative assumptions for migrants’ productivity and participation in the
           labour force, which are significant.

         Participation rate and average hours
2.41       Our forecasts for trend labour force participation and average hours are unchanged from
           March. The trend labour force participation rate is forecast to fall slightly from 63½ per cent
           in 2026 to 63¼ per cent in 2030, while trend average hours fall marginally from 32 hours

8
 For example, migrants arriving on work visas tend to have a higher participation rate than the domestic population, while dependants
and migrants on humanitarian visas tend to have lower participation rates.


                                                                  41                              Economic and fiscal outlook
Economic outlook




           a week in 2026 to 31¾ hours a week in 2030. Both decline because of our assumption that
           the population will continue to age, and that older people, on average, have lower
           participation rates and work fewer hours. The decline in average hours is more gradual
           than had been the case before the financial crisis, as we assume that the weakness in
           productivity growth since then limits workers’ capacity to use part of their increase in real
           wages to increase their amount of leisure time. The recent ONS revisions to historical
           average hours, which were published after we finalised our forecast, suggest that average
           hours growth has been lower, and output per hour higher, than we had previously thought.
           We discuss these revisions in detail in Box 2.2.

Productivity
2.42       We assume that trend productivity growth slows in 2027 to 0.7 per cent, largely because of
           the spike in energy prices.9 Productivity growth is then forecast to rise to 1.1 per cent in
           2031 as energy prices fall back. We assume the slightly higher medium term fossil fuel
           prices in financial market pricing do not reduce medium-term productivity, as firms adjust
           production processes and energy sources (see Box 2.1 for more details).

2.43       The main change to our forecast for trend productivity growth since March is a stronger
           contribution from capital deepening, largely reflecting a higher path for business investment
           following consistently stronger-than-expected outturns. This change, combined with slightly
           higher-than-expected growth in the first half of 2026, leaves the level of trend productivity
           around 0.2 per cent higher than in the March forecast in 2030. We have not changed our
           previous judgement that underlying trend total factor productivity growth reaches 0.8 per
           cent in 2030, which we explained in Briefing paper No.9: Forecasting productivity. The
           recent ONS revisions to productivity were published after we finalised this forecast, but we
           discuss them in Box 2.2. We will incorporate the impact of any changes, alongside other
           developments, in our next forecast.

         Alternative measure of productivity
2.44       Labour market data remain a key source of uncertainty for the productivity outlook. Chart
           2.10 compares an LFS-based measure of labour productivity with an alternative measure
           created by the Resolution Foundation, based on HMRC PAYE real time information on
           employees and administrative data on self-employment. The two measures now suggest
           similarly weak cumulative growth since the pandemic (shown in the right panel), despite
           considerably different paths to get there. This demonstrates the uncertainty around even
           recent trends in productivity growth. We remain cautious about interpreting trends from
           recent data from relatively short periods and we will continue to regularly review our
           productivity assumption to consider any new information, such as the component-based
           data discussed in Box 2.2.

2.45           The development and adoption of AI is widely expected to boost future productivity growth,
               and we previously analysed this in Annex B of Briefing paper No.9: Forecasting productivity.
               Sectoral data can help to shed light on the degree to which AI is already enhancing UK
               productivity growth. Initially, it might be expected that ‘AI-producing’ sectors would
               experience an acceleration in productivity growth, and then over time this would spread to
               ‘AI-adopting’ sectors. According to the latest ONS Business Insights and Conditions Survey,
               only 30 per cent of businesses reported using at least one type of AI technology in
               September 2026, though this is up from 15 per cent two years ago.

2.46           While sectoral data over short periods can be highly volatile, data on output per worker
               based on administrative sources suggests recent growth in productivity has been broad-
               based.10 Table 2.2 shows that growth between the final quarter of 2024 and the second
               quarter of 2026 has accelerated compared to previous periods in some of the sectors most
               associated with the production and use of AI services, such as IT and communications, and
               professional, scientific and technical services. But productivity growth was relatively strong in
               these sectors prior to the pandemic, and growth has also accelerated in sectors likely to be
               currently less affected by AI, such as accommodation and food, and wholesale and retail.
               We will continue to closely monitor the emerging evidence and how to reflect it in our
               forecasts for productivity and employment.




10
  We use sectoral GVA published on 30 September divided by sectoral employment. Sectoral employment is calculated using RTI
employees and WFJ self-employment (both published on 15 September), scaled to align to the total aggregate employment measure
constructed by the Resolution Foundation. Output per hour worked is our preferred measure of labour productivity, but limitations with the
LFS make it challenging to examine trends at a sectoral level. Output per worker is also less likely to be significantly impacted by the
change to the new ‘component’ approach discussed in Box 2.2.

 Box 2.2: Recent ONS revisions to historical economic data
 In the period since we closed our forecast to new data,a the ONS has released revised historical
 National Accounts and hourly productivity data.

 Blue Book-consistent Quarterly National Accounts figures
 The Blue Book is the ONS’s annual update to the sources and methods used in the UK National
 Accounts, and the Quarterly National Accounts published on 30 September were consistent with
 the 2026 Blue Book. Real GDP growth between 1997 and the second quarter of 2026 has been
 revised upward by an annual average of 0.1 percentage points. Growth in 2025 was revised
 down by 0.1 percentage points, and growth in the first half of 2026 revised up by the same
 amount. Blue Book revisions only have a small impact on the starting level of nominal GDP in
 our forecast – which is important as the denominator for fiscal aggregates – raising it by 0.1 per
 cent in the second quarter of 2026. There were also relatively limited changes to the composition
 of GDP. Given these modest revisions to the recent momentum in the economy, and to the
 starting level and composition of nominal GDP, it is unlikely that incorporating the Blue Book
 2026 revisions would have had a material impact on our economy or fiscal forecasts.
 Average hours and productivity

 Productivity growth is one of the most important, but uncertain, judgements for our economy and
 fiscal forecasts. One of the sources of uncertainty relates to challenges in accurately measuring
 both the level of, and growth in, historical productivity.




Economic and fiscal outlook                                       44
                                                                                     Economic outlook




Our preferred measure of productivity is output per hour worked.b To estimate total hours
worked, the ONS previously used a ‘direct approach’ which drew on average hours worked
reported in the Labour Force Survey. The ONS will now be moving towards a ‘component
approach’ to calculate hourly productivity that draws on a wider range of data sources to
construct estimates of labour input, in line with the latest international standards.c Revisions have,
so far, been published on a whole-economy basis up to 2024. Previous OECD research finds
that countries which rely on average hours taken directly from labour force surveys systematically
overestimate labour input and so underestimate hourly productivity.d The ONS published this
new approach after we had finalised the pre-measures forecast, so we did not reflect it in the
forecast, and it will become the official estimate for hourly productivity from November.
Chart D shows that average hours worked are materially lower under this new methodology and
have continued their pre-financial crisis downward trend, falling at an average annual rate of
0.3 per cent between 2010 and 2024. The difference between the direct and component
approaches is largely explained by reducing the use of imputation when measuring the amount
of annual leave taken, leading to higher measured annual leave and lower hours worked.e The
revisions to average hours have not changed output or income, but have significantly changed
the working hours required to produce that output.

Table A shows that the reduction in labour input, combined with the slight upward revision to
real GDP mentioned above, means that output per hour worked is now estimated to have grown
at an average annual rate of 1.0 per cent between 2010 and 2024. This is close to double the
previous estimate of 0.4 per cent a year and is just under the 1.1 per cent we expect productivity
growth to rise to by the end of our forecast. The small change in the contribution to GDP growth
from employment reflects revisions to the number of workers from taking on new data sources,
which results in a small change in output per worker.

 Ultimately, what matters for the outlook for potential output, GDP per person, average earnings
 and, consequently, for the fiscal forecast, is growth in output per worker – which combines
 forecasts for output per hour and average hours worked. And this set of ONS revisions has little
 effect on output per worker. After the data becomes the official measure of productivity in
 November, we will consider whether this, alongside any other developments and analysis,
 should result in any revisions to our next forecast.

                  This is largely driven by an explicit adjustment for annual leave that assumes workers fully utilise their holiday entitlements. While

Output gap and real GDP
Output gap
2.47                                   Our swathe of output gap models, shown in Chart 2.11, points to a widening amount of
                                       spare capacity in the economy. The models are based on a variety of methods including
                                       statistical filters on GDP, extracting common trends from surveys of capacity utilisation, and
                                       structural filters using economic relationships with variables like inflation to make inferences
                                       about the output gap. Based on these and other indicators, our central estimate is that GDP
                                       is just over 1 per cent below its potential level at the start of the forecast. In the second half
                                       of this year, we expect higher energy prices to weigh on aggregate demand to a greater
                                       extent than supply, widening the output gap further. Relative to March, higher interest rate
                                       expectations also push down on our output gap forecast. As energy prices fall back, as
                                       implied by the market pricing that underpins our forecast, we expect a steady cyclical
                                       recovery in our central forecast, with the output gap closing by 2031.

Real GDP
2.48                                   Real GDP growth was robust around the start of the Middle East conflict, with quarterly
                                       growth of 0.6 per cent in the first quarter of 2026, but we expect higher energy prices to
                                       begin to weigh on growth from the second half of 2026. Our central forecast, shown in the

                                                                                  47                               Economic and fiscal outlook
Economic outlook




              left panel of Chart 2.12, is for real GDP growth of around 1¼ per cent in 2026, a slight
              improvement on our March forecast reflecting significantly higher-than-expected first-
              quarter growth.11 In the second half of 2026 and into 2027, we expect higher global energy
              prices to feed through to the prices facing consumers and businesses, and higher interest
              rates to weigh on activity. We have therefore downgraded our central forecast for real GDP
              growth in 2027 to 1 per cent, 0.5 percentage points below our March forecast. As energy
              prices are then expected to fall back, our central forecast sees growth pick up to average
              1¾ per cent a year over the rest of the forecast period. This leaves the level of GDP in 2030
              broadly unchanged from our March forecast.

2.49          We judge that the risks around our central forecast are broadly balanced. The right panel of
              Chart 2.12 shows that, based on our historical forecast errors, there is a one-in-five chance
              that real GDP growth in 2027 could be higher than 2¼ per cent and a similar chance that
              real GDP contracts. In practice, the risks to the forecast will likely be different to those in the
              past. Upside risks to real GDP growth include a faster reduction in energy prices than we
              assume and stronger productivity growth, potentially driven by the wider adoption of AI.
              Downside risks include a larger or more persistent energy shock than implied by market
              pricing, increased geopolitical uncertainty, more disruption to trade due to uncertainty and
              the impact of tariffs, and weaker productivity growth. We explore the implications for growth
              of alternative energy price paths in Chapter 7. There is also a significant risk of wider
              unexpected shocks hitting the UK economy, with recent examples being the Covid pandemic
              and Russian invasion of Ukraine. These shocks tend to be larger on the downside than the
              upside and may result in large differences between forecast and outturn.

           with a small contribution over the forecast from higher growth in capital deepening. The
           lower forecast for net migration has very little impact on GDP per person as we judge that
           those net migrants would have had similar labour market characteristics to those of the
           general population, explained in paragraph 2.38.

Labour market
2.51       Labour market conditions continue to loosen as vacancies and the number of payrolled
           employees fall, while private sector pay growth has eased. Given the acknowledged issues
           with data quality within the LFS, we continue to draw on a wide range of evidence in our
           assessment of labour market conditions, including administrative data and surveys.12 This is
           particularly the case for the participation rate and, consequently, employment growth. The
           left panel of Chart 2.13 illustrates that non-LFS measures of employment point to a subdued
           labour market, in contrast to the continued growth in LFS employment. Survey evidence,
           such as the latest Bank of England Decision Makers Panel, also points to broadly flat
           employment growth over the past year. HMRC administrative data suggest this continues to
           be driven by lower inflows into employment rather than higher outflows from employment.
           We also expect the Employment Rights Act 2025 (ERA) to weigh on the labour market, in
           addition to previously announced policies including the increases in employer NICs and the
           National Living Wage. See Chapter 3 for a detailed discussion of the impact of policy
           measures on the labour market.

2.52       The general trend in the LFS unemployment rate matches this picture of a loosening labour
           market, rising from its post-pandemic trough of 3.6 per cent in 2022 to 5.2 per cent in the
           final quarter of 2025 (right panel of Chart 2.13). While the unemployment rate in the LFS
           fell to 4.9 per cent in the second quarter of 2026, other evidence – such as the number of
           vacancies, the KPMG and REC UK Report on Jobs, and the employment component of the
           UK PMI – signal ongoing weakness in labour demand. Given these wider indicators, we
           expect the unemployment rate to rise marginally from its current level, peaking at just above
           5 per cent in the second quarter of 2027, before falling to its estimated equilibrium rate of
           4.1 per cent. The peak in unemployment is marginally lower than in our March forecast but
           comes a year later. We previously explored scenarios with alternative trajectories for the
           unemployment rate in our March 2026 EFO.

  This is in line with an ONS recommendation to use the LFS as part of a suite of labour market indicators. See ONS, Labour market

Composition of economic activity
Households
                       Household saving rate
2.53                    In our central forecast, the adjusted saving rate, excluding pension valuation effects, falls
                        from around 5½ per cent in 2025 to just over ½ a per cent in 2031 (Chart 2.14). As
                        described in paragraph 2.30, we are forecasting real household disposable incomes to fall
                        slightly in the near term. In the face of this, we expect consumers to gradually reduce saving
                        to smooth their consumption over time. There are significant risks around this forecast. For
                        example, if higher interest rates or general economic uncertainty encourage higher
                        precautionary saving, consumption would be weaker than in our forecast.

                 Household consumption
2.54              Real household consumption has stagnated since the pandemic, with its level in 2025 only
                  1 per cent higher than in 2019. We expect real household consumption growth to average
                  ¾ of a per cent in 2026 and 2027, as weak real income growth weighs on spending
                  growth. It then picks up to grow more in line with overall GDP in the medium term,
                  following the recovery in real income growth. This leaves real consumption as a share of
                  GDP at 61 per cent at the end of the forecast, over 2 percentage points below the average
                  level from 2010-2019, and 0.4 percentage points below the level we forecast in March. The
                  relatively weak growth in consumption reflects higher energy prices (and that these
                  constitute a larger shock to real household disposable incomes than real GDP) and a higher
                  share of consumption occurring via government-provided services since the pandemic.

Business sector
                 Profits
2.55              The rate of return on corporate capital and the profit share of GDP are both currently at low
                  levels relative to recent history (Chart 2.15). After a period when firms have absorbed a
                  range of cost increases into lower profits, including the rises in employer NICs and energy
                  prices, we increasingly expect them to pass these costs on to households and rebuild their
                  profit margins. Increasing real wages by slightly less than productivity growth should push
                  up the rate of return and profit share towards more historically typical levels. The relatively
                  loose labour market is likely to underpin this process by reducing workers’ bargaining
                  power.

                  Business investment
2.56               Real business investment rose by around 2½ per cent over the first half of 2026, leaving its
                   level 2 per cent above our March forecast by the second quarter of 2026. We expect some
                   of this recent strength to unwind as headwinds from higher energy prices weigh on business
                   activity and investment over 2027. We then expect growth in business investment to pick up,
                   averaging 1¼ per cent a year. By 2030, we expect the level to be ½ a per cent above our
                   March forecast, reflecting both its recent strength and a higher profits forecast.

Government
2.57               Real government spending, the sum of government consumption and investment, is
                   expected to grow by just under 4 per cent in 2026, up from 2 per cent in 2025. Growth
                   then slows to average 1¼ per cent over the remainder of the forecast. Weaker real
                   departmental spending growth in the medium term leaves the level of total government
                   spending around ½ a per cent lower in 2030 relative to our March forecast. Within
                   government spending, consumption rises from 21 per cent of GDP in 2025 to 21¼ per cent
                   in 2026. It then settles at around 21 per cent of GDP by the end of the forecast, significantly
                   higher than the 19½ per cent in 2019. Government investment follows a similar profile,
                   increasing from just under 3½ per cent of GDP in 2025 to nearly 4 per cent in 2027, before
                   falling back to around 3½ per cent by 2031. This is around ¾ of a percentage point above
                   the level in 2019.

Current account and sectoral balances
2.58               We forecast the current account deficit of the balance of payments to narrow from just over
                   3 per cent of GDP in 2025 to around 2½ per cent by 2030, as export growth outpaces
                   import growth. Chart 2.16 shows how the matching inflow of foreign saving to fund this

                         fall from a surplus of around 1¾ per cent of GDP in 2026 to a deficit of 1¼ per cent by
                         2031. The corporate sector moves from a small deficit to a surplus by 2030 as firms rebuild
                         the rate of return and investment falls slightly as a share of GDP. The public sector deficit
                         narrows significantly over the forecast, but the profile remains similar to our March forecast.

The housing market
Mortgage rates
2.59                     We expect that the average effective interest rate on the outstanding stock of mortgages will
                         rise from 4 per cent in 2026 to around 5 per cent in 2031. This is due to an upward-sloping
                         yield curve pushing up quoted rates, and people rolling off mortgages that were fixed when
                         rates were lower. As illustrated in the left panel of Chart 2.17, this effective rate is higher
                         than the average since the financial crisis (3 per cent), but significantly lower than over the
                         decades leading up to 2008. The stock of outstanding mortgage debt is around 85 per cent
                         of household disposable income across the forecast period. This is lower than the peak
                         during the financial crisis, but higher than at any time before the early 2000s, when
                         effective mortgage rates were significantly higher. The right panel of Chart 2.17 shows that
                         mortgage payments as a share of household disposable income are expected to rise to
                         reach 4¼ per cent in 2031, the highest since 2009 and more than double the 2021 level of
                         2 per cent.

2.60                  As shown in Chart 2.18, we expect net additions to the housing stock to fall from around

                      260,000 in 2030-31. Compared to the March forecast, we expect 190,000 fewer
                      cumulative net additions over the forecast period. We expect the ’Your First Home‘ equity
                      loan scheme to only have a marginal impact on housing supply given its limited overall size.

2.61                  We have not changed our estimate from the March 2025 forecast that the National
                      Planning Policy Framework (NPPF), on its own, would boost housebuilding significantly. But
                      leading indicators, particularly housing permissions, suggest that housing supply will remain


Economic and fiscal outlook                                                                      54
                                                                                                                    Economic outlook




                  substantially weaker than would be consistent with our previous forecasts in the near term
                  (see Chart 2.19). Over the medium term, we judge that the economic environment,
                  including as a result of higher interest rates, and the wider regulatory picture, mean net
                  housing additions are likely to rise more slowly than we previously expected:

                  •    Higher interest rates since late 2022, alongside other affordability pressures and weak
                       effective demand, have weighed on the commercial viability of residential
                       development. Industry evidence suggests that higher borrowing costs and weaker
                       buyer sentiment have weighed on house price growth, reducing developers’ ability to
                       absorb rising costs.13 This points to a flatter near-term profile for net additions, with
                       some medium-term recovery expected as some of these economic conditions improve.

                  •    The weaker profile for housing supply in the near and medium term also partly reflects
                       the cumulative effect of regulatory and fiscal changes outside the planning system over
                       the last decade, particularly since 2019. These policy developments have increased the
                       cost, complexity, and timescales associated with residential development. These
                       include measures aimed at improving building safety, such as the Building Safety Levy
                       and Building Safety Regulator regime, environmental requirements such as Biodiversity
                       Net Gain and nutrient mitigation schemes, and wider fiscal measures including the
                       residential property developer tax and higher corporation tax rates. Taken together,
                       these measures appear to have weighed on housebuilding activity to a greater extent
                       than we had previously assumed.

Economic outlook




House prices and property transactions
2.62          We estimate that average house prices will grow by around 2 per cent in 2026 and by 2½
              per cent in 2027. This would continue the recent trend of house price growth being below
              CPI inflation, largely driven by higher mortgage rates and stagnating real incomes. We then
              expect house price growth to average 2¾ per cent from 2028 onwards, as real income
              growth recovers. Compared to the March forecast, growth in house prices is therefore lower
              in the near term. The outlook for house prices remains highly sensitive to the path of
              mortgage rates, the evolution in real incomes, and the impact of planning and regulatory
              changes on housing supply. The ’Your First Home‘ scheme could marginally raise house
              prices by stimulating housing demand.

2.63          In our central forecast, property transactions grow from around 1.2 million in 2026 to
              around 1.3 million in 2031, as the housing market turnover rate returns to its assumed
              medium-term equilibrium that every house is transacted on average once every 24 years
              and as the housing stock continues to grow. The number of transactions across the forecast
              period is around 4 per cent lower than in the March forecast, as higher mortgage rates are
              expected to weigh on activity, and as we have revised down our forecast for growth in the
              housing stock.

Residential investment
2.64          Real residential investment (excluding transfer costs) is forecast to rise by 0.5 per cent in
              2026, following weaker-than-expected outturns in the first half of the year, 0.2 percentage
              points lower than in our March forecast.14 Growth then picks up to average around 3 per
              cent a year over the rest of the forecast, around 2 percentage points lower than in our
              March forecast. This leaves real residential investment 6¾ per cent lower in 2030 than in
              our March forecast. This weaker outlook is due to lower planned housing completions on
              new-build investment but is partially offset by diverted investment toward improving the
              existing stock.

Nominal GDP and its composition
2.65          We expect nominal GDP growth to slow from around 4 per cent in 2026-27 to 3¼ per cent
              in 2027-28, as growth in both real GDP and the GDP deflator slows. We then expect
              nominal GDP growth to average around 3¾ per cent a year over the rest of the forecast, as
              real GDP growth rises and inflation sits at around 2 per cent. Compared to the March
              forecast, cumulative growth in nominal GDP from 2025-26 to 2030-31 is 1¼ percentage
              points higher. This difference is driven almost entirely by higher growth in the GDP deflator.
              Whole-economy inflation is higher due to the second-round effects of the imported energy
              shock through wages and profits, and a rise in the price of energy produced in the UK.

2.66          This is reflected in stronger cumulative growth in all major components of nominal GDP,
              both in income and expenditure terms. On the income side, we expect strong growth in

14
     Transfer costs are additional costs associated with buying housing, such as stamp duty land tax and estate agents’ and solicitors’ fees.


Economic and fiscal outlook                                            56
                                                                                                                Economic outlook




                   nominal corporate profits, a determinant of corporation tax receipts, averaging around 4½
                   per cent a year over the forecast period. Growth in nominal labour income, including
                   wages and salaries and mixed incomes, averages just over 3 per cent a year over the
                   forecast period. Labour income growth is a key judgement in the forecast given its
                   comparatively high effective tax rate and that income tax thresholds are currently frozen. On
                   the expenditure side, we expect nominal business investment growth to average around 2¼
                   per cent a year across the forecast, while nominal household consumption growth averages
                   3¾ per cent a year.

Comparison to external forecasters
2.67               Chart 2.20 compares our forecast for real GDP growth to a range of external forecasts. The
                   interpretation of differences with external forecasters is more complex than usual. Ongoing
                   volatility in financial and energy markets may obscure or exaggerate differences in
                   underlying assumptions about the economic outlook, depending on when the forecast was
                   constructed. Our real GDP forecast over the next two years is slightly lower than the latest
                   short-term external forecasts (published in October), as it does not incorporate the stronger-
                   than-expected GDP growth for July and August. From 2028, our forecast is at the higher
                   end of the range of long-term external forecasts (published in August), with cumulative real
                   GDP growth between 2025 and 2030 ¾ of a percentage point higher than the external
                   average. Our forecast for real GDP growth, both cumulative and year-to-year, is similar to
                   that of the Bank of England in its July Monetary Policy Report.

Economic and fiscal outlook                                       58
3          Policy measures


Introduction
3.1        The Charter for Budget Responsibility states that the responsibility for producing the costings
           of policy measures lies with the responsible policy department, which is typically HM
           Revenue and Customs for tax policies and the Department for Work and Pensions for
           welfare policies. These departments therefore hold the models and datasets that are used to
           produce these costings. The OBR’s role, as set out in the Charter, is to provide independent
           scrutiny and certification of these costings.1 Requests for further information relating to the
           methodology and datasets used for costings should therefore initially be directed to the
           relevant department.

Total effect of Government decisions
The impact of fiscal policy decisions on borrowing
3.2        This forecast incorporates the fiscal implications of policy measures announced in this
           Budget and since the March forecast, which are summarised in Table 3.1 and Chart 3.1.
           The direct effect of these measures is to increase borrowing by £7.6 billion in 2026-27,
           £3.5 billion in 2027-28, and £0.4 billion in 2028-29. The direct effect of policy measures
           reduces borrowing thereafter by £4.1 billion in 2029-30, £5 billion in 2030-31 and £12
           billion in 2031-32.

3.3        Policies that affect public spending are estimated to increase borrowing between 2026-27
           and 2030-31 by an average of £3.3 billion, before planned spending reductions are
           estimated to reduce borrowing by £4.2 billion in 2031-32. This reflects:

           •       Funding to support a £100 council tax rebate increases spending this year (2026-27)
                   by £2.6 billion.

           •       Funding for the defence investment plan is estimated to increase spending by an
                   average of £1.8 billion a year. The valuation of unfunded public sector pensions
                   increases spending by an average of £1.1 billion a year across the forecast.

           •       Reductions in planned capital spending of £0.9 billion a year from 2027-28. The civil
                   service transformation programme is estimated to reduce resource spending by £0.5
                   billion a year from 2028-29. In 2031-32, a reduction in the pre-measures spending
                   assumption further reduces resource spending by £5.5 billion.
1
  We have certified the costings for all but one measure in this forecast. Small changes were made to the policy parameters of the fuel
duty policy announced in the Budget following the costings certification deadline. As a result, we could not certify the final version of the
relevant costing but have used the Government’s estimates in this forecast and will finalise the certification for our next forecast. We do
not expect this will have a material impact on the costing we have used.


                                                                      59                               Economic and fiscal outlook
Policy measures




3.4               Changes to tax policy increase borrowing by £1.7 billion in 2026-27, before reducing
                  borrowing by an average of £6 billion over the final four years of the forecast. This reflects:

                  •     The further freeze to fuel duty and the temporary zero-rating of VAT on domestic
                        electricity reduce receipts in 2026-27 by £1.3 billion.

                  •     HMRC tax administration, compliance and debt collection measures raise an
                        additional £3.6 billion by 2031-32. The largest of these measures is the introduction
                        of timely payment for self assessment which is estimated to yield an average of £1.6
                        billion a year from 2029-30.

                  •     Changes to corporation and bank taxes are estimated to raise an average of £2.2
                        billion a year across the forecast. The reduction in writing down allowances is
                        estimated to raise approximately £1 billion a year on average from 2027-28, while
                        the temporary increase in the bank surcharge increases receipts by £1 billion in 2027-
                        28.

                  •     Further increases to tax rates on savings and property income increase receipts by an
                        average of £1 billion a year from 2028-29.

3.5               Table 3.1 also includes the indirect effects of policies on the public finances via any wider

                  and primarily driven by the impact that policy measures have on inflation (see paragraph
                  3.10) and by higher near-term borrowing increasing debt interest spending. These also
                  include a £1 billion reduction in estimated departmental underspends against their day-to-

                  further in Chapter 5.

Economic effects of policy measures
3.8                      Our forecast explicitly accounts for the economic impacts of newly announced government
                         policy where these are material. The impacts of policy on the supply side of the economy
                         are explicitly accounted for in our forecast if credible evidence suggests the measures will
                         have an additional, durable, and significant impact on potential output.2 The Government
                         has announced initial details of a range of policies since the March forecast – including at
                         this Budget – which aim to raise GDP over the longer term. We will review the potential
                         impact of such policies, over the medium- and long-term, when final details are confirmed
                         and where sufficient evidence is available – including as part of our annual summer
                         assessments of supply potential. Therefore, in this forecast, we have not made any explicit
                         adjustments to potential output due to policy changes.3

3.9                      Our assessment of the demand-side effects of fiscal policy is informed by ‘fiscal multipliers’.
                         We define fiscal multipliers as the impact of a policy package on demand which, at least in
                         the short run, can affect the output gap by changing the level of economic activity relative to
                         potential output. Our standard multipliers are drawn from the empirical literature. However,
                         there is no single multiplier that applies to all policies and at all points in the economic
                         cycle. The multiplier may vary due to macroeconomic conditions and policy instruments. We
                         therefore assess at each forecast what multipliers are appropriate.4 We judge that the
                         demand-side impact of this policy package is not sufficiently material to explicitly adjust our

2
   We define ‘significant’ as when a measure, or package of measures, changes the level of potential output by at least 0.1 per cent by the
fifth year of our forecast. See Briefing Paper No. 10: Accounting for the supply-side effects of policy.
3
   We have not included an assessment of changes to visa salary requirements announced at this Budget, as the final policy was confirmed
after the deadline for including it in the economy forecast. We will consider the impacts of these changes in our next forecast.
4
   See Chapter 2 in our June 2026 Forecast evaluation report.

           real GDP forecast. The overall package is relatively small in fiscal terms and much of the
           initial fiscal loosening has been pre-announced. Additionally, forward-looking agents may
           smooth economic activity through the initial fiscal loosening and subsequent fiscal
           tightening arising from policy.

3.10       The changes to fuel duty and the temporary cut to VAT on domestic electricity bills have
           small effects on our inflation forecast. They reduce CPI inflation by 0.08 percentage points
           in 2026-27 and 0.02 percentage points in 2027-28, before raising CPI inflation by 0.04
           percentage points in 2028-29. On a quarterly basis, at its peak, these policies reduce CPI
           inflation by 0.22 percentage points in the second quarter of 2027 and increase CPI inflation
           by 0.16 percentage points in the second quarter of 2028.5,6

Spending measures included in this forecast
Changes to departmental expenditure
3.11       Changes to departmental spending increase borrowing by £5 billion in 2026-27 and £1.2

           changes to departmental spending in greater detail in Chapter 5. The largest changes are:

           •       a £100 council tax rebate for properties in bands A to D in England which increases
                   borrowing by £2.6 billion in 2026-27;

           •       the defence investment plan, which increases departmental resource spending (RDEL)
                   by £1.8 billion a year on average;

           •       a £5.5 billion reduction to the RDEL pre-measures spending assumption in 2031-32;
                   and

           •       changes to departmental capital spending, which reduce borrowing by an average of
                   £0.9 billion a year from 2027-28.

Receipts measures included in this forecast
Tax administration, compliance, and debt collection measures
3.13   The Government has announced further HMRC tax administration, compliance, and debt
       collection measures in this Budget which are estimated to raise an additional £3.6 billion by
       2031-32. These measures are targeted at the current high levels of tax debt and reducing
       the overall tax gap through increased HMRC compliance activity, and include:

       •    The introduction of timely payment for self assessment, and part reversal of the Budget
            2025 measure that mandated coding out via the PAYE system, which brings more
            payments of self-assessment liabilities forward into the tax year in which income is
            earned. This measure is estimated to yield an average of £1.6 billion a year from
            2029-30 which reflects the timely payment system preventing some debt losses and
            changing the timing of recovery of debt. The yield is adjusted to account for self-
            assessment customers reducing their forecast liabilities and additional delayed debt
            created due to payment pressures in the transition year. Further information on the
            non-debt cash timing impacts of the measure is detailed in paragraph 3.25.

       •    Changes to HMRC’s compliance workforce which are estimated to yield an additional
            £0.4 billion in 2031-32. This accelerates the recruitment of the previously announced
            5,500 additional HMRC compliance officers and funds an additional 670 staff by the
            end of 2029-30. The recruitment of these compliance officers will be equivalent to a
            23 per cent increase to HMRC’s compliance workforce compared to March 2024. The
            expected yield has been adjusted to account for diminishing returns of additional
            compliance officers, where marginal yield from additional activity is likely to be below
            the average yield from business-as-usual compliance activity, disruption impacts, and
            optimism bias to reflect uncertainty around recruitment, performance of staff, and
            nature of future non-compliance activity.

       •    Other measures which yield £1.4 billion in 2031-32, and include extending HMRC’s
            powers to directly recover outstanding tax debts; mandating the use of e-invoicing to
            reduce opportunities for error and other forms of non-compliance; investing to place
            part of the established stock and future flows of outstanding tax debt with debt

 Box 3.1: Compliance policy and the tax gap
 In our scrutiny of tax compliance policies, in addition to considering the impact of each measure
 individually, we also consider the overall impact of the full package of measures on the projected
 ‘tax gap’. This is the difference between the amount of tax that should, in theory, be paid and the
 amount received, and so is a measure of overall non-compliance. While most tax liabilities are
 paid voluntarily through the normal operation of the tax system, other liabilities are only
 identified or recovered through HMRC compliance activity. HMRC records the additional revenue
 generated, and losses prevented through this activity as ‘compliance yield’. Other things equal,
 higher yield from compliance and debt collection activities should reduce the amount of tax that
 ultimately remains unpaid and, in turn, the tax gap.
 HMRC’s latest estimate of the tax gap was £59 billion or 6.4 per cent of total theoretical
 liabilities in 2024-25 (left-hand side, Chart A).a These estimates suggest the tax gap has risen
 since the mid-2010s, following a period of decline, although the increase appears to have
 flattened in recent years.b As shown on the right-hand side of Chart A, small businesses have
 accounted for a growing share of the tax gap in recent years, rising from around 40 per cent of
 the total in 2017-18 to more than 60 per cent since 2021-22. Of the compliance measures
 announced in this Budget, a third of the yield is expected to reduce the tax gap, with the residual
 reflecting either debt acceleration or payments. Of the 15 measures expected to reduce the tax
 gap announced this Budget, four target the small business tax gap.

Note: This chart reflects when announced yield is estimated to impact the tax gap. Yield estimates shown here are ‘debt management
adjusted’ and therefore differ from total yield. Debt management yield that changes the timing of payments, rather than the amount
of tax ultimately collected, is excluded, while yield that reduces amounts otherwise lost through write-offs or remissions is included.
HMRC estimates the latter using the age of debt in scope of each measure and the associated estimated impairment rate. The non-
adjusted total compliance yield expected from these policies in 2029-30 is around £12 billion.
Source: HMRC, OBR


Estimates of the yield from compliance measures are highly uncertain. This reflects uncertainty
around how taxpayers will respond to interventions and around the effectiveness of the
operational delivery of such measures. To account for this, we apply a range of adjustments
including attrition, which reflects how behavioural responses can erode yield over time, and
optimism bias, to reflect the risk that evidence or delivery assumptions overstate yield. These
adjustments are intended to produce a central estimate, but the resulting yield will nonetheless
remain uncertain.
Looking top-down, the historical and projected future path of the tax gap indicates both upside
and downside risk to the forecast. The forecast assumes a significant and sustained decline in the
tax gap following a period across which it has been broadly flat. However, there have been
previous periods of steeper declines in the tax gap, most significantly in the early 2010s. Given



                                                                            67                      Economic and fiscal outlook
Policy measures




 these risks, we will continue to monitor and evaluate the tax gap outturn compared to the
 estimated yield from compliance and debt measures, and, as set out in the article we published
 in October 2025, will undertake additional work with HMRC to further improve the approach
 used to produce these costings, including standardising the bottom-up assumptions we use to
 arrive at a central estimate of the yield.d

Changes to corporation and bank taxes
3.16       The Budget includes several changes to corporation and bank taxes, which increase receipts
           by £2.3 billion in 2031-32. These include:

           •      A reduction to the writing down allowance (WDA) main rate from 14 to 12 per cent,
                  alongside an increase in the new first-year allowance introduced in January 2026 from
                  40 to 50 per cent from April 2027.7 This is estimated to raise an average of £1 billion
                  per year from 2027-28.

           •      A temporary 3 percentage point increase to the bank surcharge, from 3 to 6 per cent,
                  effective from 1 January 2027 to 31 March 2028. Over the 15 months this is in effect,
                  it is estimated to yield a total of £1.5 billion. This is net of an estimated £190 million
                  reduction in receipts8 as some banks are assumed to move profits out of the UK in
                  response to the higher tax rate. The size of this effect is uncertain.

           •      Restricting sovereign wealth and overseas public pension funds’ immunity from direct
                  taxes on UK income and gains from January 2029 raises £0.7 billion by 2029-30.
                  This estimated yield includes a behavioural effect which significantly reduces the
                  costing to reflect the affected population restructuring their assets, increasing leverage,
                  and an attrition adjustment. This costing is deemed highly uncertain because of a lack
                  of administrative data on the tax and assets of the affected group, and uncertainty
                  surrounding the degree to which these groups will respond to minimise the impact of
                  these changes.

Increase to tax rates on savings and property income
3.17       This Budget further increases the tax rates on savings and property income. These rates
           were already set to increase by 2 percentage points in April 2027 following measures
           announced at Budget 2025. From April 2027, the basic, higher, and additional rates will
           increase by a further 2 percentage points to 24, 44, and 49 per cent respectively. Together,
           the measures announced at this Budget are estimated to raise £1.0 billion by 2031-32:

           •      The additional 2 percentage point increase in all rates of saving income tax is
                  estimated to yield £0.6 billion a year on average from 2028-29. A behavioural
                  response, primarily reflecting additional usage of other tax advantaged assets, such as
                  Individual Savings Accounts, reduces the yield by around £0.2 billion a year.

           •      The further 2 percentage point increase in all rates of property income tax is estimated
                  to raise £0.4 billion a year on average from 2028-29. The costing of this policy
                  includes small behavioural responses, reflecting additional tax-motivated incorporation
                  and higher non-compliance, that reduce the yield by £0.1 billion a year. The potential

Changes to dividend and capital gains taxation
3.18       The Budget includes the following changes to the dividend and capital gains tax (CGT)
           regimes:

           •      Changes to the taxation framework for distributions from companies to individuals
                  from April 2028 which is estimated to raise £0.8 billion by 2029-30. This introduces a
                  number of changes to the system which aim to prevent company owners paying tax at
                  lower CGT rates rather than at dividend income tax rates. The costing includes a
                  significant reduction in the estimated yield over time to reflect the targeted tax base’s
                  ability to plan to reduce the additional tax liability.

           •      A reduction to the lifetime limit for the business asset disposal relief (BADR) from £1
                  million to £500,000 from April 2027 increases receipts by £0.3 billion in 2029-30.
                  The introduction of a 10 per cent CGT rate for qualifying founders of high-growth or
                  innovative companies from April 2027 reduces receipts in 2029-30 by £0.3 billion.

Other changes to taxes
3.19       This forecast also incorporates other changes to taxes which raise £1.1 billion in 2026-27,
           £1.3 billion in 2027-28, before falling to £0.2 billion by 2031-32. These include:

           •      The Government has announced the abolition of the carbon price support (CPS) from
                  April 2027 and provided funding for the Low Carbon Contracts Company (LCCC)
                  enabling it to reduce contracts for difference (CfD) levies on billpayers. These
                  measures cost an average £0.4 billion a year from 2027-28 onwards.9 These
                  measures are intended to offset the increase in electricity bills for non-energy-intensive-
                  industry consumers due to the British Industrial Competitiveness Scheme (BICS) and an
                  uplift to the Supercharger scheme.

           •      In Budget 2025, fuel duty rates were frozen and the reversal of the 5p fuel duty cut
                  delayed until September 2026. The Government has since announced a further freeze
                  to fuel duty rates and delay of the reversal until January 2027, with this Budget
                  extending these until June 2027. According to the latest policy, on 1 July 2027 the
                  temporary five pence cut – first introduced in 2022 – will be reversed in full alongside
                  an uprating of fuel duty rates. If implemented, it would lead to fuel duty rates rising
                  from 52.95 pence per litre to 60.59 pence per litre. This policy costs £0.6 billion in
                  2026-27 and £0.8 billion in 2027-28. The reversal of the 5p cut has now been
                  delayed five times and fuel duty has not been uprated with RPI since 2011. Paragraph
                  3.43 and Chapter 7 sets out the fiscal impacts if this latest iteration of fuel duty policy
                  were to again not be implemented.
9
 This measure also increases annually managed expenditure by £0.8 billion in 2027-28 as firms eligible for BICS and the Supercharger
uplift receive backdated support relating to 2026-27. This increases the total cost of the scheme to £0.5 billion a year for 2027-28
onwards.

           •      A cut to VAT on domestic electricity from 5 per cent to 0 per cent until April 2027
                  announced in July, with this Budget extending the cut until July 2027. This is expected
                  to cost £0.7 billion in 2026-27 and £0.3 billion in 2027-28.

           •      The Government has announced a package of temporary business rates measures
                  through the remainder of the current valuation period, at a net cost of around £0.5
                  billion in 2028-29. These measures include a real-terms freeze in business rates
                  liabilities for properties in certain sectors, increased reliefs for pubs and live music
                  venues, and higher eligibility thresholds for small business rates relief. The Budget also
                  extends existing schemes that limit increases in business rates bills following
                  revaluation. The cost of these measures is partly offset by a two-year extension of the
                  1p transitional relief supplement.

           •      The Budget also incorporates mayoral strategic authorities into the business rates
                  retention system on a permanent basis. This increases the share of net business rates
                  revenues flowing into local government by approximately £0.5 billion in 2031-32.10
                  The measure means that a larger share of future growth in business rates revenues
                  accrues to mayoral authorities rather than central government. This policy forms part
                  of the Government’s wider devolution roadmap, which we discuss further in
                  paragraph 3.38.

Measures impacting the Government’s balance sheet
3.20       The Government has increased the funding available for the National Wealth Fund and the
           British Business Bank (BBB) to invest in financial assets, such as loans and equity. This raises
           the Government’s financing requirement by £1.9 billion a year on average, and increases
           debt by £3.9 billion in 2031-32. The new investments are estimated to reduce borrowing by
           £0.5 billion in 2031-32, reflecting £0.6 billion of additional income, which is only slightly
           offset by expected losses due to loan write-offs and calls on guarantees. Taken together, the
           net impact of these measures is to increase debt by £3.4 billion in 2031-32.

3.21       The Government has also announced additional capacity for UK Export Finance (UKEF) to
           provide guarantees to back the purchases of British exports by foreign buyers. UKEF expects
           to issue a total of £40 billion of additional guarantees evenly between 2027-28 and 2030-
           31. This reduces borrowing by £0.2 billion a year over the medium-term but materially
           increases the public sector’s exposure to geopolitical risk. The £7 billion expansion of the
           Growth Guarantee Scheme offered by the BBB is expected to increase borrowing by £0.1
           billion in 2026-27 and 2027-28 and £0.2 billion thereafter, reflecting the expected calls on
           the guarantees, which outweigh the fee income earned.

3.22       The ‘Your First Home’ Equity Loan Scheme will provide loans to first-time buyers of newbuild
           homes totalling £0.7 billion in 2027-28 and of £1 billion from 2028-29 onwards. This
           increases debt by £0.7 billion in the first year, thereafter the annual increase in debt from
