3.24      The only highly uncertain measure not discussed above is the measure increasing the
          Company Share Option Plan (CSOP) scheme individual limit and removing the interaction
          with Enterprise Management Incentives so individuals can benefit from both allowances
          fully. There are high levels of uncertainty around the level of additional take-up of these
          schemes and the extent to which additional take-up maximises the increased options
          allowances.

Classification treatment of policies
3.25      On the advice of Treasury classification experts and pending an ONS decision on
          classification this section sets out how we are recording the following new Budget
          transactions:

          •      Fees levied on landlords to register with local authorities are recorded as central
                 government taxes and so increase receipts, while fees on developers to participate in
                 the equity loans scheme are recorded as sales of goods and services and so decrease
                 spending. These decisions do not affect borrowing or debt.

          •      The introduction of timely payment for self assessment means that affected taxpayers
                 make payments towards both their previous and current year liabilities during the
                 transition year of 2029-30. These overlapping payments, together with the part
                 reversal of the Budget 2025 measure that mandated coding out via the PAYE system,
                 increase cash receipts in 2029-30 by £17 billion. As explained further in Chapter 6 the


Economic and fiscal outlook                                      72
                                                                                        Policy measures




            additional cash receipts reduce the CGNCR and public sector net debt in 2029-30, as
            both are measured in cash terms. We assume that the ONS will make an
            extraordinary accruals adjustment in 2029-30 to remove the temporary cash timing
            effect from these overlapping payments but will not do so in subsequent years.

3.26   The Treasury has also decided to switch the British Business Bank and National Housing
       Bank from departmental expenditure limits (DEL) to annually managed expenditure (AME).

Update on previous measures
3.27   We cannot review and re-cost all previous measures at each fiscal event, but we do look at
       those where the original (or revised) costings seem to be under- or over-performing, and
       costings that were identified as particularly uncertain.

3.28   The yield from the Budget 2025 measure to no longer exempt salary-sacrificed pension
       contributions from NICs, above an annual £2,000 threshold, has been revised up by an
       average of £0.4 billion in 2029-30 and 2030-31. This is driven by outturn data suggesting
       higher usage and average contributions than previously assumed and a stronger nominal
       earnings forecast.

3.29   In response to underperformance in the delivery model of their targeted case review (TCR)
       regime, DWP has decided to insource 2,500 FTE staff who were previously working for a
       third-party contractor to boost their productivity, as well as to hire an additional 900 FTE
       staff to undertake more case reviews. We included AME savings from this operational
       expansion in our pre-measures forecast. Despite the Government’s intention for these
       additional 900 staff to be permanent, we were informed after the closure of our pre-
       measures forecast that they would not be funding these staff beyond 2028-29. We will
       review both the resourcing and AME savings of this measure at our next forecast.

Update on previous indirect effects judgements
3.30   Together with the Treasury and departments responsible for implementation of the various
       policy measures, we have established transparent processes for monitoring and evaluation
       of the supply-side impacts of policies on potential output. We use these processes to check
       whether observed outcomes are consistent with the assumptions underpinning our economy
       forecast. We have made no explicit adjustments to the forecast as a result of monitoring and
       evaluation at this event. For larger policies, there is not yet evidence that their effects differ
       materially from those incorporated in our forecast, while policies experiencing delivery
       issues have relatively small effects in the forecast.

3.31   As set out in Chapter 2, in this forecast we have revised down our projection for new
       housing supply over the forecast period by 190,000. We have not, however, at this point
       revised our previous estimates of the positive contribution that the National Planning Policy
       Framework (NPPF) reforms would make to housing supply over this period. Instead, our
       assessment is related to the wider regulatory and fiscal environment and to sharp rises in
       construction costs. This illustrates the challenges associated with assessing the economic

                                                  73                       Economic and fiscal outlook
Policy measures




           effects of regulatory policies, where the full range of relevant regulatory changes affecting
           any specific sector can be difficult to identify and quantify.11 We will keep our NPPF
           assumption under review. A feasibility study for the evaluation of the NPPF reforms is
           expected from MHCLG in early 2027.

3.32       We have previously not included an assessment of the potential economic impacts of the
           Employment Rights Act 2025 (ERA), given it was dependent on ongoing consultations and
           secondary legislation. We have made an assessment at this event, following the conclusion
           of most of the consultations and the implementation of various components of the reforms.
           Our analysis suggests that the ERA will likely have a negative economic impact from
           negative impacts on businesses, particularly through increased uncertainty, lost flexibility
           and higher labour related costs, which will more than offset any potential positive impact on
           worker productivity.

3.33       However, the size of the impact of the ERA is highly uncertain, partly due to the difficulties
           mentioned above in assessing the economic effects of regulatory policy. It is likely that the
           negative impact from the final set of reforms is substantially less than the initial policy
           proposals put forward in 2024 would have been. There is also still some uncertainty about
           the final policy design of the zero-hour contract related measures. However, overall, we
           judge there is currently not clear evidence that this negative impact is above our 0.1 per
           cent potential output threshold, so we have not made an explicit adjustment to our forecast.
           We will keep this judgement under review.

3.34       The latest evidence on the increase in employer NICs announced at Autumn Budget 2024
           does not suggest that its impacts have been materially different from our expectations.
           Sharper falls in employment among lower-paid sectors are consistent with our judgement
           that these firms would be most affected by the reduction of the secondary threshold and its
           interaction with the National Living Wage. That said, it remains too early to draw firm
           conclusions about the scale of the policy’s impacts relative to our original assumptions,
           given the wider effects impacting the labour market (see Chapter 2). A full evaluation is
           expected from HMRC in 2027.

3.35       At previous events we have incorporated estimates of the increase to labour supply from
           government employment support programmes. The extension to Talking Therapies, which
           we estimated would increase employment by 10,000 in our November 2023 forecast, was
           intended to increase both the number of completed treatments and the average number of
           sessions. However, this extension has not been delivered. Connect to Work, which we
           assumed – in our 2023 forecasts – would increase employment by around 25,000, aimed
           to help people with health conditions and disabilities into employment. The number of
           participants supported is now expected to be around a third lower than we assumed by
           2028-29. These examples illustrate the challenges of making explicit adjustments to the
           economy forecast for departmental spending policies, given that the impacts are highly
           sensitive to subsequent changes to policy and operational design, possible reprioritisation of

11
   For example, the full range of relevant regulatory changes affecting any specific sector can be difficult to identify and quantify, making it

              funding, delivery challenges, and wider changes to spending programmes which may have
              offsetting effects.12

3.36          The latest evidence on the expansion of 30 hours free childcare, suggests take-up has been
              stronger than expected. This was announced at Spring Budget 2023 and, as of June 2026,
              the number of active users of the offer was 17 per cent above the DfE target. This has
              increased spending on the policy relative to original expectations. However, higher take-up
              does not necessarily imply a larger labour supply effect – we originally estimated that this
              would increase employment by 60,000. Some parents receiving the additional support may
              have worked similar hours in its absence – particularly those on higher incomes – and it is
              possible that individuals earning above the £100k cut-off threshold have reduced their
              hours worked to receive the childcare benefit. The DfE evaluation for this policy is expected
              in 2027.

Policy risks
3.37          Our legislation requires that our forecasts reflect only current government policy. As such,
              when the Government sets out ‘policy ambitions’ or ‘intentions’, we ask the Treasury to
              confirm whether they represent firm policy. We use that information to determine what
              should be reflected in our forecast. Where they are not yet firm policy, we note them as a
              source of risk to our central forecast. A full database of risks to this forecast and the
              changes from previous updates will be available on our website.

3.38          The Government’s devolution roadmap sets out initial details of the transfer of powers and
              resources in England. This includes policies for local retention of income tax receipts from
              April 2028 and discretionary power for mayoral and foundation strategic authorities to
              introduce an Overnight Visitor Levy. We will incorporate any material economic and fiscal
              impacts of these reforms into our forecast once the detailed policy and implementation
              arrangements have been finalised.

3.39          The Timms Review of Personal Independence Payment and Milburn Report on youth, health,
              and employment have both published interim findings with final recommendations expected
              later this year. We will assess the economic and fiscal impacts of any resulting policy
              changes once the Government has responded and the policy details are sufficiently clear.

3.40          The Government has announced several future spending commitments following the end of
              the current Spending Review period. As discussed in paragraphs 5.16 to 5.19, this includes
              the Government’s commitment to reach 3.5 per cent of GDP spent on defence by 2035 and
              a commitment to significantly increase council house building. We will incorporate the
              economic and fiscal impacts of the final announcements made in these areas after the next
              Spending Review.

3.41          The Government has announced its intention to establish a National Care Service in
              England and has brought forward the Casey Commission’s recommendation on its design

12
     See paragraph 4.12 of our “Briefing paper 10: Accounting for the supply-side effects of policy”.


                                                                      75                                Economic and fiscal outlook
Policy measures




           to 2027. The fiscal cost of these reforms is highly dependent on the final design of the
           reforms and the timing of their introduction. For example, the Health Foundation has
           previously estimated that reforms to social care could increase spending by between £3.5
           billion and £17 billion a year, depending on the generosity and design of the system.13 We
           will incorporate the economic and fiscal impacts once the detailed policy and funding
           arrangements have been confirmed. Based on the current policy regime, we projected in the
           2026 Fiscal risks and sustainability report that adult care spending in the UK would rise as a
           share of GDP from 1.2 per cent to 1.8 per cent over the next 50 years, largely reflecting
           demographic pressures associated with the UK’s projected growing old-age population.
           These spending pressures are expected to be most pronounced in the 2030s as the baby-
           boom cohort reaches their 80s, given the concentration of adult social care costs towards
           the end of life.

Extensions of temporary policies
3.42       The Budget includes several extensions to policies that are described as temporary and
           therefore not included in the forecast beyond the short term. This continues a pattern of
           such extensions at successive fiscal events. This introduces risk into the medium-term
           forecast which is produced on the basis of these temporary policies expiring.

3.43       At this Budget, fuel duty rates have again been frozen for a further 10 months until July
           2027, from when the ‘temporary’ 5p cut to rates were first introduced at Spring Statement
           2022.14 The cumulative cost of freezing fuel duty rates between 2010-11 and 2027-28 and
           the 5p cut, relative to the stated policy that they will increase in line with RPI inflation, has
           risen to £139 billion. As shown in Table 7.1, if fuel duty were to remain frozen until 2029-
           30, public sector net borrowing would be £4.3 billion higher.

3.44       Other smaller measures which have been extended through repeated short-term policy
           decisions include various reliefs for business rates, the universal credit surplus earnings limit
           – which has been extended for the tenth consecutive year – and tariff suspension extensions.

The long-run impact of government policy
3.45       The Charter for Budget Responsibility requires us to assess the long-term impacts of new
           government policies, where these are material. We therefore report on new policies with
           long-run economic and fiscal impacts, especially where these differ from the effects that are
           expected to have occurred by the five-year timeframe at which we are required to produce
           our medium-term forecasts.

living at home. The higher figures correspond to wider schemes e.g. including the costs of residential care.

Economic and fiscal outlook                                       76
                                                                                                                   Policy measures




The long-run economic impact of measures
3.46       The 2 percentage point increase in property income tax rates in this Budget will be
           implemented on top of the 2 percentage point increase announced at Budget 2025. Both of
           these measures will come into force from April 2027 and represent a material reduction in
           returns to private landlords. This follows various measures over the past 10 years that have
           also reduced private landlord returns.15 These measures collectively risk leading to a steady
           rise in rents through two effects. First, potential landlords are less likely to enter the market,
           reducing long-run rental supply, which risks a steady long-term rise in rents if demand
           outstrips supply – which we think is more likely than not. Second, landlords are likely to pass
           on more of these costs to renters in the long-run through a combination of some current
           landlords leaving, thereby giving more price setting power to remaining landlords, and
           greater long-run flexibility to raise rents. Such rises in rents would offset at least some of the
           reduction in the commercial viability to landlords from the measures introduced in recent
           years.

3.47       The India-UK Free Trade Agreement could increase real GDP by amounts rising to 0.13 per
           cent by 2040, according to the Government’s impact assessment.16 As these economic
           benefits are expected to build up gradually over the long-term, we do not make an explicit
           adjustment in the medium-term forecast. The UK is also currently negotiating with the EU on
           various policies, including sanitary and phytosanitary measures and alignment between the
           UK Emissions Trading Scheme and EU Emissions Trading System. The economic impact will
           depend on the outcome of these negotiations.

The long-run fiscal impact of measures
3.48       In our Fiscal risks and sustainability reports (FRS), we have highlighted the long-term costs of
           the triple lock policy which, under its current design, uprates the state pension each year by
           the greater of average earnings growth, CPI inflation, or 2.5 per cent. In the 2026 FRS
           long-term scenarios, an ageing population – combined with the triple lock – drove a
           significant projected increase in state pension spending from 5 per cent of GDP in 2025-26
           to 9 per cent of GDP in 2075-76. We estimated that the triple lock accounted for around
           one third of this increase. In the FRS baseline long-term scenario, which assumes pensions
           are uprated by the triple lock, public sector debt is projected to reach 300 per cent of GDP
           by 2075. In a scenario where the state pension is instead assumed to be uprated by
           earnings each year, debt was projected to reach 260 per cent of GDP by 2075.

3.49       Under the adjusted triple lock system, which is expected to take effect from April 2030, the
           state pension will be uprated by the highest of CPI inflation, 2.5 per cent, or the increase
           required to maintain its value relative to earnings at 2029-30 levels. As a result, if the value
           of the state pension moves above its value relative to earnings in 2029-30 (due to periods
           in which inflation or 2.5 per cent is higher than earnings growth) then in subsequent periods

15
   For example, changes to mortgage interest relief, the stamp duty land tax surcharge and capital gains tax allowances, and the
introduction of the Renters’ Rights Act.
16
   See Department for Business and Trade, Impact assessment of the Free Trade Agreement between the UK and India, 2025. The
agreement came into effect in 15 July 2026.


                                                                  77                              Economic and fiscal outlook
Policy measures




           (where earnings growth is higher than inflation or 2.5 per cent) it would converge back
           toward this relative value. This removes the so-called spending ratchet effect under the
           current triple lock whereby increases in pension values relative to earnings are permanently
           retained and compounded over time.

3.50       We have incorporated the adjusted triple lock into our central medium-term forecast from
           2030-31 but it does not currently affect state pension costs over the forecast period.17
           However, the adjusted triple lock is likely to materially reduce the growth of state pension
           spending over the long term relative to the current triple lock. The exact long-term impact of
           this change will be highly dependent on the future relationship between earnings growth,
           inflation, and the 2.5 per cent floor. To illustrate this, Chart 3.4 applies earnings uprating,
           the current triple lock and the adjusted triple lock to historical outturn data for earnings and
           inflation since 1990. This shows that:

           •      1990-2007: Average earnings growth was typically above both inflation and 2.5 per
                  cent.18 As a result, the illustrative pension values under both the current triple lock and
                  adjusted triple lock would have remained very close to those produced by earnings
                  uprating alone.

           •      2008-2014: Inflation or 2.5 per cent consistently exceeded earnings growth. As such,
                  pensions under both the current and adjusted triple lock would have been uprated by
                  the same amount each year and seen their value relative to earnings rise.

           •      2015-2025: Earnings growth exceeded inflation or 2.5 per cent over seven of the 10
                  years. These are the years in which the current and adjusted triple lock mechanisms
                  would have produced different pension value outcomes:

                  •     under the current triple lock, pensions would be uprated by earnings growth in
                        these years, maintaining the gaps with earnings indexation that develop when
                        inflation or the 2.5 per cent targets bind. This illustrates the ratchet effect of the
                        current triple lock, under which this gap can only widen over time.

                  •     under the adjusted triple lock, pensions would instead have been uprated by
                        inflation or 2.5 per cent in these years, narrowing the gap with earnings
                        indexation. In a scenario where earnings continued to exceed inflation and 2.5 per
                        cent in subsequent years, this gap would eventually close under the adjusted triple
                        lock.

3.51       Over the period from 1990 to 2025, the adjusted triple lock would have produced annual
           outcomes relative to earnings growth and the current triple lock that varied significantly
           across the different economic environments described. Averaging over the period as a
           whole, state pension payments would have been on average 1.4 per cent lower each year
           than under the current triple lock and 4.4 per cent higher than under earnings uprating.

version of the triple lock in both 2030-31 and 2031-32.
18
   Average earnings growth exceeded CPI or 2.5 per cent in all years between 1990 and 2007 apart from 1995 when CPI was 0.1
percentage point above average earnings growth.

                                  relative to the current triple lock. Provisional analysis based on the historical experience
                                  described above suggests that if the next 50 years looked similar to the last 35 years on

                                  around 25 per cent of GDP in 2075-76 relative to retaining the current triple lock.19 Debt
                                  would be around 10 per cent of GDP higher in the long-term than under an earnings
                                  uprating scenario, reflecting the likelihood that there would continue to be periods where
                                  the inflation or 2.5 per cent uprating mechanisms would bind.

4          Public sector receipts


Summary of the receipts forecast
4.1        In the central forecast, total public sector receipts are projected to rise as a share of GDP
           from 40.1 per cent of GDP (£1.2 trillion) in 2025-26 to 42.9 per cent of GDP (£1.6 trillion)
           in 2031-32. Within this, as Chart 4.1 shows, National Accounts taxes as a share of GDP
           (the ‘tax take’) are forecast to increase sharply from 36.0 per cent of GDP in 2025-26 to
           37.9 per cent of GDP in 2027-28, surpassing the previous historical high, before then rising
           more gradually to reach a peak of 38.6 per cent of GDP at the end of the forecast period.1
           This would be a 5.7 per cent of GDP increase on the pre-pandemic level of 32.9 per cent of
           GDP in 2019-20.

4.2        Table 4.1 details the main drivers of the expected 2.6 per cent of GDP increase in the tax
           take from 2025-26 to 2031-32. Increases in personal taxes (income tax and National
           Insurance contributions) contribute 1.6 per cent of GDP to this increase, reflecting forecast
           earnings growth, frozen personal tax thresholds until April 2031, and policy measures that
           raise self-assessment receipts. Capital taxes contribute 0.5 per cent of GDP to the increase
           due to projected rises in equity prices and the expected impact of inheritance tax and capital
           gains tax (CGT) policy changes largely announced in Autumn Budget 2024. Onshore
           corporation tax contributes a further 0.4 per cent of GDP to the increase mainly due to
           strong expected near-term profits growth.

4.3        Relative to our March 2026 forecast, National Accounts taxes as a share of GDP are
           forecast to be 0.2 per cent of GDP higher this year and just 0.1 per cent of GDP higher in
           2030-31, as higher forecast receipts in nominal terms are almost offset by higher forecast
           nominal GDP. Of the rise in nominal receipts, around four-fifths of the increase in 2030-31
           compared to March is due to changes in the underlying forecast, primarily due to a stronger
           nominal earnings forecast and higher forecast asset prices and profits. Around one-fifth is
           accounted for by the tax policy changes announced in this Budget.

4.4                      Chart 4.2 shows the contributions to the change in the National Accounts tax-to-GDP ratio,
                         which is forecast to be 5.7 percentage points higher in 2031-32 than it was in 2019-20, just
                         before the Covid pandemic. They are:

                         •       2019-20 to 2025-26: Nearly all major taxes grew as a share of GDP during this
                                 period, adding 3.1 percentage points to the tax-to-GDP ratio by 2025-26. The
                                 contribution of income tax rose sharply after 2022-23 due to the freezing of personal
                                 tax thresholds combined with the subsequent period of high inflation. NICs receipts fell
                                 in 2024-25 due to the reductions to employee and self-employed NICs announced in
                                 Autumn Statement 2023 and Spring Budget 2024, but then rose again in 2025-26
                                 due to the employer NICs rises announced in Autumn Budget 2024. The rise in
                                 onshore corporation tax was driven by the increase in the main rate from 19 per cent
                                 to 25 per cent in April 2023 and resilient profits in higher tax-paying sectors. Oil and
                                 gas receipts rose sharply in 2022-23 due to high energy prices and the introduction of
                                 the energy profits levy, then fell back as prices declined. Fuel duty was the only major
                                 tax to see a persistent fall as a share of GDP after the pandemic because of successive
                                 duty rate freezes, the 5p rate cut announced in Spring Statement 2022, and the
                                 increasing use of electric vehicles.

                         •       2025-26 to 2031-32: The further forecast increase in the tax take of 2.6 per cent of
                                 GDP over this period is mainly driven by further expected growth in personal taxes,
                                 capital taxes and onshore corporation tax, as described in paragraph 4.2. This is
                                 expected to be slightly offset by further declines in fuel duty receipts, alongside
                                 reductions in receipts from alcohol and tobacco duties. Much of the increase is
                                 expected to take place by 2027-28, which is also temporarily boosted compared to
                                 2028-29 by expected one-off receipts from the reforms to the previous non-domiciled
                                 tax regime, with more gradual increases then expected from 2028-29 onwards.

4.5                   Chart 4.3 shows the same projected 5.7 percentage point rise in the tax-to-GDP ratio from
                      2019-20 to 2031-32 decomposed into the impact of forecast and policy changes:

                      •       The inherited tax system adds 0.8 percentage points to the tax-to-GDP ratio over this
                              period. This is the increase in tax revenues that is driven by the structure of the tax
                              system in place at the time of the pre-pandemic Budget in March 2020, combined with
                              the expected evolution of the economy over the period to 2031-32.

                      •       Underlying forecast changes, including outturn and economy forecast changes, add
                              an expected additional 0.7 percentage points, which is mainly due to higher forecast
                              profits and earnings.

                      •       The direct impacts of government policy decisions from Spring Budget 2020 to Spring
                              Budget 2024 increase the tax-to-GDP ratio by a further 1.7 percentage points between
                              2019-20 and 2031-32, primarily through freezes to personal tax thresholds and the
                              increased headline rate of corporation tax.

                      •       The direct impacts of Government policy decisions from Autumn Budget 2024
                              onwards add a further 2.5 percentage points to the tax-to-GDP ratio between 2024-
                              25 and 2031-32, including through the measures announced at this Budget that
                              increase it (by 0.2 percentage points).

4.6                   Chart 4.4 illustrates the sensitivity of the receipts forecast to some of the key drivers of
                      expected receipts growth using ready reckoners. The chart shows the impact of assuming
                      that the level of average earnings, nominal consumption and equity prices are higher or


Economic and fiscal outlook                                             84
                                                                                                                      Public sector receipts




                   lower than our central forecast by their median absolute forecast difference in each year.2
                   By 2031-32, this would mean the levels of nominal earnings and consumption, two of the
                   largest tax bases, are around 6 per cent higher or lower than in the central forecast, and
                   equity prices around 10 per cent higher or lower. Additionally, we assume in the downside
                   sensitivity that the tax gap remains flat as a share of GDP from 2024-25,3 and in the upside
                   sensitivity that it falls as a share of GDP by double the rate in our central forecast.

4.7                The left panel shows the overall impact on public sector receipts of these upside and
                   downside sensitivities relative to the central forecast, and the right panel decomposes this
                   impact by economic and fiscal variables:

                   •       In the upside sensitivity, receipts are higher by £82 billion (2.2 per cent of GDP) in
                           2031-32.4 The largest driver is the impact of higher nominal earnings on income tax
                           and NICs, boosting receipts by £50 billion in 2031-32, while higher equity prices
                           increase capital taxes, onshore corporation tax, self-assessed income tax and interest
                           and dividend receipts by £12 billion. Higher nominal consumption increases VAT
                           receipts by around £10 billion in 2031-32. The narrowing of the tax gap raises
                           receipts by around £10 billion.

                   •       In the downside analysis, receipts are lower by a symmetric amount, reducing receipts
                           by £82 billion (2.2 per cent of GDP) in the final year.

2
  We take the median of the absolute level differences at each forecast horizon between outturns and the central forecasts. We use data
from all spring forecasts since 2011 and all autumn forecasts since 2013. The median value is used as it is less skewed by shocks such as
the Covid pandemic. These differences broadly increase each year over the forecast period. The median absolute level difference in the
final year is 6.4 per cent for nominal earnings, 5.9 per cent for nominal consumption, and 10.3 per cent for equity prices.

  The tax gap is the difference between the amount of tax that should, in theory, be paid to HMRC ('theoretical tax liability'), and the

Changes in receipts since the March 2026 forecast
4.8     Table 4.2 shows that in the central forecast, receipts in nominal terms are expected to be
        £12 billion higher in 2026-27 than in the March 2026 forecast, with the difference rising to
        £20 billion in 2030-31. This is primarily driven by:

        •     Underlying forecast differences, which increase receipts by £14 billion by 2030-31,
              due to stronger-than-expected in-year receipts outturn and stronger forecast growth in
              profits, nominal earnings and equity prices (discussed further in paragraph 4.9 below);

        •     PSNB-neutral forecast differences, which are offset in spending, including changes to
              depreciation, VAT refunds and environmental levies, which increase receipts by £1.4
              billion in 2030-31;

        •     The direct effect of Government decisions announced since the March 2026 forecast
              including in this Budget, which reduces receipts by £1.7 billion in 2026-27 but
              increases receipts by £6.6 billion in 2030-31. This is mostly due to near-term tax
              reductions on VAT on domestic electricity and business rates, and further delays to the
              rise in the fuel duty rate, that are later offset by measures boosting self-assessed
              income tax, including the modernisation of the distributions framework, and the
              increase to savings and property income tax rates;

        •     The indirect effects of Government decisions announced in this Budget, which lower
              receipts by £2.1 billion in 2030-31, partly due to a small negative effect on nominal
              GDP including from some measures reducing the GDP deflator.

4.9         Chart 4.5 shows changes in the nominal receipts forecast compared to March 2026, split
            into the contributions due to changes to the pre-measures economy forecast, receipts
            outturn data, and policy changes. Higher forecasts for nominal profits and nominal
            earnings are the largest drivers – increasing pre-measures receipts by £10 billion and £9
            billion, respectively, in 2030-31 compared to the March forecast. Other economic drivers
            include stronger-than-expected equity price growth which raises the forecast by £3 billion in
            2030-31, and slightly weaker expected employee numbers which reduce it by an almost
            equivalent amount. Stronger-than-expected PAYE income tax and NICs outturn receipts so
            far this year increase the forecast by £4.2 billion in 2031-32, while weaker outturn for
            stamp duty land tax reduces it by £3.3 billion.

4.10              HMRC cash outturn receipts for 2026-27, received up to August 2026 which is the final
                  outturn data included in our pre-measures forecast, were £3 billion above the profile
                  consistent with our March forecast for 2026-27. The latest data up to September 2026 –
                  received after closing the pre-measures forecast – show this has increased to £7 billion
                  against the March profile. This is likely to primarily reflect the stronger-than-expected growth
                  in nominal earnings and profits so far this year.5

4.11              In this forecast we expect public sector current receipts for the full year of 2026-27 to be
                  £12 billion higher than forecast in March, which remains broadly consistent with the latest
                  September cash receipts. Income tax (excluding self-assessed), NICs and onshore
                  corporation tax receipts are expected to remain stronger than in our March forecast through
                  the rest of the year as is the case in the September data. As outlined in Box 4.1, oil and gas
                  revenues, which have been lower than expected up to August, are expected to recover in the
                  remainder of the year due to the higher oil and gas prices. CGT receipts, mainly received in
                  January 2027, are expected to be £5 billion higher than expected in March, reflecting an
                  updated assessment of the strength in receipts seen in 2025-26 (paragraph 4.33).
                  However, CGT receipts are typically volatile from year to year, so this is a particularly
                  uncertain part of the in-year forecast.

4.12              Overall receipts in 2026-27 are expected to be relatively backloaded, with relatively more
                  receipts expected in the final three months of 2026-27 than in a usual year. This is partly
                  due to expected receipts from the Temporary Repatriation Facility introduced alongside

           reforms to the previous non-domiciled tax regime. As we set out when these reforms were

Tax-by-tax analysis
Income tax (excluding self-assessed) and National Insurance contributions
4.13       Income tax (excluding self-assessed) and National Insurance contributions (NICs) are
           forecast to raise £520 billion (16.2 per cent of GDP) in 2026-27, a 7.6 per cent increase
           from 2025-26. This rise is driven by strong nominal earnings growth combining with frozen
           tax thresholds. Receipts are then forecast to rise to £610 billion (16.5 per cent of GDP) in
           2030-31, before remaining broadly flat as a share of GDP in 2031-32 as thresholds return
           to being uprated by CPI from April 2031. Almost all of this growth comes from higher PAYE
           income tax because it is a more progressive tax than NICs, and is therefore boosted by the
           freezes to tax thresholds. A further temporary increase is expected to income tax receipts
           between 2026-27 and 2028-29 due to the Temporary Repatriation Facility announced as
           part of previous reforms to the non-domiciled regime.

4.14       Budget 2025 extended the allowances and threshold freezes in income tax and NICs to
           April 2031, meaning the majority will be frozen for most of the 2020s. This increases the
           sensitivity of the receipts forecast to nominal earnings changes through ‘fiscal drag’. Using
           our standard ready reckoners, a 1 percentage point increase in nominal earnings and CPI
           would raise income tax and NICs receipts by an average of £7 billion a year under the
           current policy of frozen thresholds to April 2031. This is around £2.5 billion a year higher
           than if thresholds were increasing with CPI, reflecting the additional fiscal drag generated.6
           This is also likely to have an adverse effect on financial work incentives by increasing the
           marginal tax rate on each additional hour of work. Following our standard approach, we
           have estimated that, taken together, the threshold freezes in income tax and NICs from
           2022-23 to 2030-31 could decrease labour supply by 215,000 in average-hours
           equivalent (AHE) terms by 2030-31.7

4.15       Table 4.3 shows that relative to the March forecast, non-SA income tax and NICs receipts
           are expected to be £9 billion higher in 2026-27. This reflects the higher-than-expected
           PAYE receipts received so far this year, which has likely been driven by nominal earnings
           slowing by less than expected compared with the March forecast. Nominal earnings growth
           has been particularly strong in the public sector, in part due to the earlier timing of pay
           awards compared with previous years that may have boosted receipts against our previously
           assumed profile for receipts in 2026-27. Over the rest of the forecast, receipts are expected
           to be £8 billion higher, on average, than expected in March. This is driven by the higher
           outturn this year and a higher forecast for nominal earnings growth next year, which are

exclude any wider effects on other receipts, spending, or the broader economy.

2026 Fiscal risks and sustainability report.

        only partly offset by a weaker forecast for the number of employees and the effects of
        Government decisions in 2029-30 and 2030-31.

4.16    The main effect of Budget policy decisions is through the measure to introduce timely
        payment for self-assessment and part reversal of the Budget 2025 measure that mandated
        coding out via the PAYE system, explained in more detail in paragraph 3.13. This transfers
        receipts from PAYE to SA IT that were previously associated with the coding out measure.
        The indirect effects of policy decisions have a very small downward impact on receipts in
        most years.

Self-assessed income tax
4.17    Self-assessed income tax (SA IT) is forecast to raise £63 billion in 2026-27 (2.0 per cent of
        GDP), a 17.2 per cent increase on 2025-26. This sharp increase compared to 2025-26 is
        driven by the previously announced reforms to the non-domiciled tax regime, which are
        expected to generate additional receipts from this year onwards, the personal tax threshold
        freezes, and forecast growth in self-employment and dividend income. Receipts are then
        forecast to rise to £94 billion (2.5 per cent of GDP) in 2031-32, driven by further expected
        receipts from the non-domiciled tax regime reforms, the reclassification of carried interest
        into SA IT from CGT, and policy measures announced at this Budget.

4.18    Table 4.4 shows that compared to March, SA IT receipts are £1.0 billion lower in 2026-27
        but higher thereafter by increasing amounts, peaking at £11 billion in 2030-31. The pre-
        measures forecast changes reflect a stronger profits forecast boosting dividend income, and
        the impact of higher equity prices on the carried interest forecast. The main effect of Budget
        policy is through the measure to introduce timely payment for self-assessment. This is
        estimated to increase accrued SA IT receipts by £3.5 billion in 2029-30 and £6.5 billion in
        2030-31. This is partly offset by reductions in PAYE IT receipts, outlined above, which lower
        the total net yield of the measure by around two-thirds in each year. This measure is

Economic and fiscal outlook                             90
                                                                                           Public sector receipts




        expected to increase cash SA IT receipts by around £21 billion in 2029-30, only partly offset
        by a reduction of around £5 billion in PAYE IT cash receipts. Paragraph 3.25 provides
        further detail on the accounting treatment of this measure. The modernisation of the
        distributions framework is estimated to increase SA IT receipts by around £2 billion in 2029-
        30 and 2030-31, partially offset by the expected reduction in CGT receipts from this
        measure. The increase to savings and property tax rates is estimated to increase SA IT
        receipts by an average of £0.8 billion a year from 2028-29.

VAT
4.19    VAT receipts are forecast to raise £186 billion in 2026-27 (5.9 per cent of GDP), a 3.3 per
        cent increase from 2025-26. Receipts are then expected to rise to £230 billion by 2031-32
        (6.0 per cent of GDP). The modest rise in VAT as a share of GDP over the forecast reflects
        the assumption that non-compliance will reduce over the forecast period, and that
        temporary VAT policies such as the zero-rating of VAT on domestic electricity will end.

4.20    As shown in Table 4.5, in the pre-measures forecast, receipts have been revised down by
        £1.2 billion in 2026-27 compared to March. Around a third of this is from an adjustment to
        reflect the September 2025 Tribunal verdict that locum doctors are VAT exempt and can
        reclaim overpayments backdated up to four years. Another third reflects reduced receipts
        due to some private hire vehicle operators (PHVOs) changing their business models outside
        London since the Budget 2025 decision to charge VAT on PHVOs. Over the rest of the
        forecast, pre-measures VAT receipts are lower in each year compared to March and are
        forecast to be £2.9 billion lower by 2030-31, largely due to a reduction in the proportion of
        goods and services assumed to be paying the standard rate of VAT.

4.21    The direct effect of policy measures announced at this Budget initially reduce VAT receipts by
        £1 billion over 2026-27, largely from temporary zero-rating of VAT on domestic electricity.
        From 2028-29 onwards, Budget measures increase VAT receipts by £1.1 billion each year.


                                                       91                       Economic and fiscal outlook
Public sector receipts




          Of this increase, £0.8 billion is from new measures to increase VAT paid by PHVOs and
          locum doctors, and the extension of VAT online marketplace liability rules, while the rest is
          largely from measures aimed at reducing non-compliance and collecting tax debt. Further
          details are provided in Chapter 3. The indirect effects of Budget policies lower VAT receipts
          in each year.

Onshore corporation tax
4.22      Onshore corporation tax is expected to raise £107 billion (3.4 per cent of GDP) in 2026-27,
          an increase of 11 per cent from 2025-26. This increase is driven by higher profits in both
          the non-financial and financial sectors. Receipts are then forecast to rise to £136 billion (3.6
          per cent of GDP) by 2031-32. This is mainly driven by the forecast for profits as a share of
          the economy increasing slightly over the forecast period.

4.23      Table 4.6 shows that relative to March, onshore corporation tax is expected to be £5.8
          billion higher in 2026-27 with higher receipts from the financial sector explaining around
          three-quarters of the upward revision. This reflects higher-than-anticipated profits from both
          retail and investment banks so far this year and a delay in the effect on receipts from the
          Financial Conduct Authority’s motor finance compensation scheme, which raises expected
          receipts by £1.5 billion in 2026-27 and reduces receipts by £1.2 billion in 2027-28.

4.24      Receipts are expected to be £8 billion higher in 2030-31 than forecast in March. The pre-
          measures forecast is £5.7 billion higher in 2030-31 due to higher receipts this year and a
          higher forecast recovery in the profit share relative to March (discussed further in paragraph
          2.55). This is only partially offset by decreases to the forecast due to the net effect from
          company tax data updates and modelling changes to estimates of group relief and capital
          allowances. Budget policies overall increase receipts by £2.3 billion in 2030-31. This
          includes £1.1 billion from a reduction in the main rate of writing down allowances, £0.7


Economic and fiscal outlook                                       92
                                                                                                     Public sector receipts




          billion from restrictions to sovereign wealth funds' and overseas public pension funds'
          immunity from direct tax, and £0.5 billion from tighter rules around companies’ ability to
          use foreign branch losses against UK profits. Further information on these measures is
          provided in Chapter 3. The indirect effects of Budget policies lower receipts in each year.

4.25      Receipts from the bank surcharge, not included in Table 4.6, are expected to raise £2.4
          billion in 2027-28. This is an increase of £1.2 billion from 2025-26 and is £1.1 billion
          higher than expected in the March forecast. This reflects the announcement at this Budget
          that the rate of the surcharge will increase from 3 per cent to 6 per cent from January 2027
          until March 2028. Receipts are then forecast to fall back to £1.6 billion by 2031-32.

Oil and gas receipts
4.26      Offshore corporation tax, petroleum revenue tax (PRT), and the energy profits levy (EPL) are
          expected to raise £5.1 billion (0.2 per cent of GDP) in 2026-27, a 25 per cent increase on
          2025-26 due to higher oil and gas prices. Oil and gas receipts are then expected to fall
          over the remainder of the forecast, as the recent rises in energy prices are expected to
          unwind, down to £1.9 billion by 2028-29. Receipts are then forecast to decline further to
          £0.3 billion in 2030-31 and close to zero in 2031-32, primarily due to the expiry of the EPL
          in September 2029 and the expected continued fall in oil and gas production.

4.27      These forecasts are based on the North Sea Transition Authority’s (NSTA) projection for total
          oil and gross gas production, shown in Chart 4.6. This shows that production has been
          declining since the start of the 2000s and that this decline is expected to continue at around
          10 per cent a year over 2027 to 2031. The projection includes production from currently
          producing fields (including Jackdaw), and the NSTA’s estimate of production from



                                                                93                          Economic and fiscal outlook
Public sector receipts




                                                      development of new fields, which includes Rosebank, and from future discoveries.8 The
                                                      forecast annual pace of decline would be around 14 per cent a year if there were no
                                                      production from any new fields.

4.28                                                  Relative to our March forecast, oil and gas receipts are expected to be £2.6 billion higher in
                                                      2026-27, largely due to significantly higher oil and gas prices. However, the increase has
                                                      been partly offset by the latest assessment of the impact on receipts of mergers and
                                                      acquisitions (M&A) activity, described in Box 4.1. Receipts from the energy profits levy are
                                                      now expected to be £9 billion in total between 2026-27 and 2030-31, up from £2.5 billion
                                                      at the March forecast. The EPL is now expected to remain in place until September 2029,
                                                      when the Energy Security Investment Mechanism (ESIM) is expected to trigger based on
                                                      anticipated average oil and gas prices. This is two years later than in the March forecast.


         Box 4.1: The impact of mergers and acquisitions on oil and gas revenues
         Recent merger activity by firms operating in the UK continental shelf has resulted in companies
         with a large stock of tax losses combining operations with tax-paying companies, allowing
         greater use of losses against taxable profits. The 2021 to 2023 period saw expansion of larger
         and mid-sized oil and gas companies, outside of the major international groups. Since 2024,
         several of these companies have merged with portfolios held by major international companies.
         The impact of this activity was a key factor behind the downward revision to our November 2025
         forecast for offshore corporation taxa and in this forecast has muted the upward impact on

  The NSTA’s oil and gas projections are based on the informed judgement, which is guided by survey data, comparisons with other

factors which could impact the output from fields. More information is available from the Data and Insights section of the North Sea
Transition Authority website.


Economic and fiscal outlook                                                                                94
                                                                                                                   Public sector receipts




receipts this year of higher oil and gas prices. Our tax forecasts are generated by HMRC’s North
Sea microsimulation model, which captures M&A activity by combining information on company
ownership of fields and tax returns data. The mergers in 2025 and 2026 had a similar pattern
of dampening the receipts forecasts in both this and the November 2025 forecast, largely
through making the stock of historical losses more readily accessible to profitable companies.
The left panel of Chart A shows the impact on losses used of the mergers incorporated at this
forecast. The blue bars show the change in losses used due to recently completed mergers,
which increase by a net £2.7 billion between 2026 and 2031. The frontloaded pattern reflects
the assumption that, once historical losses become accessible, they are used as quickly as
possible. Higher oil and gas prices in the early part of the forecast also generate more profits
against which the losses can be used. This frontloading of loss utilisation results in a faster
rundown of the stock of outstanding losses, leaving fewer losses available for use in the later
years of the forecast.

The forecast also includes an assumption that a further 20 per cent of the stock of losses become
accessible from possible future mergers.b The impact of this, shown by the yellow bars, is to
increase losses used by £2.2 billion between 2026 and 2031.
The right panel shows the impact on receipts from the greater losses used, which mirrors this
pattern, with greater reductions in the early years of the forecast when more losses are available
against taxable profits. The net impact between 2026-27 and 2031-32 is a reduction of £1.3
billion from completed mergers and £0.8 billion from the adjustment for future mergers.

Road taxes
4.29             Fuel duty is expected to raise £24 billion (0.8 per cent of GDP) in 2026-27, which is £0.6
                 billion less than in 2025-26, largely due to higher pump prices reducing demand. Relative
                 to the March forecast, receipts are expected to be £0.7 billion lower this year mostly due to
                 policy changes and lower demand. In May 2026, the Government announced that fuel duty
                 rates would remain unchanged until January 2027. In this Budget, the Government has
                 announced that rates will remain unchanged until July 2027 at which point the 5p cut first
                 implemented in March 2022 will be unwound and then uprated by RPI. Receipts are then
                 forecast to increase by £1.8 billion (7 per cent) in 2027-28 and by £0.9 billion in 2028-29
                 assuming this policy is implemented.

4.30             On the assumption that the latest policy is implemented, fuel duty receipts are forecast to
                 peak in nominal terms at £27 billion (0.8 per cent of GDP) in 2028-29 before falling by
                 around £2.1 billion by 2031-32. This reflects the turning point when the additional revenue
                 from the assumed increase in fuel duty rates is forecast to be outweighed by the declining
                 tax base due to a rising electric vehicle (EV) share. Chart 4.7 shows that in practice fuel duty
                 revenues have been consistently lower than forecast. This is largely because despite
                 successive policy commitments to raise fuel duty rates, they have not actually been increased
                 since 2011-12. If the duty rate were to again remain unchanged at its current level
                 throughout the forecast period it would reduce receipts, on average, by £4 billion a year
                 between 2027-28 and 2031-32.

4.31             Vehicle excise duty (VED) receipts are expected to be £9 billion (0.3 per cent of GDP) in
                 2026-27, a 6 per cent increase compared to 2025-26 driven by an increase in vehicle
                 registrations. Across the forecast period, receipts are expected to increase in each successive
                 year, rising to £14 billion by the forecast horizon. The introduction of the electric vehicle

           excise duty (eVED) from April 2028 adds over £2 billion to VED receipts by 2031-32.
           Relative to the March forecast, VED receipts are expected to be lower by £0.2 billion on
           average across the forecast following weaker stock and outturn updates for non-EVs. This
           has been slightly offset by higher eVED receipts from higher fuel prices inducing faster-than-
           expected switches to EVs.

Capital taxes
4.32       Capital gains tax (CGT) is forecast to raise £26 billion (0.8 per cent of GDP) in 2026-27,
           around a 5 per cent increase from 2025-26. Receipts are then forecast to rise to £41 billion
           in 2031-32 (1.1 per cent of GDP), largely driven by a forecast of rising equity prices.

4.33       Relative to the March forecast, before the impact of policy measures, CGT receipts are
           expected to be £4.8 billion higher this year largely due to a revised assessment of the
           forestalling of receipts in 2025-26, which is explained below.9 Thereafter, CGT receipts are
           forecast to be higher than the March forecast by an average of £3.1 billion each year,
           driven by the 2025-26 receipts reassessment and the impact of higher forecast equity
           prices. The Budget 2026 policy to reform the taxation framework for distributions from
           companies to individuals is estimated to reduce CGT receipts by £1.2 billion in 2031-32.
           However, this is more than offset by the expected increase in SA income tax receipts from
           this measure. The net impact of the new Founders' Relief and the lower Business Asset
           Disposal Relief lifetime limit reduce receipts by an average of £0.1 billion a year from
           2027-28.

4.34       CGT receipts for 2025-26, which largely relate to 2024-25 asset disposals, were 78 per
           cent higher than the previous year. This was partly due to forestalling – an increase in the
           disposal of assets in 2024-25 ahead of anticipated CGT policy rate increases at Autumn
           Budget 2024. In the March 2026 forecast, we made a judgement that £4.3 billion of
           liabilities were brought forward due to this forestalling. This assumption was based on the
           average magnitude of forestalling seen ahead of anticipated policy changes before Budget
           2021 and ahead of rate changes in 2010. We assumed that £1.1 billion of these forestalled
           liabilities related to transactions that would have taken place later in that same year,
           resulting in a net £3.2 billion of additional liabilities in the 2024-25 tax year.

4.35       HMRC have since reviewed this forestalling assumption using a more detailed analysis of
           the pattern of asset disposals across the year.10 Compared with a 'normal' CGT tax year, the
           distribution of disposals in 2024-25 suggests that around £5.8 billion of CGT liabilities were
           brought forward due to forestalling. The analysis also suggests that a higher proportion of
           these forestalled liabilities than previously assumed were likely to have been brought
           forward from later in that year (£4.3 billion), because the pattern of disposals later in the
           year was relatively weak compared to previous years. This suggests there was a net £1.5
           billion of additional disposed liabilities in the 2024-25 tax year due to forestalling. The

9
  This also includes the impact of recent analysis of 2025-26 SA receipts which suggests that CGT receipts were £2.5 billion stronger than
assumed at the March forecast, some of which was offset by a reallocation which reduced SA IT receipts by £1.8 billion.
10
   HMRC extracted asset-level information from taxpayer returns and constructed a counterfactual path of asset disposals across the tax
year using 2023-24 data. This was compared to the actual level of asset disposals in 2024-25 to estimate the impact of forestalling.

Public sector receipts




           larger in-year unwinding increases the estimate for structural disposals in 2024-25. This,
           alongside the subsequent lower unwinding, boosts the forecast for disposals and receipts in
           the next three years compared to March.

4.36       CGT is one of the most volatile and therefore uncertain tax streams. This is reflected in the
           average size of the difference between CGT forecasts and outturn. Over the last ten
           forecasts, the one-year ahead CGT average absolute forecast difference was 24.5 per cent
           of forecast, compared with 4.3 per cent for total public sector current receipts.11 Revenues
           are sensitive to movements in asset prices, in particular equity prices (see Box 3.1 in the
           March 2026 EFO), which are typically highly volatile from year to year. In addition, receipts
           are highly sensitive to behavioural decisions made by a relatively narrow group of higher
           net worth taxpayers, who often have flexibility in choosing when to dispose of assets in
           response to changes in asset value or to policy changes.

4.37       Property transaction taxes are forecast to raise £17 billion (0.5 per cent of GDP) in 2026-
           27, a 0.6 per cent decline relative to 2025-26. This is driven by the share of year-to-date
           receipts in 2026-27 implying a lower annual total for the year compared to 2025.26.The
           recent fall in house prices in areas of London and the South East, where the level of
           property prices are higher than the national average, partly informs this judgement. Given
           the progressivity of the stamp duty land tax system, this has a relatively large effect on
           overall receipts. Receipts are then forecast to rise gradually to £26 billion (0.7 per cent of
           GDP) in 2031-32, driven by rising property transactions and house prices over the
           remainder of the forecast period.




11
   The average absolute forecast difference is calculated by taking the average of the absolute values of differences between outturns and
the central (point) forecasts, ignoring whether the differences are positive or negative. This indicates the absolute size of forecast
differences and therefore is a measure of how accurate the forecasts are.

4.38   Relative to the March forecast, property transaction tax receipts are expected to be around
       £3.2 billion lower in 2026-27 and an average of £3.9 billion a year lower over the rest of
       the forecast. Changes are largely due to the lower-than-expected in-year receipts for both
       residential and commercial sectors and reductions to the forecasts for house prices and
       property transactions.

4.39   Inheritance tax (IHT) receipts are forecast to raise £9 billion (0.3 per cent of GDP) in 2026-
       27, a 7.2 per cent increase on last year. Receipts are expected to continue to increase over
       the forecast, reaching £15 billion (0.4 per cent of GDP) in 2031-32, driven by rising equity
       and house prices, a growing proportion of UK deaths subject to inheritance tax, and the
       impact of policies announced in Autumn Budget 2024. Relative to the March forecast, IHT
       receipts are expected to be £0.5 billion lower on average, in every year of the forecast,
       largely driven by lower in-year outturn which is partly offset by higher equity prices.

4.40   An increasing proportion of IHT revenue is forecast to come from changes to the inheritance
       tax regime announced at and since Autumn Budget 2024, including taxing inherited
       pension pots and introducing changes to agricultural and business property reliefs, which
       account for around one-sixth of total revenue by the end of the forecast. The behavioural
       responses to these measures and the tax base for inheritable pension wealth are particularly
       uncertain.

Business rates
4.41   Business rates are expected to raise £35 billion in 2026-27, a 4.5 per cent increase relative
       to 2025-26. Business rates bills are calculated by multiplying the rateable value by the
       multiplier (which is typically uprated by CPI inflation) less reliefs for which the property is
       eligible. Growth in 2026-27 reflects several changes in the business rates system that took
       effect in April 2026. These include a revaluation in which the rateable values of non-
       domestic property were updated, a neutral change to multipliers with a lower rate for retail,
       hospitality and leisure properties and a higher rate for high-value properties and several
       temporary measures to cap bill increases resulting from the revaluation.

4.42   The 2026-27 estimate for business rates in England is based on forecast information from
       local authorities which was not available when we finalised the March forecast. This
       indicates that receipts this year will be £2 billion lower than previously assumed. Over the
       rest of the forecast period, the higher path for CPI inflation pushes up the multiplier, adding
       around £1 billion to the business rates forecast by 2030-31. This leaves business rates little
       changed from our March forecast by that year. In this Budget, the Government has
       announced a number of further measures which reduce business rates charged on
       properties in the retail, hospitality and leisure sectors, which lower receipts by £0.4 billion in
       2027-28 and £0.6 billion in 2028-29.




                                                  99                       Economic and fiscal outlook
Public sector receipts




Excise duties
4.43    Alcohol duty receipts are expected to raise £13 billion in 2026-27, a 2.7 per cent increase
        relative to 2025-26. Receipts are then anticipated to increase to £14 billion by 2031-32,
        driven by increases to the duty rate that offset an assumed average 1 per cent decline in
        alcohol consumption in each year. Compared to the March forecast, we assume a lower
        consumption trend, reflecting recent outturns, that reduces forecast receipts by an average
        of £0.2 billion each year.

4.44    Tobacco duty receipts are expected to raise £7 billion in 2026-27, a 1 per cent decrease
        relative to 2025-26, as higher duty rates only partly offset falling consumption. Receipts then
        fall to £6 billion by 2031-32 as consumption continues to decline. Vaping duty, effective
        from October 2026, raises £0.2 billion in 2026-27 and £0.7 billion by 2031-32.
        Compared to the March forecast, tobacco receipts are broadly unchanged.

4.45    Betting and gaming receipts are expected to raise £5 billion in 2026-27, a 31 per cent
        increase relative to 2025-26, driven by the increase to tax rates on remote gambling
        announced at Budget 2025. Thereafter, receipts are forecast to grow by an average of 3.4
        per cent a year to £6 billion in 2031-32. Compared to the March forecast, before the
        impact of policy measures, receipts are higher by an average of £0.2 billion. This is largely
        due to stronger-than-expected remote gaming duty receipts, suggesting a slower-than-
        expected reduction in activity in response to the rate increase. The increase to machine
        gaming rates announced at this Budget increases receipts by a further estimated £0.1 billion
        a year from 2027-28.

Other taxes
4.46    VAT refunds are forecast to cost £31 billion in 2026-27, a 2.7 per cent increase from 2025-
        26. Repayments then grow over the forecast in line with government consumption, reaching
        £36 billion by 2031-32. Relative to our March forecast, refunds are higher across the
        forecast period, by £0.1 billion in 2026-27 before rising to £0.5 billion in 2030-31. This is
        due to strength in outturn data feeding through the forecast, particularly for local
        authorities. VAT refunds are offset by spending and are neutral for public sector borrowing.

4.47    Environmental levies are expected to total £14 billion in 2026-27, rising to a forecast £16
        billion by 2031-32. This is due to an increase in electricity supply and generation in the
        capacity market and contracts for difference (CfD) forecasts, and an expected £1.1 billion
        increase from Sizewell C regulated asset base (RAB) levy receipts. Compared to the March
        forecast, environmental levies are on average £1.2 billion lower each year. This is mostly
        due to lower CfD receipts reflecting the Government’s decision to provide funding to reduce
        CfD levies on billpayers (see paragraph 3.19), the near-term rise in wholesale electricity
        prices, and project delays reducing electricity generation. Receipts relating to the capacity
        market scheme are lower towards the forecast horizon due to lower-than-expected prices at
        recent auctions. Most receipts from environmental levies are directly offset in spending
        because they are used to provide subsidies for energy generators. This means they are
        generally neutral for public sector borrowing.

Economic and fiscal outlook                      100
                                                                               Public sector receipts




4.48   Council tax receipts are expected to raise £54 billion in 2026-27, a 7 per cent increase
       from 2025-26 driven largely by increases in the rates of council tax in England, Scotland
       and Wales. Receipts are then forecast to rise to £70 billion by 2031-32 as both council tax
       rates and the number of households are assumed to grow. Receipts are broadly unchanged
       from our March forecast. Council tax receipts are directly offset by local authority spending
       and so they are neutral for public sector borrowing. For more information on the council tax
       rebate announced at this Budget, see paragraph 3.11 in Chapter 3.

4.49   Home Office fees are forecast to raise £6 billion in 2026-27, a 2.5 per cent increase on
       2025-26. Relative to our March forecast, receipts are £0.3 billion lower in 2026-27 due to
       lower-than-expected immigration health surcharge receipts. Thereafter, overall receipts are
       on average £0.2 billion higher in every year due to higher projected total in-country work
       visas, which is partly offset by our lower net migration forecast.

Other receipts
4.50   Interest and dividend receipts are forecast to raise £44 billion in 2026-27, a 7.2 per cent
       increase on 2025-26. Receipts then rise throughout the forecast, driven mainly by interest
       received from funded public sector pensions and accrued interest on student loans. Receipts
       are forecast to be higher than expected in March by around £3 billion in 2027-28 and
       2028-29. This reflects higher inflation increasing accrued interest on student loans and
       higher interest rates boosting returns on public sector assets, deposits and reserves. By
       2030-31, receipts return to the level assumed in the March forecast, driven by changes to
       the assumed rate of return on funded pension assets and inflation subsiding.

4.51   Gross operating surplus (GOS) is the sum of general government depreciation and public
       corporations’ (PCs’) operating surplus and is projected to reach £86 billion in 2026-27,
       rising to £105 billion by the forecast horizon. Relative to March, GOS is expected to be an
       average of £0.6 billion a year higher. General government depreciation is expected to rise
       from £69 billion in 2026-27 to £87 billion in 2031-32. This is £0.8 billion higher in 2026-
       27 than expected in March, with the difference rising to £2.7 billion in 2030-31. This is
       mostly due to higher depreciation rates in outturn, which we assume will persist throughout
       the forecast. Depreciation is offset by spending and is neutral for public sector borrowing.
       Changes in PC operating surplus since March largely reflect revisions to the outturn.




                                               101                      Economic and fiscal outlook
Economic and fiscal outlook   102
5     Public sector expenditure


Summary of the expenditure forecast
5.1   As Chart 5.1 shows, in the central forecast, public spending is expected to increase from
      44.5 per cent of GDP in 2025-26 to 45.7 per cent of GDP in 2027-28, before gradually
      declining to 44.7 per cent of GDP by 2031-32. This would be 5.5 percentage points higher
      than the pre-pandemic level in 2019-20, and 5.0 percentage points higher compared to
      the pre-financial crisis level of spending in 2006-07.

5.2   As shown in Table 5.1, the change in public spending over the forecast period (2026-27 to
      2031-32) is the result of changes in both departmental expenditure limits (DEL), which fall
      as a share of GDP in every year of the forecast, and annually managed expenditure (AME),
      which is forecast to peak in 2027-28 before falling gradually until 2030-31:

      •    Departmental expenditure limits are expected to rise as a share of GDP from 21.1 per
           cent in 2025-26 to 21.5 per cent in 2026-27 before gradually falling to 20.0 per cent
           by 2031-32. The planned fall in the later years of the forecast is explained by
           decreases in both day-to-day spending (resource DEL or RDEL) from 16.9 per cent of
           GDP to 16.3 per cent of GDP between 2027-28 and 2031-32, and in capital
           spending (capital DEL or CDEL) which falls from 4.0 per cent of GDP in 2027-28 to
           3.7 per cent of GDP in 2031-32. After the end of the current Spending Review period,
           these projections are based on assumptions set by the Government for overall total
           RDEL and CDEL, rather than on detailed departmental spending plans. As set out later
           in this chapter, there are significant pressures on departmental spending over this
           period.

      •    Annually managed expenditure is expected to rise as a share of GDP from 23.4 per
           cent of GDP in 2025-26 to 24.9 per cent in 2027-28, then fall to 24.4 per cent by
           2030-31, before rising again in 2031-32 to 24.6 per cent. Welfare spending and debt
           interest spending both rise from 2025-26 to 2027-28, and then are broadly stable
           until rising slightly again in 2031-32. There are forecast declines from 2027-28 in
           locally financed expenditure and in other areas of AME spending, as payments from
           compensation schemes decline and the deficit in unfunded public sector pension
           schemes falls as a share of GDP.

5.3   Chart 5.1 shows that total public spending as a share of GDP is higher in the near term
      relative to the March 2026 forecast. The difference then narrows so that the latest forecast is
      only slightly higher in 2030-31 at 44.6 per cent of GDP, compared to the 44.3 per cent of
      GDP forecast in March. These near-term increases are mainly driven by higher forecast
      spending on debt interest and welfare, explained in more detail later in this chapter.
