5.4                     As Chart 5.1 shows, the spending-to-GDP ratio in 2031-32 is forecast to be 5.5 percentage
                        points above the pre-pandemic level in 2019-20. Over that period there have been
                        significant changes in both the level and composition of public spending as illustrated in
                        Chart 5.2:

                        •     2019-20 to 2025-26: Spending spiked upward in 2020-21 to 13.1 per cent of GDP
                              above 2019-20 levels, driven by the introduction of temporary pandemic-related
                              income support schemes and loan guarantees, and higher departmental and welfare
                              spending. By 2025-26, while overall public spending had fallen back sharply from this
                              peak it was still 5.3 per cent of GDP higher than in 2019-20, due to higher debt
                              interest, welfare, and departmental spending.

                        •     2025-26 to 2031-32: Spending is forecast to rise by 1.1 per cent of GDP in 2026-27,
                              primarily due to higher debt interest spending and RDEL. Over the subsequent five
                              years, spending is forecast to fall by 0.9 per cent of GDP, mainly reflecting a decline in
                              RDEL (falling by 1.1 percentage points), but also CDEL and other AME. These declines
                              more than offset a forecast rise in debt interest of 0.3 percentage points and welfare of
                              0.3 percentage points.

                       •       Underlying forecast changes, which raise spending by 3.3 per cent of GDP relative to

Changes in spending since the March 2026 forecast
5.6   Table 5.2 shows that spending is forecast to rise by £31 billion a year on average over the
      forecast period compared to the March 2026 forecast. The main drivers of this increase
      include:

      •    Underlying forecast differences, which increase spending by £27 billion a year on
           average. This is primarily driven by debt interest spending which is £14 billion higher a
           year on average across the forecast period, largely due to higher Bank Rate and gilt
           rate expectations. Higher CPI uprating increases welfare spending by £5 billion a year
           on average. Changes in the local authority net spending and public corporations’
           capital expenditure forecasts described in paragraphs 5.26 and 5.32 further increase
           spending by £4 billion a year on average.

      •    PSNB-neutral forecast differences, which are offset in receipts, raise spending by £1.3
           billion a year on average.

      •    The direct effect of Government decisions announced in this Budget increases
           borrowing across the first five years of the forecast by £3.3 billion a year on average,
           driven largely by higher defence spending. The largely offsetting effects on DEL and
           AME of the unfunded pensions valuation are discussed in more detail in paragraphs
           5.8 and 5.32. Spending policy decisions are described in more detail in Chapter 3.

      •    The indirect effects of Government decisions announced in this Budget increase
           spending by £0.1 billion a year on average. This mainly reflects higher debt interest
           from the near-term increase in borrowing due to policy, and a reduced departmental
           underspend judgement in 2027-28. These effects are partly offset by lower benefit
           uprating due to some policy measures slightly reducing inflation.

Analysis of expenditure by category
Spending within departmental expenditure limits (DEL)
5.7       As set out in Table 5.1, spending subject to departmental expenditure limits makes up
          almost half of all public spending. The current spending review period runs to 2028-29 for
          resource, and 2029-30 for capital spending. As stated in paragraph 5.2, beyond these
          years there are no detailed departmental spending plans and so our forecast is based on
          assumptions for total resource and capital spending that are set by the Government. In line
          with the Charter for Budget Responsibility, the next spending review will take place in 2027
          and set DEL budgets for a minimum of three years. In the rest of this section, ‘RDEL
          spending’ refers to departmental resource, or day-to-day, spending, and ‘CDEL spending’


Economic and fiscal outlook                                       108
                                                                                                        Public sector expenditure




           refers to departmental capital, or investment, spending.1 Total DEL (or TDEL) is the sum of
           both RDEL and CDEL.

5.8        When analysing spending in real terms (that is, adjusted for the GDP deflator) over the
           forecast and when comparing real growth rates between this forecast and the last we have
           made the following adjustments:

           •      We have removed from the October 2026 forecast the reductions to RDEL related to
                  the 2024 valuation of unfunded public service pension schemes. The Government
                  Actuary’s Department recommended a lower employer contribution rate in the
                  valuation reports published thus far, primarily because of increases to the discount
                  (‘SCAPE’) rate.2 Employer pension contribution rates are reduced from 2027-28,
                  providing savings to departments, and the government has correspondingly reduced
                  RDEL by £16 billion a year. The lower pension contributions act to increase net
                  spending and increase AME by a similar amount to the reduction in RDEL, making
                  these changes broadly neutral for overall spending.3 The unfunded pensions forecast is
                  covered in more detail in paragraph 5.32.

           •      We have restated the March 2026 forecast for classification changes between DEL and
                  AME made at this forecast. For RDEL, this includes the switch of central government
                  subsidies to public corporations,4 and business rates under-indexation compensation
                  and relief grants from AME to DEL,5 which increase RDEL by an average of £3.3 billion
                  over the forecast period. For CDEL, the switch of the British Business Bank from DEL to
                  AME decreases CDEL by an average of £0.1 billion over the forecast period. 6

5.9        In cash terms, total departmental spending (TDEL) is forecast to rise over the forecast period
           to reach £762 billion in 2031-32, with RDEL set to reach £621 billion and CDEL £141
           billion, as shown in Table 5.4. As shown in Table 5.3 and Chart 5.4 by the dotted line, in
           real terms and excluding the adjustments above, RDEL spending is set to grow by an
           average 1.1 per cent over the forecast period, with fastest growth this year at 2.8 per cent. If
           we include the reduction in RDEL due to the 2024 valuation changes (the solid blue line in
           Chart 5.4), the real rate of growth of RDEL is on average 0.7 per cent. Real CDEL spending
           is set to grow at a rate of 0.4 per cent on average over the forecast. Within this there are

   More formally, unless otherwise stated these terms refer, respectively, to public sector current expenditure (PSCE) in RDEL and public

  The Superannuation Contributions Adjusted for Past Experience (SCAPE) rate is the discount rate used by unfunded public service

to CPI+2.0% per year in May 2026.

average £1.1 billion increase in spending between 2027-28 and 2031-32.

£1.5 billion a year.

  The government have moved business rate under-indexation compensation and relief grants from AME to DEL. This was not fully
captured in our March 2026 AME forecast and so adjusting for this decreases TME by around £0.2 billion a year.

  The government has moved spending on British Business Bank expected guarantees and write-offs from DEL to AME. This was captured
in our March 2026 forecast and so has no effect on TME.

        Departmental spending allocations in 2026-27
5.10      As Table 5.4 shows, RDEL is now forecast to be £555 billion in 2026-27, an increase on
          our March forecast of £7.2 billion. This difference is made up of:

          •      Policy measures which have increased spending by £5.5 billion in 2026-27 – primarily
                 a £2.6 billion council tax rebate and a £2.1 billion increase in defence spending.

          •      A set of reclassifications of AME spending into DEL (as set out in paragraph 5.8) have
                 overall increased DEL spending by an estimated £3.3 billion.

          •      These are partially offset by our judgement that overall departments will underspend
                 their resource budgets by a larger amount (£3.5 billion) than our previous March
                 forecast (£1.8 billion), based on our assessment of the most recent spending outturn
                 data so far this year.




                                                              111                            Economic and fiscal outlook
Public sector expenditure




    5.11        The lower panel of Table 5.4 shows that CDEL is now forecast to be £128 billion in
                2026-27 which is a £5.2 billion increase on our March forecast. This difference is
                almost entirely a reduction in our underspend judgement, which increases spending by
                £5.2 billion. We now expect that departments will underspend their capital budgets by
                £4.5 billion, compared to the £9.7 billion we estimated in March. Capital underspends
                were lower than we expected in 2023-24, 2024-25, and 2025-26 and departments
                have spent a higher-than-average proportion of capital than expected at this point in the
                year.7 Lower underspends may indicate departments have become better able to spend
                their budgets during periods of fast real growth in capital spending than was the case in
                the past.8

         Departmental spending allocations in the rest of the Spending Review period
5.12       As shown in the top panel of Table 5.4, RDEL is expected to increase by £18 billion over the
           remaining two years of the Spending Review period (2027-28 to 2028-29), to reach £573
           billion by 2028-29. This is £9 billion lower than we expected in the March 2026 forecast,
           with the main changes due to:

           •      Policy changes, including the reduction in RDEL to reflect lower employer pension
                  contributions, which lower RDEL spending by an average of £13 billion a year.
                  Excluding the pension change, RDEL policies increase spending by an average £3.0
                  billion, primarily reflecting higher defence funding.

           •      Classification changes increasing the forecast by an average £1.8 billion.

           •      Forecast changes reflecting updated estimates of the composition of DEL, with more
                  included as fiscal spending, further increase spending by an average £0.7 billion.

           •      An increase in our underspend judgement in 2028-29 reflecting the spending
                  pressures discussed in more detail below.

5.13       As shown in the lower panel of Table 5.4, CDEL is expected to increase by £6.0 billion over
           the remaining three years of the Spending Review period for capital spending, to reach
           £134 billion by 2029-30. Compared to the March 2026 forecast capital spending is on
           average forecast to be £1.4 billion lower over this period, with the main changes due to:

           •      Policy changes which reduce spending by an average of £1.5 billion a year. These are
                  primarily reductions to departmental capital budgets announced as part of the funding
                  for the defence investment plan.

           •      Small changes to the underspend judgements on average reduce spending by £0.3
                  billion to reflect lower pressures on capital budgets, and small classification changes

                  reduce spending by an average of £0.1 billion. Both are offset by forecast updates

                  average £0.5 billion.

         Pressures in the Spending Review period
5.14       There are some significant pressures on departmental spending limits over the remainder of
           the Spending Review. Particular risks include:

           •      Higher-than-expected inflation reduces the real spending power of departments as
                  DEL budgets are set in cash terms. Based on the increase in inflation in this forecast
                  compared to the March 2026 forecast, nominal spending would need to be on
                  average £2 billion higher in each year of the Spending Review to maintain the real
                  value of departmental spending plans. Compared to the inflation forecast in March
                  2025, when the current Spending Review envelope was set, nominal spending plans
                  would need to be on average £7 billion higher in each year than the original
                  Spending Review envelopes to maintain the real value of departmental spending
                  plans.

           •      Central government spending on public sector pay makes up around 40 per cent of
                  total RDEL. If all pay settlements were on average 1 per cent higher than expected this
                  would increase public sector pay spending by around £2 billion per year. Higher
                  inflation increases the general risk that there will be pressure for higher pay
                  settlements than anticipated when budgets were set.

           •      Efficiency savings: as part of the 2025 Spending Review, the Government published
                  departmental efficiency plans, with the aim of delivering £14 billion in annual
                  efficiency savings by 2028-29.9 Some past efficiency programmes have not delivered
                  the initially expected levels of savings.10 If this were to be the case again then this
                  would place further pressure on spending envelopes.

           •      Other pressures: some specific areas of spending where cost pressures could be
                  significantly higher or lower than expected include:

                   •      Special educational needs and disabilities (SEND) provision, where the number
                          of children with education, health and care plans (EHCP) increased by 12.5 per
                          cent between January 2025 and January 2026. While data suggests growth in
                          referrals has slowed over the same period, the impact that the reforms
                          announced in February 2026 will have on spending in this area remains very
                          uncertain.11 Elsewhere within schools the falling number of pupils in secondary

efficiency and savings performance which reports £16 billion of efficiencies and savings in 2025-26. This does not split out cash-releasing
and non-cash-releasing savings and so the implications for future spending plans and pressures are not clear. A more detailed
assessment will be published by large departments in their 2026-27 Annual Reports and Accounts.
11
   Department for Education, Education, health and care plans. September 2026

                          schools could reduce pressures on school spending if it results in a reduction in
                          the number of schools and teachers.12

                   •      Accommodation for asylum seekers, where the number of asylum seekers in
                          receipt of Home Office support has fallen by 12 per cent between June 2025
                          and June 2026, and at 90,000 is 25 per cent below the September 2023 peak
                          of 120,000.13 The numbers of asylum claims, and detected illegal arrivals, were
                          5,000 (6 per cent) and 500 (16 per cent) lower in 2025-26 compared to 2024-
                          25, suggesting the number of asylum seekers in receipt of Home Office support
                          will fall further if the Government can maintain the current level of claim
                          processing. The implications for local government spending is discussed in
                          paragraph 5.27.

                          pressures on prisons through the early prisoner release scheme could increase
                          costs in probation services given existing staffing shortages estimated at 3,150
                          sentence management staff in 2026-2714 mean the marginal cost of expanding
                          these services will be high. There are additional pressures in courts with crown
                          case backlogs standing at around 80,000 and open tribunal cases reaching the
                          highest level since 2012-13 at 860,000.15 16

5.15       There is a particular risk to RDEL spending plans in 2028-29 because they are due to be
           reconsidered in the next spending review. Historically, the real growth of RDEL has typically
           been higher than originally planned in the final year covered in overlapping Spending
           Reviews. Based on our assessment of these and wider pressures, at this event we assume
           that departments will not underspend against existing RDEL plans in 2028-29. This follows
           the judgement made in our November 2025 forecast to reduce the underspend assumption
           in 2028-29 to account for pressures from SEND spending. We have kept our underspend
           assumptions largely unchanged for CDEL.

         Departmental spending after the Spending Review period
5.16       Following the end of the Spending Review period, the Government provides an overall
           assumption for total resource and capital spending for the remainder of the forecast:

           •      Total departmental resource spending is assumed to reach £620 billion by the end of
                  the forecast, implying average annual real terms growth of 0.8 per cent over these
                  three years. In the final year of the forecast, the real-terms growth rate is reduced to
                  0.7 per cent, a reduction of £5.5 billion to the pre-measures spending assumption of
                  keeping RDEL constant as a share of GDP.

   Between 2019 and 2026 primary schools in England reduced the number of teachers and classes, but not schools, in response to
falling primary school pupils. The outlook on school funding in England, Institute for Fiscal Studies. September 2026

Economic and fiscal outlook                                       114
                                                                                                Public sector expenditure




          •      Total departmental capital spending is assumed to reach £140 billion by 2031-32,
                 implying real terms growth of 0.9 per cent in the final two years of the forecast.

5.17      To illustrate the spending implications of these assumed envelopes, we consider what
          commitments in some areas of spending imply for growth in spending in areas not covered
          by commitments, often called ‘unprotected’ spending. In terms of day-to-day departmental
          spending (RDEL), we assume the following:

          •      Spending on the NHS grows at 2.6 per cent a year in real terms after 2028-29, in line
                 with growth rates set at the 2025 Spending Review.

          •      Spending on defence is expected to rise from 2.6 per cent of GDP in 2026-27 to 2.7
                 per cent of GDP in 2029-30, and to then rise to 3.0 per cent of GDP in 2030-31 and
                 to 3.1 per cent of GDP in 2031-32, in line with the Government’s Defence Investment
                 Plan and its commitment announced at this Budget to reach 3.5 per cent of GDP in
                 2035. The total additional 0.9 per cent of GDP on defence spending in 2035-36
                 would represent around £35 billion in 2026-27 terms. We assume that the fraction of
                 NATO-qualifying defence spending that is spent on day-to-day expenditure within the
                 RDEL envelope is held constant to that planned in 2029-30.

          •      Spending on Official Development Assistance (ODA) grows in line with gross national
                 income (GNI) after 2028-29, in line with the Government’s commitment to spend 0.3
                 per cent of GNI on ODA by 2027.

          •      Underlying spending on special educational needs (SEND) grows by 0.4 per cent a
                 year in real terms reflecting the Treasury’s stated assumption, and spending on the
                 remaining core mainstream schools budget is held flat per pupil in real terms as a
                 policy-neutral baseline.

5.18      Within the assumed envelope for total RDEL spending provided by the Treasury, these
          assumptions would leave other ‘unprotected’ RDEL spending (accounting for just over a
          quarter of day-to-day departmental spending) falling by 2.6 per cent a year in real terms in
          the final three years of the forecast. Spending would have to be £11 billion higher in 2031-
          32 for unprotected spending not to fall in real terms from 2028-29. As outlined in Box 4.2
          in the March 2024 Economic and fiscal outlook (EFO), as spending reviews have
          approached, governments have increased annual real growth in resource spending over
          spending review periods by an average 1.1 per cent, equivalent to £6 billion in 2028-29.17
          Capital spending would need to be an average £2 billion a year higher for the remaining
          years of the current spending review period to restore its real terms value to that set out in
          the 2025 Spending Review. These calculations do not include the funding for HMRC and
          DWP compliance, debt, and fraud and error programs set out in Table 3.2. Including this
          funding would marginally increase the fall in unprotected departments to 2.7 per cent a
          year in real terms in the final three years of the forecast.

5.19                 There are other longer-term government policy ambitions which would create further
                     pressure on spending beyond the current spending review period but are not yet possible to
                     fully quantify as policy details have not been finalised. These include:

                     •      the ambition to carry out the biggest council housebuilding programme “since the
                            post-war boom ended in the early 1980s”,18 the cost of which would depend on the
                            final details of the programme and its scale. The capital grant required to cover the
                            cost of building a new property for social rent varies between around £150,000 and
                            £200,000 depending on location. The Government expects the existing Social and
                            Affordable Homes Programme (SAHP) to deliver 180,000 new social homes between
                            2026 and 2036.19 To illustrate the potential costs of this commitment, we assume that
                            to return council housebuilding to 1980s levels would require at least a further 22,000
                            houses to be built each year in addition to those expected through the SAHP scheme.20
                            If all these additional houses required capital grants, the additional cost would be
                            between £3 billion and £4 billion each year.

                     •      As discussed further in Chapter 3, the Government intends to reform social care
                            provision and establish a National Care Service in England. Estimates previously
                            produced by the Health Foundation suggest that, depending on the generosity and
                            design of the system, costs could vary between £3.5 billion and £17 billion a year.21

Welfare spending
5.20       Total welfare spending in the forecast refers to AME spending on social security and tax
           credits. Around half is subject to the Government’s ‘welfare cap’, which excludes the state
           pension and those payments most sensitive to the economic cycle (we discuss performance
           against the cap in Chapter 7). The welfare spending forecast is based on the determinants
           in the latest economy forecast – principally population, unemployment, earnings, and
           inflation – and informed by the latest outturn data and Department for Work and Pensions
           (DWP) models.

5.21       Welfare spending is forecast to rise this year by £19 billion (5.7 per cent) to £353 billion or
           11.1 per cent of GDP (Table 5.5). It is then forecast to rise in nominal terms by an average
           of £16 billion (4.4 per cent) a year over the rest of the forecast period, reaching £431
           billion or 11.3 per cent of GDP in 2031-32. This would be 1.3 per cent of GDP higher than
           its pre-pandemic level of 10.1 per cent of GDP in 2019-20, with 0.5 per cent of GDP of this
           increase occurring between 2025-26 and 2031-32. Chart 5.6 shows that this rise is driven
           primarily by health-related and pensioner benefit spending, which are forecast to be 0.4 per
           cent of GDP and 0.3 per cent of GDP higher respectively in 2031-32 than 2025-26, while
           spending on all other welfare is forecast to fall by 0.2 per cent of GDP over the same
           period. We explore trends in welfare as a share of GDP further in Box 5.1.

5.22       Health-related benefit caseloads have seen very high rates of growth since the pandemic,
           with the incapacity caseload growing from 2.5 million to 3.7 million between 2019-20 and
           2025-26, and the disability caseload growing from 4.7 million to 6.9 million over the same
           period.22 The extent to which these rates of growth will persist is a highly uncertain
           component of the forecast. We currently assume that some but not all of the drivers of this
           growth will persist into the medium term, with health-related spending forecast to grow by
           an additional 0.4 per cent of GDP over the forecast period on top of the 0.7 per cent of
           GDP growth observed between 2019-20 and 2025-26. If incapacity and disability
           caseloads kept increasing at post-pandemic growth rates, spending in 2031-32 would be
           roughly £25 billion higher than in our central forecast. If growth fell back to pre-pandemic
           rates, spending in 2031-32 would be roughly £13 billion lower than in our central forecast.

5.23       The latest outturn data on health-related benefit caseloads suggests both downside and
           upside risks to this judgement. Over the last 18 months, the approval rates at assessment
           have been falling for both incapacity and child/working-age disability benefits, such that a
           lower share of new claims receive a benefit award. If this trend were to continue then it
           could lead to a reduction in future caseloads and expenditure forecasts. Conversely,
           incapacity off-flow rates have been falling for several years in outturn. If sustained, this
           could lead to lower off-flows at subsequent forecasts than we are currently expecting,
           resulting in higher caseloads and expenditure forecasts.



22
  Incapacity caseloads here refer to claimants in Great Britain on either employment and support allowance, the universal credit health
journey, or both. Disability caseloads refer to claimants in England and Wales on disability living allowance, personal independence
payment, or attendance allowance.

       Box 5.1: Trends in welfare spending
       Chart A shows that welfare spending as a share of GDP this year (11.1 per cent) is relatively
       high by historical standards, though below the 12.3 per cent peak seen in 2012-13 around the
       height of the labour market downturn following the global financial crisis. The most significant
       trend since 2019-20 is the rise in health-related welfare spending, which after remaining fairly
       stable at roughly 2 per cent of GDP between 1993-94 and 2019-20 then rose to 2.8 per cent of
       GDP by 2025-26, and is forecast to rise to 3.2 per cent of GDP by 2031-32. The 1.0 per cent of
       GDP forecast rise in health-related welfare spending between 2019-20 and 2031-32 explains
       over three quarters of the overall rise in welfare spending expected over that period (1.3 per cent
       of GDP). This rising trend is against a backdrop where in steady state, we would expect welfare
       spending to be falling as a share of GDP (by roughly 0.1 per cent of GDP each year), as GDP
       growth per person should outstrip uprating when inflation, earnings growth, and GDP growth
       are all stable.

 The left panel of Chart B breaks down the components of the expected annual change in welfare
 spending as a per cent of GDP across the forecast. The forecast can be split out into three
 periods:

                    •       Relatively high growth in 2026-27, where welfare spending rises by 0.2 per cent of GDP.
                            This predominantly reflects growing caseloads for health-related benefits and the cost of
                            uprating benefits by inflation and earnings outstripping growth in GDP per person.a
                            Upwards pressure on pension spending from growth in the 66+ population is offset in
                            this year by the introduction of the state pension age rise to 67 in April 2026, meaning
                            state pension expenditure is largely unchanged as a share of GDP.
                    •       Lower overall growth is then forecast between 2027-28 and 2030-31. Through this
                            period, a rising 66+ population and rising health-related caseloads continue to push up
                            on welfare spending, but these upward pressures are mostly offset first by the rise in the
                            state pension age reducing state pension spending in 2027-28 and 2028-29, and then
                            by GDP growth per person outstripping the cost of uprating from 2029-30 onwards.
                            Through this period, there is also constant downward pressure from what are expected to
                            be mostly temporary trends – falling forecasts for unemployment and fraud and error, as
                            well as the impact of policy decisions on welfare awards.
                    •       An increase in growth is then forecast in 2031-32, where welfare spending is expected to
                            rise by 0.1 per cent of GDP. Around half of this increase compared to the previous years
                            is explained by an unwinding of temporary downward pressures, with unemployment and
                            fraud and error expected to be more stable by the final year of the forecast. The rest is
                            largely explained by an uptick in growth in housing-related spending across universal
                            credit and housing benefit. Increases in the pensioner population continue to place
                            upward pressure on the forecast and are no longer offset by the impact of the state

Policy decisions on awards includes the saving from freezing local housing allowance rates compared to keeping them maintained at
the thirtieth percentile, the cost of increasing the UC standard allowance at a rate faster than CPI uprating, the saving from halving

The right-hand side of Chart B presents the cumulative change in welfare spending as a share of
GDP across the forecast compared to 2025-26, which totals a 0.5 per cent of GDP rise between
2025-26 and 2031-32. Upwards pressure from underlying trends in pensioner demography and
health-related welfare caseloads together increase spending by 1.2 per cent of GDP over the
forecast. This is partially offset by factors including the impact of policy award decisions and the
rise in the state pension age from 66 to 67 between 2026 and 2028, which reduces spending by
0.3 per cent of GDP over the forecast.
The upward pressures reflect underlying trends in demography which are very likely to persist
beyond the medium term, while, as described above, the downward pressure largely reflects
temporary or one-off factors that run their course during the forecast period. In particular, the
rise in the state pension age will be complete by March 2028, meaning it has no further
downward impact on welfare spending from 2029-30 onwards. The next rise in the state
pension age is currently expected to occur between 2037 and 2039, meaning the state-pension-
age population and therefore pensioner spending is expected to grow significantly until then. The
degree to which health-related caseloads continue to place upward pressure beyond the medium
term is highly uncertain as set out in paragraph 5.22.

growth per person for 2026-27 (3.4 per cent), which means that inflation and triple lock uprating increase welfare spending this year
as a share of GDP

5.24       Relative to the March 2026 forecast, welfare spending is expected to be higher across the
           forecast by an average of £5 billion a year, and by £6 billion by 2030-31. Table 5.5 shows
           that this increase is driven by:

           •      Higher benefit uprating increases forecast welfare spending by £8 billion in 2030-31.
                  This is mostly driven by higher CPI uprating (£6.1 billion) and higher earnings
                  increasing the triple lock uprating for pensions (£1.5 billion).23

           •      Higher forecast health-related universal credit (UC) caseloads increase spending by £3
                  billion in 2030-31. This is partly driven by the latest outturn data which shows high
                  volumes of new claims for health-related UC in the period since the March forecast.

           •      Lower forecast working-age disability benefit onflows decrease spending by £1 billion
                  in 2030-31. New claims for personal independence payment (PIP), the working-age
                  disability benefit, have been higher than expected in 2026, but a much lower
                  proportion of these claims are being awarded the benefit after assessment compared
                  to previous years. The net impact of these two factors reduces the forecast for PIP
                  onflows compared to March.

           •      Lower forecast UC fraud and error reduces spending by £2 billion in 2030-31.
                  Following analysis of recent trends in fraud and error in our 2026 Welfare trends
                  report, we have revised down assumptions around the prevalence of fraud and error
                  in UC spending in the forecast period.24 The outlook for fraud and error is a
                  particularly uncertain element of the forecast.

5.26      Local authority and HRA borrowing has been at elevated levels since the pandemic,
          averaging £16 billion a year over the past four years compared to an average of £8 billion
          in the four years preceding the pandemic. We assume that borrowing levels will not fall
          significantly from these levels in the near term, but from 2028-29 onwards will fall to an
          average of £10 billion per year largely reflecting responsibility for funding special
          educational needs (SEND) provision moving to central government. These judgements are
          largely unchanged from March 2026, with net borrowing now expected to be an average of



                                                                  123                               Economic and fiscal outlook
Public sector expenditure




           £1.5 billion higher each year, across the forecast period. The main components of the local
           authority and HRA borrowing forecast are:

           •       HRAs are the ring-fenced financial accounts used by most LAs in England, Scotland,
                   and Wales to manage the income and spending related to the running of council-
                   owned housing stock. HRA net borrowing has risen strongly in the last decade from
                   £0.2 billion in 2015-16 to a forecast £7 billion this year (see Box 5.2). We forecast
                   HRA net borrowing will decline slightly to £6.4 billion by 2031-32. HRA net borrowing
                   is around £1 billion on average higher than in March, driven by evidence that local
                   authorities have not reduced borrowing in the face of higher interest rates to the
                   degree we previously expected.

           •       The cost of SEND deficits is forecast to grow from £3 billion in 2025-26 to £6 billion
                   by 2027-28 as the volume and cost of school-aged children in receipt of EHCPs
                   grows. From 2028-29, all SEND spending will be funded from within central
                   government DEL and so local authorities will no longer have to finance this. The
                   estimated cost of SEND deficits is broadly unchanged from March.

           •       Spending enabled by Exceptional Financial Support (EFS)25 is forecast to stay broadly
                   flat at around £1 billion over the next five years, following a period in which it
                   increased from £0.3 billion in 2021-22 to £1.4 billion in 2025-26. We previously
                   incorporated a forecast for EFS for only one financial year ahead, but given this
                   sustained rise we now assume it will continue in all years of the forecast.

           •       We expect other current spending to be in surplus and reduce overall LA borrowing by
                   an average of £4.6 billion each year, over the forecast period. This reflects our
                   assumption that rising council tax revenues, higher central government grants, the ‘fair
                   funding’ reforms, and central government funding of SEND from 2028-29 strengthens
                   the financial position of LAs over the forecast period.

           •       Net capital spending is forecast to fall slightly over the forecast period to £7 billion in
                   2031-32. We assume that the financial pressures facing local authorities and higher
                   interest rates will lead to lower spending on capital projects, but this is highly
                   uncertain. Relative to March, capital borrowing is higher by around £1 billion on
                   average each year over the forecast period, which reflects local authorities not
                   reducing borrowing in the face of higher interest rates by as much as expected.

5.27       The local authority and HRA forecasts are highly uncertain as they reflect decisions that will
           be taken by individual local authorities in response to the varied social, economic and
           financial environments that they face. Financial pressures on local authorities have
           increased in recent years which is shown by the increased numbers of authorities being
           granted EFS, from one in 2017-18 to 37 to date in 2026-27. In addition to HRA pressures,
           discussed in Box 5.2, some of the main pressures and risks in the forecast period include:

25
   EFS is a mechanism offered by central government to local authorities that would otherwise be at risk of being unable to meet their
statutory requirement to balance their budget. It is primarily provided through capitalisation directions, which allow local authorities to use
capital resource, including borrowing and capital receipts, to fund spending. A smaller proportion is typically delivered through additional
council tax flexibilities.


Economic and fiscal outlook                                         124
                                                                                                         Public sector expenditure




           •      SEND – local authorities will continue to face financial pressures from SEND related
                  spending until it is fully funded by central government from 2028-29, with local
                  authorities now expecting significantly higher deficits up to 2027-28 compared to their
                  own previous estimates.26 There is uncertainty around the impact that the recent
                  reforms in February 2026 will have on costs and on the numbers of children that
                  receive support. Further, while the Government has committed to reimburse local
                  authorities for 90 per cent of the deficits they accumulated on SEND spending through
                  to the end of 2025-26,27 the Government has made no commitment in relation to
                  deficits accumulated after that point.

           •      Temporary accommodation and asylum – English local authority spending on housing
                  support outside of the HRA has grown from £2 billion in 2010-11 to £4 billion in
                  2025-26, driven by increases in the cost of provision of housing for homeless families
                  and for refugees transitioning out of asylum accommodation. The Government’s
                  ambitions to reduce the level of homelessness and improve the standard of temporary
                  accommodation are likely to increase pressure on local authority spending in this area.

           •      Social care – which in 2025-26 accounted for around 30 per cent of local government
                  current expenditure in England – £44 billion – compared with around 20 per cent in
                  2010-11. Adult social care costs are likely to increase further given the ageing
                  population, rising demand among working-age adults, inflation and the minimum
                  wage. Local authorities in England also spent around £17 billion on social care for
                  children in 2025-26 and the number of children in England looked after by local
                  authorities grew from 64,000 in 2010 to 82,000 in 2025.28


     Box 5.2: The financial position of local authority Housing Revenue Accounts
     Housing Revenue Accounts (HRAs) are the financial accounts that the majority of local authorities
     use for the management, maintenance and improvement of around 2 million council homes in
     England, Scotland and Wales, which is around 7 per cent of the total stock of homes. Over the
     past decade, the financial position of many HRAs has come under increasing pressure. This
     reflects constrained rental income growth and rising expenditure costs.
     Rental income accounts for around 90 per cent of HRA income and is largely determined by
     government policy on social rents and the stock of housing. The stock of council housing in
     England has declined from over 5 million in 1980 to 1.6 million today, largely due to houses
     purchased under Right to Buy and some large-scale voluntary transfers of homes to private
     registered housing providers. In parallel, rents have largely not kept up with the rate of inflation
     in the last decade. Between the late 2000s and mid-2010s, social housing landlords, including
     local authorities, were generally permitted to increase rents by inflation plus an additional uplift.
     Policy reform in the 2015 Summer Budget required social rents in England to fall by 1 per cent a
     year between 2016-17 and 2019-20. Thereafter, rent increases resumed but remained subject

 to government regulation and have often been capped below prevailing inflation rates. Earlier
 this year, the government introduced a 10-year settlement from April 2026 to allow social
 housing landlords to increase rents by CPI+1 percentage point annually.
 The cost of construction and maintenance in the housing sector has increased substantially over
 the past decade, growing by over 40 per cent.a The impact of this is illustrated in the right panel
 of Chart A, which shows that the total costs of HRA capital works has risen even while the volume
 of works has fallen. Following the Grenfell Tower fire and subsequent reforms to social housing
 regulation, social landlords, including councils, have faced further spending pressure to meet the
 growing obligations to improve the quality and safety of housing, while also meeting separate
 obligations to improve the energy efficiency of the housing stock.
 As a result of these trends, as shown in the left panel of Chart C, the HRA net borrowing position
 has increased from around £0.1 billion to over £6 billion in the last decade. This reflects that
 HRA capital expenditure has increased from £5 billion in 2014-15 to £10 billion in 2024-25,
 and total spending has risen from £10 billion to £18 billion, while in the same period income
 has only increased from £10 billion to £12 billion.

 Many of the factors that have driven higher spending are likely to persist. While rental revenues
 are now set to rise above inflation, an ageing housing stock, growing regulatory requirements,
 and a backlog of repair, remediation and energy efficiency works, will continue to place pressure
 on expenditure. For example, the Government expects that meeting the new Decent Homes
 standard, set to be fully enforced by 2035, will cost local authorities around £5 billion.b The
 financial positions of individual HRAs vary significantly across local authorities, but these
 pressures are also likely to severely constrain the ability of many local authorities to significantly
 increase new council house construction.

Debt interest spending
5.28             Debt interest spending as a share of GDP is forecast to rise from 3.9 per cent of GDP in
                 2026-27 to reach 4.2 per cent of GDP in 2031-32 (Chart 5.7). This would be above the
                 previous highs reached in the 1960s and 1980s and more than double the average of the
                 ten years prior to the pandemic. The increase in debt interest costs since the pandemic has
                 been driven by both the rising stock of debt and by the sharp increases in interest rates over
                 that period. However, interest rates are not high by historical standards, as is also shown in
                 Chart 5.7. The effective interest rate29 on government debt of around 4 per cent over the
                 forecast period is less than half the 1980s peak, of 10 per cent, and below the average rate
                 over the two decades preceding the 2008 financial crisis of 7.5 per cent.

5.29             In nominal terms, debt interest spending is forecast to rise from £124 billion in 2026-27 to
                 £160 billion in 2031-32, an average year-on-year increase of around £7 billion. This is
                 driven by new borrowing which increases the stock of debt on which interest accrues each
                 year. In addition, an average of £184 billion of maturing gilts are refinanced each year,
                 typically at higher interest rates than the debt they replaced, which also contributes to the
                 rise in spending. In 2030-31, the year-on-year increase in debt interest spending slows to
                 £2.2 billion because the alignment of RPI with CPIH30 reduces RPI growth and therefore the
                 uplift applied to index-linked gilts. In the final year of the forecast, year-on-year growth in
                 debt interest spending returns to the previous trend, increasing by £10 billion, reflecting new
                 borrowing and significant redemptions in that year from the large volume of gilts issued
                 during the Covid period. Chapter 6 has further discussion of the impact of redemptions and
                 the change in the maturity composition of gilt issuance.

5.30    Compared to March, debt interest is forecast to be £14 billion higher on average over the
        forecast period. This largely reflects higher RPI inflation in the near term and higher Bank
        Rate and gilt rates expectations in the medium term:

        •     Revisions to the RPI inflation forecast increase spending by £12 billion in 2026-27 and
              £6.5 billion in 2027-28, and then by an average of £1.2 billion until 2030-31.

        •     Higher Bank Rate and gilt rates are forecast to increase debt interest costs compared
              to March 2026 by £3.9 billion in 2026-27 and then by an average of £11 billion a
              year throughout the forecast period.

        •     Financing and other factors are expected to decrease spending in the first two years of
              the forecast, by an average of £0.9 billion, but then increase it by £0.8 billion in
              2030-31.

        •     The effects of policy decrease debt interest in 2026-27 by £0.9 billion, which reflects
              the direct impact on inflation of policy measures this year, and then raises it by an
              average of £0.8 billion a year thereafter mostly due to increased borrowing.

PSNB-neutral spending and other annually managed expenditure
5.31    PSNB-neutral spending includes spending which is offset in receipts and therefore does not
        affect public sector net borrowing (PSNB). PSNB-neutral spending has increased by £0.9
        billion on average since the March 2026 forecast. The main changes are increases in
        depreciation and VAT refunds, and decreases in environmental levies, which are described
        in paragraphs 4.46-4.51 of Chapter 4.



Economic and fiscal outlook                      128
                                                                                                 Public sector expenditure




5.32      Some of the main other items of AME spending include:

          •      Net spending on unfunded public service pensions is forecast to move from a deficit of
                 £1.1 billion in 2026-27 to a deficit of £14 billion in 2027-28. This is primarily due to
                 the 2024 valuation of these schemes, described in more detail in paragraph 5.8,
                 which reduces employer contributions and increases net pension spending by around
                 £16 billion a year from 2027-28 onwards. The lower employer contributions represent
                 a saving for departments, which are offset by a broadly equivalent reduction in RDEL,
                 leaving overall spending around £1.1 billion higher a year. Spending is also around
                 £2 billion higher than in March due to higher forecast inflation and pensioner
                 caseloads. From 2027-28, the deficit is expected to remain largely flat in cash terms at
                 around £15 billion and therefore to fall slightly as a share of GDP.

          •      Public corporations excluding the HRA are forecast to spend £8 billion on capital by
                 2030-31, around £1 billion more than we forecast in March 2026. This is driven by a
                 combination of new outturn data which was around £0.6 billion higher than expected
                 in March and changes to the nominal GDP forecast.

          •      The cost of the Post Office redress and Infected Blood compensation schemes is
                 expected to peak at £3.8 billion in 2026-27 before falling to £0.2 billion in 2031-
                 32.31 Spending on these schemes was £1.5 billion less in 2025-26 than expected in
                 the March 2026. Given the ongoing rollout of these schemes, there remains
                 uncertainty around the number of eligible claimants, average award amounts and how
                 quickly claims will be processed which could change the total amount of spending and
                 its profile.

          •      Capital spending on the construction of the Sizewell C nuclear power station, which is
                 estimated to be £3.7 billion in 2026-27 before rising slightly to £3.9 billion in 2031-
                 32. Compared to the March 2026 forecast, spending is expected to be an average of
                 £0.8 billion higher between 2026-27 and 2029-30 reflecting Sizewell C’s revised plan
                 to frontload more of the total capital spending.

  The Post Office redress schemes refer to the following redress schemes: Horizon Shortfall Scheme, HSS Appeals, Overturned

Economic and fiscal outlook   130
6     Fiscal aggregates


Introduction
6.1   This chapter details how changes in our pre-measures forecast, and policy measures since
      our March 2026 forecast, affect key summary measures of the public finances, including:

      •    deficit aggregates, including public sector net borrowing (PSNB), the difference
           between expenditure and receipts; the current budget deficit, the difference between
           current (day-to-day) expenditure and receipts; the primary deficit, the difference
           between non-interest expenditure and receipts; and cyclically adjusted measures of the
           deficit, which take account of the position of the economy relative to its potential level
           of output;

      •    financial transactions, which capture the effects of public sector net lending, sales or
           purchases of financial assets, and interventions which affect the Bank of England’s
           (BoE’s) balance sheet; and

      •    balance sheet aggregates, including public sector net debt (PSND), which includes all
           debt obligations net of liquid financial assets; public sector net financial liabilities
           (PSNFL), which includes all financial liabilities and financial assets; and public sector
           net worth (PSNW), which includes all financial and non-financial assets and liabilities.

Deficit aggregates
Borrowing and the current budget deficit
6.2   Chart 6.1 shows that public sector net borrowing increased sharply to 10.2 per cent of GDP
      in 2009-10 following the financial crisis, before falling in each subsequent year to reach 2.0
      per cent of GDP in 2018-19. It then increased again to a record high of 14.7 per cent of
      GDP in 2020-21 due to the pandemic and the policy response to it, reflecting both the large
      rise in cash borrowing and the fall in nominal GDP in that year. After the pandemic,
      borrowing fell to around 5 per cent of GDP in 2021-22 and remained close to that level for
      the following three years before falling again to 4.4 per cent in 2025-26.

6.3   Chart 6.1 and Table 6.1 show that, in our central forecast, PSNB is expected to fall slightly
      from 4.4 per cent of GDP (£134 billion) in 2025-26 to 4.2 per cent (£133 billion) in 2026-
      27. It then declines steadily to reach 1.8 per cent of GDP (£67 billion) in 2030-31 and
      remains flat as a share of GDP in 2031-32. PSNB is now expected to be higher in each year
      of the forecast compared to March, by 0.5 per cent of GDP in the near term and 0.2 per
      cent of GDP in 2030-31.


                                               131                      Economic and fiscal outlook
Fiscal aggregates




6.4                     Chart 6.1 and Table 6.1 also show that the current budget deficit is forecast to fall from 1.5
                        per cent of GDP in 2025-26 (£47 billion) to 1.3 per cent (£41 billion) in 2026-27. It is then
                        forecast to improve in each year to reach a surplus of 0.4 per cent of GDP (£15 billion) in
                        2029-30, rising to 0.6 per cent in 2030-31 (£22 billion) but then falling to 0.5 per cent
                        (£21 billion) in 2031-32. As with PSNB, the forecast for the current budget deficit has seen a
                        moderate deterioration in each year compared to the March.

6.6                   Both PSNB and the current budget deficit are expected to fall gradually as a share of GDP
                      over the forecast, but these improvements begin to level off by the end of the forecast
                      period, with PSNB remaining flat and the current budget deficit declining slightly as a share
                      of GDP in 2031-32. Box 6.1 investigates the underlying drivers of this trend in more detail.


          Box 6.1: The forecast rise in underlying borrowing from 2030-31 to 2031-32
          In the central pre-measures forecast, that is before accounting for the effects of new policy
          measures, public sector net borrowing falls year on year by around half a per cent of GDP until
          2030-31, at which point it is forecast to rise by 0.1 per cent to 2.0 per cent of GDP (Table A).
          This is an unusual pattern because typically at the end of the forecast – when the economy is
          usually assumed to be growing at trend and departmental spending is often assumed to be
          rising in line with nominal GDP – borrowing would be expected to be falling as a share of GDP.
          This is often driven by ‘fiscal drag’, where tax receipts rise as a share of GDP because earnings
          are assumed to rise faster than CPI inflation (which is used to uprate personal tax thresholds at
          the end of the forecast) and by some other areas of underlying buoyancy in the tax regime.

          Table A: Pre-measures public sector borrowing
                                                                             Per cent of GDP
                                                        Outturn                        Forecast
                                                        2025-26 2026-27 2027-28 2028-29 2029-30 2030-31 2031-32
             Public sector net borrowing (a - b)             4.4    4.0     3.2        2.8      2.2 1.9     2.0
             of which:
               Total managed expenditure (a)                 44.5       45.4   45.4     45.1         44.6    44.5         44.7
               Public sector current receipts (b)            40.1       41.4   42.2     42.3         42.5    42.6         42.6

                                                                        133                       Economic and fiscal outlook
Fiscal aggregates




 In the current forecast, however, public sector current receipts increase by an average of 0.3 per
 cent of GDP a year between 2026-27 and 2030-31, but are then flat at 42.6 per cent of GDP in
 2031-32. Total managed expenditure falls between 2026-27 and 2030-31, by an average of
 0.2 per cent of GDP, but then rises slightly in the final year. So the rise in PSNB in 2031-32 in
 the pre-measures forecast is driven by flat receipts and slightly rising spending as shares of GDP.
 The drivers of these trends relate to the emergence of pressures that we identified in the 2026
 Fiscal risks and sustainability (FRS) report and which will intensify over the longer term.
 Specifically, the upward pressure on pension spending from the ageing population, the reduction
 in tax revenues from the loss of fuel duty due to the transition to electric vehicles, and the
 increasing cost of debt interest due to the rising stock of debt. Looking at these components in
 more detail:

     •   Welfare spending is forecast to rise by around 0.1 per cent of GDP in 2031-32. This
         reflects increasing health-related caseloads and forecast growth in the state pension
         caseload due to the ageing population. Over previous years of the forecast, the
         underlying upward trend in pensioner spending is suppressed by the increase to the state
         pension age between April 2026 and March 2028. This is assessed in more detail in Box
         5.1.
     •   Fuel duty receipts are forecast to decline slightly as a share of GDP in 2031-32, as the
         loss of revenue due to the transition to electric vehicles is greater than the increase in
         revenue from the assumed increase in fuel duty rates at this point. In addition, there are
         small declines in revenue as a share of GDP from assumed declines in the consumption
         of tobacco and alcohol, and from declining oil and gas revenues, as discussed in
         Chapter 4. Overall, the decline in revenues from these sources push up on borrowing by
         around 0.1 per cent of GDP.
     •   Debt interest spending increases borrowing by a further 0.1 per cent of GDP in 2031-32.
         This is driven by the rising stock of debt and by the rising number of gilts that are forecast
         to be refinanced at this time horizon at higher interest rates, discussed further in
         paragraph 5.29.
 Overall, in 2031-32, these three effects increase borrowing by around 0.3 per cent of GDP,
 which more than offsets all the other pre-measures year-on-year changes to tax and spending
 which are forecast to reduce borrowing in 2031-32 by around 0.2 per cent of GDP. This
 includes some fiscal drag as earnings are forecast to rise faster than CPI at this time horizon.
 However, this effect is more muted than in previous years of the forecast where the personal tax
 thresholds are frozen.
 In this Budget, the Government has decided to reduce the pre-measures assumption for the
 growth in departmental spending in 2031-32, which, combined with policies to increase
 taxation, helps reduce the forecast for borrowing in this year by 0.2 per cent of GDP. This means
 that, after the impact of policy, borrowing remains flat between 2030-31 and 2031-32 as a
 share of GDP. This is an early indication of the additional pressures that the long-term trends
 highlighted in the 2026 FRS will create for policy-makers aiming to put the public finances on a
 sustainable path over the coming decades.



Economic and fiscal outlook                       134
                                                                                   Fiscal aggregates




Change in forecast borrowing and the current budget deficit since March
6.7   Table 6.2 and Chart 6.3 show that, compared to the March 2026 forecast, PSNB is now
      forecast to be higher in each year, by £18 billion in 2026-27, £16 billion in 2027-28, and
      an average of £11 billion each year thereafter. This reflects:

      •    Upwards revisions to the pre-measures spending forecast, which increase PSNB by an
           average of £28 billion per year. This primarily reflects higher debt interest spending,
           due to higher RPI inflation and interest rates, which increase borrowing by an average
           of £14 billion each year. Other changes include higher welfare spending largely due
           to higher CPI inflation, and higher capital spending by departments, described further
           in Chapter 5.

      •    Upwards revisions to the pre-measures receipts forecast which reduce PSNB by an
           average of £17 billion per year. These are largely driven by a higher forecast for
           income tax and NICs and corporation tax, reflecting higher forecasts for nominal
           earnings and profits, described further in Chapter 4.

      •    The direct effect of Budget policy measures, which increase PSNB in each year until
           2028-29, reflecting policy decisions which reduce receipts in 2026-27 and increase
           spending in each year. From 2028-29 onwards a set of tax measures reduce
           borrowing by £7 billion per year on average. A reduction to the pre-measures
           assumption for total resource departmental spending further reduces borrowing in
           2031-32 by £6 billion.

      •    The indirect effects of Budget policy measures decrease PSNB in 2026-27 by £1.3
           billion, before increasing it by an average of £2.1 billion per year thereafter. This
           reflects the impact of policies which lower inflation in 2026-27 and in subsequent
           years the debt interest costs from higher borrowing.

6.8   The lower panel of Table 6.2 shows changes in the forecast for the current budget deficit
      since March. As with PSNB, the current budget deficit has deteriorated in each year, with a
      £9 billion reduction in the surplus forecast for 2029-30, the target year for the
      Government’s fiscal mandate. Differences between the revisions to the forecasts for PSNB
      and the current budget deficit reflect the increase in capital spending in this forecast
      compared to March (as set out in Chapter 5 and shown in the green bars in Chart 6.3),
      partly offset by a higher forecast for depreciation (described in paragraph 4.51). These
      effects mean that in 2026-27 the deterioration in the current budget deficit is £11 billion
      less than for PSNB. The net impact of changes to capital spending and depreciation on the
      current budget deficit reduce over the forecast period until largely cancelling each other out
      in 2030-31.

6.9               The forecasts for deficit aggregates such as PSNB and the current budget deficit are
                  particularly uncertain given that they measure the difference between around £1.5 trillion a
                  year of both receipts and spending by the end of the decade. A small percentage change in
                  the forecast for either can therefore lead to a large percentage change in the forecast for
                  the deficit. For example, in 2029-30 year on year receipts growth is only forecast to be 2
                  percentage points higher than spending growth, but as a result PSNB is forecast to fall by
                  26 per cent compared to the previous year.

6.10              Chart 6.4 highlights this uncertainty by showing a range of possible outcomes around the
                  central forecast for PSNB, based on the distribution of historical shocks. It shows a 60 per
                  cent chance of PSNB falling between a surplus of 1.0 per cent of GDP (£38 billion) and a
                  deficit of 4.4 per cent of GDP (£168 billion) in 2031-32. The key current risks and
                  uncertainties around the forecasts for tax, spending and wider fiscal aggregates are
                  assessed in detail in Chapter 7.

Other deficit measures
6.11                   Other deficit measures also provide relevant information on the state of the public finances.
                       The primary deficit, which excludes net interest spending, is a useful proxy of the extent to
                       which discretionary spending is covered by revenues. All measures of the deficit can be
                       presented in cyclically adjusted terms, correcting for an estimate of the position in the
                       economic cycle, which provides a rough indication of the underlying or structural deficit:

                       •    The primary deficit moves from 1 per cent of GDP this year to a surplus of 1.7 per cent
                            of GDP in 2031-32. If achieved, this would be the largest primary surplus since 2000-
                            01. Compared to the March forecast the primary deficit is an average of 0.1 per cent
                            of GDP higher in each year until 2029-30, but the same in 2030-31. This reflects that
                            the medium-term deterioration in PSNB compared to March can be seen as driven
                            largely by higher net interest spending, with other changes to spending and receipts
                            broadly offsetting.

                       •    Cyclically adjusted measures of the deficit are 0.7 per cent of GDP below the
                            unadjusted metrics in 2026-27 and 2027-28. The gap narrows over each of the
                            subsequent years until 2031-32 when it closes. This reflects the negative output gap
                            this year and subsequent closing of the output gap across the forecast.




Economic and fiscal outlook                                       138
                                                                                                                      Fiscal aggregates




Financial transactions
6.12       Changes in public sector net debt and wider balance sheet aggregates, including public
           sector net financial liabilities, are calculated by combining changes in borrowing with
           changes in financial transactions and valuation effects. Financial transactions capture the
           effects of public sector net lending, sales or purchases of financial assets, and interventions
           which affect the Bank of England’s (BoE’s) balance sheet. They also convert the accrued
           measures of tax and spending which underpin our forecast for borrowing into the cash
           flows relevant to debt. Valuation effects capture changes in the value of the assets or
           liabilities held by the public sector which count toward the measure of net debt.

6.13       In nominal terms public sector net debt is forecast to increase in each year from 2026-27
           onwards, by an average of £129 billion a year. Table 6.3 breaks down the contribution to
           the year-on-year increases in PSND from public sector net borrowing, pre-measures
           financial transactions, policy measures which affect financial transactions, and valuation
           effects. It shows:

           •       Public sector net borrowing is forecast to increase PSND in each year from 2026-27,
                   by an average of £92 billion, as discussed in paragraph 6.7.

           •       Pre-measures financial transactions increase debt in each year of the forecast, due to:

                           •     The cost of student loans, which increase debt on average £9 billion a year
                                 over the forecast reflecting new lending to students.

                           •     Repayments to the Bank of England’s Term Funding Scheme (TFS) which
                                 reduce debt by £26 billion in 2026-27. The TFS then has no further effect
                                 until 2030-31 where it reduces debt by £16 billion due to the expected one-
                                 off payment as the scheme comes to an end.

                           •     Losses on gilts held within the Asset Purchase Facility (APF), incurred when
                                 gilts are sold for less than their face value, raise debt by an average of £4.3
                                 billion a year.

                           •     Net lending and purchases of equity by public financial institutions (PuFins)
                                 increase debt by an average of £3.1 billion each year.1

           •       Cash flow timing effects, which convert the accrued measures of tax and spending in
                   the forecast for borrowing into the cash flows relevant to debt, raise debt by an
                   average of £19 billion a year. Of this, accruals adjustments for taxes contribute an
1
  These illiquid financial assets acquired by the PuFins are not counted within public sector net debt, which therefore only records the gilts
issued to finance their acquisition. But the assets are included within public sector net financial liabilities, which are described from
paragraph 6.27. The interest payable on the gilts issued to finance the activities of the PuFins is captured within our forecast for central
government debt interest spending, the income on the assets is mostly recorded within the interest receipts forecast, and the losses on the
assets are recorded as capital spending. For more information on how PuFins affect the public finances and the associated risks and
uncertainties see Lockwood, I., M. Hanson and T. Baiju, Forecasting public financial institutions, OBR articles, August 2026. UK
Government Investments produces experimental estimates of the performance of the assets of PuFins relative to financing costs in its
Financial Investment Report 2026.


                                                                    139                                Economic and fiscal outlook
Fiscal aggregates




             average of £10 billion a year and the removal of modified interest on student loans an
             average of £9 billion a year.

        •    The direct effects of policy measures on financial transactions increase debt in every
             year of the forecast, apart from 2029-30, by on average of £2.7 billion. This is mainly
             due to policies which increase the funding for the National Wealth Fund and British
             Business Bank to invest in financial assets (see paragraph 3.20 for more detail). In
             2029-30, the policy to introduce timely payment for self assessment decreases debt by
             £17 billion due to the additional tax payments brought forward to that year (see
             paragraph 3.13 for more detail). These cash payments have no effect on accrued
             measures such as PSNB or PSNFL.

        •    Valuation effects increase debt by £11 billion in 2026-27 and an average of £1.2
             billion thereafter. These largely reflect the consequences of new gilts being issued at a
             discount to the face value they are recorded at in the public finances, an effect which
             subsides over the forecast as we assume that a greater proportion of gilts are issued
             for nearer to face value.

6.14    The bottom panel of Table 6.3 shows that, relative to March, year-on-year growth in debt is
        forecast to be higher in each year of the forecast, aside from 2029-30 (where the
        downwards revision reflects the policy to introduce timely payment for self assessment).
        These upwards revisions are largely driven by higher PSNB, reflecting the factors set out in
        paragraph 6.7. There is a particularly large upward revision to PSND in 2026-27, of £33
        billion, which is driven by revisions to financial transactions and valuation effects which add
        a combined £16 billion to debt, in addition to the increase to the PSNB forecast in this year.
        Revisions to underlying financial transactions and valuation effects across the subsequent
        forecast years are modest, reflecting the offsetting effects of upwards revisions to cash flow
        timing effects and lower effects from APF sales losses and student loans.

Asset Purchase Facility
6.15       The impact of the Bank of England’s Asset Purchase Facility (APF) on fiscal aggregates over
           the forecast period is dependent on changes in Bank Rate, gilt yields, and the assumed pace
           at which the stock of gilts held within the APF is unwound. After reducing its gilt holdings by
           £70 billion in 2025-26, the Monetary Policy Committee decided at its September 2026
           meeting that active sales will be paused until April 2027. Thereafter, the MPC stated that the
           APF will unwind with a constant pace of active sales of £20 billion a year, which results in




                                                                141                             Economic and fiscal outlook
Fiscal aggregates




           an average annual reduction in gilts of £53 billion a year over the forecast, when including
           redemptions (Table 6.4).2

6.16       The APF’s direct impact on the current budget deficit, the Government’s target measure for
           its fiscal mandate, is driven only by APF interest losses. APF valuation losses on sales or
           redemptions do not affect the current budget deficit but do increase cash transfers from
           central government to the Bank. Both interest losses and valuation losses on sales impact
           PSND and PSNFL (Table 6.5).

6.17       APF interest losses are forecast to decrease the forecast current budget surplus in 2029-30
           by £8 billion. This is £3.3 billion greater than forecast in March due to higher market-
           implied Bank Rate and the slower pace of the unwind of gilt holdings. APF transactions
           increase PSNFL and PSND by around £12 billion on average a year over the forecast
           period. The slower assumed pace of APF unwind reduces sales losses by £0.4 billion by
           2029-30 compared to the March forecast. This means that, overall, the impact of APF
           interest and sales losses on PSNFL is £3.0 billion higher in 2029-30 than we forecast in
           March.3

billion will be sold following the MPC’s intended annual sales pace of £20 billion starting in April 2027. This means the APF will not be
fully unwound until 2071.
3
  A full estimation of the APF’s impact on the public finances requires consideration of central government financing. Interest losses and

6.18       Chart 6.5 shows outturn and projections for the nominal quarterly income, outlays and

           under the MPC’s multi-year plan. Based on these projections the estimated cumulative
           lifetime cost of the APF in net present value terms is £114 billion or in real terms is £140
           billion.4 As shown in Chart 6.5, the APF income flows which underpin this estimate are very
           sensitive to the future path of interest rates, which is highly uncertain. A comprehensive
           assessment of the overall economic impact of the quantitative easing (QE) programme
           would take account of its effects on, among other things, government financing costs, asset
           prices and labour and financial markets.5 The Bank of England has estimated that reduced
           debt interest costs due to QE have resulted in fiscal savings of between £50 billion and
           £125 billion.6
