Financing requirement
6.19               Public sector net borrowing is comprised of central government, local government, and
                   public corporations’ borrowing. The central government net cash requirement (CGNCR),
                   which only covers central government and is on a cash basis, forms the basis of the Debt
                   Management Office’s (DMO’s) financing remit and so is the primary driver of the net
                   issuance of gilts.

6.20               Table 6.6 shows the relationship between the CGNCR and PSNB. First, financial
                   transactions are added to borrowing to get the cash requirement of the whole public sector
                   (PSNCR). The cash requirements for non-central government (local authorities, public
                   corporations and Network Rail) are then removed to get to the CGNCR excluding Network
                   Rail (CGNCR ex), which is the measure that feeds directly into the Government’s gilt
                   issuance plans, which also cover the rolling over of maturing debt securities.

6.21               The CGNCR ex is forecast to be £144 billion this year, to peak at £150 billion in 2028-29,
                   and then to fall to £105 billion at the end of the forecast. It is on average £36 billion higher
                   per year than PSNB across the forecast period, which largely reflects the additional
                   financing required for the financial transactions set out in paragraph 6.13. Changes since
                   the March forecast are broadly similar to changes in PSNB in 2028-29 and 2030-31. In
                   2026-27 and 2027-28, the change in CGNCR ex compared to March is considerably less
                   than the change in PSNB. This is because the inflation driven increases to the cost of index-
                   linked gilts only affects the accrued measures of borrowing and does not need to be
                   financed for many years. In 2029-30, the £17 billion of additional cash payments



Economic and fiscal outlook                                        144
                                                                                                                  Fiscal aggregates




           associated with the policy to introduce timely payment for self assessment (explained in
           further detail in paragraph 3.13) reduces the CGNCR ex, but does not affect PSNB.

6.22       As shown in Chart 6.6, gross gilt issuance by the DMO averages 8.1 per cent of GDP (£287
           billion) per year across the forecast period (blue bars). Gross gilt issuance comprises new
           financing and the refinancing of the gilts which redeem each year. Net gilt issuance (black
           line) is therefore lower at an average of 2.9 per cent of GDP per year, reflecting the profile
           of redemptions of gilts (green bars), which average 5.2 per cent of GDP. There is a
           particularly high volume of redemptions in the final year of the forecast (£229 billion). This
           reflects the maturity of a significant number of gilts issued during the Covid pandemic – £92
           billion of the record volume of gilts issued in 2020-21 and 2021-22 redeem in 2031-32.
           This is one factor that is pushing up debt interest costs in the final year of the forecast, as
           explained in paragraph 5.28, as these gilts are expected to be refinanced at higher interest
           rates.

6.23       More generally, the composition of gilts issued by the DMO in recent years has become
           skewed toward shorter maturities. As interest rates are lower at shorter maturities this has
           decreased interest costs for the Government in the near term. However, it increases the
           value of gilts that are redeemed and require refinancing in future, so increasing sensitivity to
           changes in rates. This is discussed in more detail in Box 6.1 in the November 2025 EFO.

6.24       As the APF unwinds and sells the gilts that it holds (yellow bars), this adds to the total gilts
           that must be held by the private sector (orange line),7 which contributes to an average of 4.3
           per cent of GDP each year over the forecast. This is more than 50 per cent higher than the
           average annual increase in private sector holdings of gilts between 2000-01 and 2022-23.


7
  The forecast assumes that the active sales of gilts held within the APF are made directly to the private sector. The Bank of England and
HM Treasury are considering a new model from April 2027, under which the DMO would instead purchase gilts sold by the APF, with
these gilts subsequently cancelled and the purchases financed by a corresponding amount issued by the DMO. If implemented, this would
shift the associated increase in private sector gilt holdings from APF sales to gross gilt issuance, while keeping overall private sector
holdings unchanged. A final decision on whether to proceed with this change has not been taken and so it is not reflected in our forecast.
See HM Treasury, Update on the Asset Purchase Facility (APF), September 2026.

Debt and other balance sheet aggregates
Public sector net debt
6.25                   Chart 6.7 shows that PSND is forecast to rise from 95.5 per cent of GDP in 2026-27 to
                       97.3 per cent of GDP in 2028-29, before declining to 95.6 per cent of GDP in the final year
                       of the forecast. The reduction in debt from 2029-30 onwards largely reflects the forecast
                       reduction in PSNB, with a one-off cash flow timing effect from the policy to introduce timely
                       payment for self assessment in 2029-30, and the one-off set of TFS loan repayments to the
                       Bank of England in 2030-31, described in more detail in paragraph 6.13. Compared to
                       our March forecast, net debt is 0.7 percentage points higher this year and on average 0.8
                       percentage points higher in every year throughout the forecast. This predominantly reflects
                       higher forecast PSNB as explained in paragraph 6.7.

Public sector net debt (excluding the Bank of England)
6.26                   Chart 6.7 also shows that public sector net debt excluding the Bank of England (PSND ex
                       BoE) is forecast to rise from 92.4 per cent of GDP in 2026-27 to peak in 2028-29 at 94.7
                       per cent of GDP, and then to decline to 94 per cent of GDP in the final year of the forecast
                       (right panel of the Chart 6.7). Compared to our March forecast, PSND ex BoE is higher by
                       0.1 per cent of GDP this year and lower by 0.1 per cent of GDP in 2030-31. Compared to
                       March, PSND ex BoE has not increased as much as PSND, reflecting a downward revision to
                       PSND ex BoE outturn in 2025-26 and the revised APF unwind profile, which raises headline
                       PSND by more than PSND ex BoE. The difference between PSND and PSND ex BoE as a
                       share of GDP narrows from 3.2 percentage points this year to just 1.6 percentage points in
                       2031-32 due to the winding down of the TFS and APF (see Box 6.2 for more detail).

          Box 6.2: The trajectories of PSND and PSND excluding the Bank of England
          Public sector net debt (PSND) is the stock of the public sector’s gross debt liabilities minus its
          liquid financial assets. In 2016, the ONS also produced a new measure of public sector net debt
          excluding the Bank of England (PSND ex BoE), which excludes assets and liabilities on the Bank
          of England’s balance sheet, with a historical series extending back to April 1997. Material
          differences between the two measures emerged in the 2010s, due to:
                    •   The Bank’s quantitative easing (QE) programme introduced in 2009. The creation of
                        central bank reserves to purchase assets for QE, held in the Asset Purchase Facility (APF),
                        initially increased PSND as the reserve liabilities came to have a higher value than the
                        assets.a
                    •   The Term Funding Scheme (TFS), which was introduced by the Bank in 2016 to support
                        bank lending. The loan assets held by the TFS are not counted as liquid assets in PSND,
                        while the reserves created by the Bank for this scheme are liabilities that increase PSND.
          These schemes therefore initially increased PSND and not PSND ex BoE. As Chart A shows, this
          led to a divergence between the two measures which widened as the schemes expanded over the
          2010s and early 2020s, with the gap reaching 13.1 per cent of GDP in 2021-22. Subsequently
          the gap has narrowed as the APF has been unwound and loans on the TFS repaid to reach 4.2
          per cent of GDP in 2025-26. We assume this will continue over the forecast period with the gap
          expected to narrow to 1.6 per cent of GDP in 2031-32. This means that, between 2021-22 and
          2025-26, PSND fell marginally as a share of GDP because the unwind of the APF and TFS more
          than offset the upward pressure on debt from high levels of government borrowing. By contrast,
          PSND ex BoE rose steadily over this period, though from a lower level, because the APF and TFS
          effects are excluded. PSND ex BoE therefore provides a better picture of the underlying trajectory
          of the fiscal position driven by government borrowing over this period.

 a
   The reserves created to finance APF gilt purchases are recorded in PSND as liabilities at their nominal value, while the gilt assets are
 recorded at their redemption value rather than the market price paid at purchase. As the gilts were purchased at prices above
 redemption value their valuation in PSND is lower than the valuation of the reserves, which leads to an increase in PSND.




Public sector net financial liabilities
6.27                     Public sector net financial liabilities (PSNFL) is the balance sheet measure that is targeted in
                         the Government’s fiscal rules. It includes non-debt liabilities such as funded pensions, and
                         additional illiquid financial assets such as loans (most notably student loans) and equity
                         (including the assets of funded pension schemes). In nominal terms, from a stock of £2.6
                         trillion in 2025-26, PSNFL is forecast to increase by £133 billion in 2026-27, £83 billion in
                         2029-30 and by smaller amounts in the following two years. Table 6.7 shows the year-on-
                         year changes in PSNFL are mainly driven by PSNB, as well as:

                         •    Valuation changes in funded pension schemes, which reduce PSNFL in 2026-27 by £8
                              billion, but increase it thereafter. This reflects a judgement that equity assets held
                              within the schemes will increase in value faster than the pension liabilities in 2026-27,
                              but that this effect reverses over the remainder of the forecast.

                         •    Asset Purchase Facility sales losses, incurred when gilts are sold for less than their
                              redemption value. These add to PSNFL by amounts rising year on year until 2029-30
                              and around £5 billion by 2031-32.

                         •    The discount on new issuance of central government gilts (gilt premia) pushes up
                              PSNFL by £8 billion in 2026-27 and £2.6 billion in 2027-28. This effect subsides, with
                              smaller increases of £0.9 billion a year on average thereafter, as gilt prices rise and
                              new debt is assumed to issued nearer to face value.


Economic and fiscal outlook                                         148
                                                                                        Fiscal aggregates




         •     Increases in the value of general government equity assets. These capture growth in
               public sector equity assets not held in funded pension schemes and include the equity
               holdings of PuFins. They reduce PSNFL by an average of £4.2 billion a year.

         •     Reserves and other valuation changes, which increase PSNFL by £2.5 billion in 2026-
               27 largely due to a reduction in the value of the UK’s foreign reserve assets. They have
               little effect thereafter as we assume a constant exchange rate.

6.28     As shown in Chart 6.8, PSNFL as a share of GDP is forecast to rise to 83.2 per cent in
         2026-27 and peak at 83.8 per cent in 2027-28, before falling to 83.1 per cent in 2029-30
         and then to 81.7 per cent by 2031-32. Relative to March, as shown in Table 6.8, PSNFL is
         higher in each year of the forecast, by 0.5 per cent of GDP in 2026-27, rising to 1.1 per
         cent by 2030-31. This is driven by a higher level of PSNFL in each year, partly offset by
         stronger nominal GDP growth over the forecast. These cash revisions reflect:

         •     Revisions to PSNFL outturn for 2025-26, which lower liabilities in each year of our
               forecast by £2.0 billion.

         •     Upwards revisions to pre-measures PSNB in each year, which add a total of £56
               billion to PSNFL by 2030-31.

         •     The effect of policy measures on PSNB, which increase PSNFL in each year until 2030-
               31, but reduce it by £0.1 billion by 2031-32.

         •     Valuation changes, which increase PSNFL by a total of £13 billion. This reflects
               valuation changes in funded pension schemes, which are only partly offset by faster
               growth in the value of general government equity assets.

Public sector net worth
6.29                   Public sector net worth (PSNW) is the broadest measure of the Government’s balance sheet,
                       capturing changes in the value of non-financial assets (NFAs) and the liabilities associated
                       with unfunded pension schemes, in addition to what is included in PSNFL. Inverted PSNW
                       falls by 3.5 per cent of GDP from 63.3 per cent in 2026-27 to 59.8 per cent in 2031-32,
                       driven by a 1.9 per cent of GDP rise in the value of NFAs. Compared to our March forecast,


Economic and fiscal outlook                                              150
                                                                                                                 Fiscal aggregates




                       inverted PSNW is 0.3 per cent of GDP lower in 2026-27, largely due to lower-than-forecast
                       outturn for 2025-26. This difference gradually reverses, leaving it 1.4 per cent of GDP
                       higher by 2030-31, reflecting the upwards revisions to our forecast for PSNFL, in
                       combination with faster forecast growth in unfunded pensions liabilities.

6.30                   Chart 6.9 shows that all four measures of the balance sheet, as a share of GDP, are

                       until 2028-29, before declining slightly in each subsequent year. PSNFL and PSNW both rise
                       until 2027-28 and fall slightly in the final four years. PSND, PSND ex BoE and PSNFL are
                       forecast to be higher than their 2025-26 levels at the forecast horizon, while PSNW is

6.31                   Box 6.3 discusses the impact of the possible reclassification of water companies for the
                       public sector balance sheets measures and for borrowing and the current budget deficit.




                                                                    151                           Economic and fiscal outlook
Fiscal aggregates




 Box 6.3: Potential impact of water companies on the public finances
 Water companies in England and Wales have been classified within the private sector since
 1989. The Office for National Statistics (ONS), however, is currently reviewing this classification
 in response to the Water (Special Measures) Act 2025, which increased the degree of
 government control over the operation of water companies.a,b The Government has also recently
 announced its intention to further increase public control of the industry, including by removing
 caps on government share ownership in water firms.c If the ONS were to reclassify, then the
 relevant assets, liabilities, revenue and expenditure of providers would be incorporated to the
 public sector.
 The 2025 Fiscal risks and sustainability report assessed the potential impact of reclassifying the
 full water sector on the public sector balance sheet in 2023-24, showing that this could:
     •   Increase gross liabilities, which increase PSNFL, by £91 billion (3.3 per cent of GDP),
         including £66 billion of debt. Borrowing by water companies has increased in recent
         years with around half (52 per cent) of debt inflation-linked.

     •   Increase financial assets, which all reduce PSNFL, by £12 billion (0.4 per cent of GDP).
         This includes £5 billion in cash held by water companies, the only financial assets which
         would reduce PSND.
     •   Overall, increase PSNFL by £78 billion (2.8 per cent of GDP). PSNW, as the widest
         measure of the balance sheet, would additionally include the £94 billion of water
         companies’ physical assets, including water pipelines, treatment facilities, and reservoirs
         which account for 88 per cent of their total assets.
 This increase in PSNFL would be incorporated as a one-off increase to its level at the point of
 reclassification. Following the initial increase in the level of PSNFL, only the level of borrowing by
 water companies would directly affect the trajectory of PSNFL, and so affect the Government’s
 supplementary fiscal target which is for PSNFL to be declining as a share of GDP in the third year
 of the forecast.

 Table B illustrates the potential impact of reclassification on the level of borrowing and the
 current budget deficit if the revenue and expenditure of providers were to be moved into the
 public sector, by using data from the outturn years between 2020-21 and 2024-25.d, e Within
 these years, we estimate that reclassification could:
     •   Increase public sector receipts by £13 billion (0.5 per cent of GDP) a year on average.
         Within this, revenue from consumer bills averaged £12 billion and interest receipts
         averaged £0.4 billion a year.
     •   Increase current spending by £11 billion (0.4 per cent of GDP) a year. Within this,
         operating costs, mainly comprising of wages and contracted services, averaged £7
         billion, and dividend and debt interest payments averaged £3.8 billion.

     •   Increase capital spending by £6 billion (0.2 per cent of GDP) a year. Most investment by
         water companies is on capital additions of property, plant and equipment.




Economic and fiscal outlook                      152
                                                                                                              Fiscal aggregates




    •    Increase the current budget deficit, in most years, by £1.0 billion a year (less than 0.1 per
         cent of GDP). This is because, in outturn, current spending and depreciation is largely
         offset by receipts, and most borrowing by water companies is to fund investment.
    •    Increase net borrowing by £4.4 billion (0.2 per cent of GDP) a year. The increase in
         borrowing rises from £0.2 billion in 2020-21 to £7 billion by 2024-25. This is because
         both capital and current spending have increased by more than receipts over this period.

The impact of reclassification on borrowing and the current budget deficit over the forecast
period would depend on the business plans of water companies at the time of reclassification,
and on any future policy or regulatory changes. Recent outturn data, discussed above, suggests
that it could have a small impact on the current budget deficit forecast as receipts largely offset
current spending and depreciation in the sector. However, the forecast for borrowing will also
reflect capital spending, which is set to remain at an elevated level over the next five years as
water companies invest to upgrade their capital stocks. The Ofwat Price Review 2024 has
approved £104 billion of expenditure (2022-23 prices) by water companies between 2025-26
and 2029-30.f This would point to higher current and capital spending compared to Table B,
averaging around £25 billion a year of total spending in nominal terms over this time period.

7     Fiscal context, targets and
      uncertainty


Introduction
7.1   In this chapter, we assess the Government’s performance against its fiscal rules alongside a
      wider analysis of fiscal sustainability. This includes an examination of the fiscal context, the
      main risks to the fiscal outlook, and long-term fiscal pressures. As such, in this chapter we:

      •    assess the fiscal context for this Budget, by summarising changes in public sector net
           borrowing and public sector net debt in the post-war period, comparing the UK fiscal
           position to that of other advanced economies, and assessing the outlook for longer-
           term fiscal sustainability in the UK;

      •    set out the Government’s fiscal targets and estimate their likelihood of being met
           under current policy conditions in our central forecast, and assess the fiscal position
           against wider metrics for fiscal sustainability; and

      •    examine key risks and uncertainties around the central forecast using analysis based
           on: an assessment of specific risks to the central forecast; patterns of shocks to, and
           variations in, key macroeconomic and fiscal determinants; and analysis of the impact
           on the fiscal forecast of alternative economic scenarios.

The fiscal context
Current and historical fiscal position
7.2   The left panel of Chart 7.1 shows that over the past two decades, public sector net
      borrowing (PSNB) as a share of GDP has, on average, been markedly higher than over the
      preceding 60 years. Borrowing has averaged 5.8 per cent of GDP in the period since the
      global financial crisis (5.3 per cent of GDP excluding the pandemic-hit year of 2020-21),
      three times the post-war average of 1.9 per cent of GDP in the period up until 2008.

7.3   Following the Covid pandemic, borrowing remained at around 5 per cent of GDP for four
      years until 2024-25. In the following two years, it has fallen and is now forecast to reach
      4.2 per cent of GDP in 2026-27. In the latest central forecast, under the government’s
      planned fiscal consolidation, borrowing is expected to fall further to 1.8 per cent of GDP by
      2031-32, which, if achieved, would be broadly in line with the post-war, pre-2008 average.
      The right panel of Chart 7.1 shows that in successive forecasts since the pandemic,
      governments have typically planned to reduce borrowing to the pre-2008 average of below


                                                155                      Economic and fiscal outlook
Fiscal context, targets and uncertainty




                       2 per cent of GDP. However, in practice over the period since then, there has so far only
                       been a slight decline in borrowing in outturn years to 4.4 per cent of GDP in 2025-26. This
                       has been as a result of the fiscal impact of subsequent economic shocks, weaker-than-
                       expected growth, and discretionary policy decisions to delay consolidation.

7.4                    The left panel of Chart 7.2 shows changes in public sector net debt, excluding the Bank of
                       England (PSND ex BoE), also referred to as underlying debt, as a share of GDP since 1948-
                       49. The three decades following the Second World War saw a steady fall from the very high
                       debt level of around 250 per cent of GDP reached immediately after the war. This fall was
                       supported by high real economic growth and a substantial fall in defence spending. From
                       the late 1970s, debt broadly stabilised between 20 and 40 per cent of GDP, and stood at
                       around 36 per cent on the eve of the financial crisis in 2007-08.

7.5                    The fiscal impact of the financial crisis led underlying debt to rise sharply to reach a peak of
                       79 per cent in 2014-15 before then falling slightly in the late 2010s. It then once again rose
                       markedly due to the impact of the pandemic to reach 86 per cent in 2020-21. Following the
                       pandemic, after an initial decline, underlying debt has continued to rise in all years, due to
                       the relatively elevated level of borrowing in this period described above, to reach 92 per
                       cent of GDP in 2026-27. This would be 57 percentage points of GDP higher than the pre-
                       financial-crisis level.

7.6                    The right panel of Chart 7.2 compares the forecast changes in underlying debt over the
                       post-pandemic period relative to actual outturn. Successive previous forecasts have expected
                       debt to fall or stabilise over the medium term. In practice, this has yet to occur for the same
                       reasons that the recent forecasts for falling borrowing, described above, have not yet been
                       delivered. In the latest forecast, PSND ex BoE is projected to peak in 2028-29 at 95 per cent
                       of GDP and then subsequently fall slightly. If this were to be achieved, it would still leave the
                       level of debt 7 percentage points of GDP higher in 2031-32 than the November 2022

Economic and fiscal outlook                                       156
                                                                                     Fiscal context, targets and uncertainty




                        forecast peak; 5 percentage points higher than the 2025-26 figure of 89 per cent of GDP;
                        and at elevated levels compared to the past 50 years.

International context
7.7                     As discussed above, unexpected shocks and low growth have contributed to elevated levels
                        of borrowing and significant increases in public debt in the last two decades. While these
                        factors have affected nearly all advanced economies, the UK has experienced a more
                        significant deterioration in the public finances than most. The left side of Chart 7.3 shows
                        that while general government net borrowing in the UK (blue line) has followed a similar
                        overall trend to other G7 (purple line) and advanced (yellow line) economies since 2006, it
                        has consistently been at a higher level and saw larger spikes in response to shocks. Between
                        2022 and 2026, UK net borrowing on an internationally comparable basis was 5.1 per cent
                        of GDP, 1.3 percentage points higher than the average for other G7 economies and 3.8
                        percentage points higher than the average for other advanced economies.

7.8                     Based on the IMF forecasts shown in these charts, borrowing in the UK is expected by the
                        IMF to decline by 2.4 per cent of GDP to 1.4 per cent in 2031 – which would be close to the
                        IMF’s forecasts for the average for other advanced economies.1 As described in paragraph
                        7.3, recent similar plans have subsequently been delayed due to economic shocks, weaker-
                        than-expected growth, and changes to government policy.

7.9                     The right panel of Chart 7.3 shows that the higher borrowing in the UK than in comparable
                        economies over the previous two decades has contributed to a larger increase in its debt
                        stock over time. General government net debt in the UK as a share of GDP (on an
                        internationally comparable basis) has gone from 8 percentage points higher than the

1
 See IMF, World economic outlook, October 2026. The IMF fiscal projection is based on our March 2026 forecast, adjusted for
differences in assumptions.


                                                                 157                              Economic and fiscal outlook
Fiscal context, targets and uncertainty




                       average for other advanced economies in 2006 to 53 percentage points higher in 2026.
                       Over this period among G7 economies, the UK has experienced the largest increase in net
                       debt as a share of GDP, increasing from 25 percentage points below the average of other
                       G7 economies in 2006 to 6 percentage points higher in 2026. Even with the fiscal
                       consolidation planned over the forecast period, the IMF expects UK general government net
                       debt to decline only marginally between 2026 and 2031, to end broadly level with the IMF’s
                       forecasts for the average of other G7 economies, and 54 percentage points higher than its
                       level in 2006.

                       Long-term sustainability of the public finances
7.10                   In the 2026 Fiscal risks and sustainability report, we explored the major sources of long-
                       term fiscal pressure and set out projections for the public finances over the next 50 years. In
                       nearly all of the long-term scenarios we produced, including under several different
                       assumptions for long-term policy settings, demographic and other cost pressures were
                       projected to eventually move borrowing and debt onto an ever-rising trajectory. The blue
                       line in Chart 7.4 shows how the upward trajectory of debt in the FRS baseline long-term
                       scenario starts to accelerate onto an unsustainable path in the 2040s.

7.11                   The baseline long-term scenario shown in Chart 7.4 took, as its starting point, the March
                       2026 forecast for a fiscal consolidation in which the primary balance moves from a deficit
                       of 1.4 per cent of GDP in 2025-26 to a surplus of 1.5 per cent of GDP in 2030-31.
                       However, Chart 7.1 showed that successive recent plans for fiscal consolidation have not yet
                       been delivered. If the long-term projections were instead based on the primary deficit in
                       2030-31 remaining at its 2025-26 level, the yellow line shows that debt is projected to start
                       to rise onto an unsustainable upward trajectory in the early 2030s. This highlights the
                       importance of early policy action to keep debt on a sustainable trajectory.

Economic and fiscal outlook                                       158
                                                                                               Fiscal context, targets and uncertainty




7.12                    Some of the pressures which drive the increase in debt in the FRS long-term scenarios, such
                        as the impact of an ageing population on state pension caseloads and declining tax
                        revenue from emissions-related taxes, are already starting to affect the fiscal position over
                        the medium term. Box 6.1 explains how some of these factors contribute to the increase in
                        borrowing in the pre-measures forecast between 2030-31 and 2031-32, underlining the
                        importance of early action to address these pressures.

7.13                    The Government’s decision to replace the state pension triple lock with an adjusted triple
                        lock from April 2030 should, in itself, reduce some of the long-term upward pressure on the
                        public finances that was identified in the 2026 FRS. Provisional analysis suggests that, if the
                        next 50 years are similar to the previous 35 years, the adjusted triple lock could reduce the
                        projected increase in debt by around 25 per cent of GDP in 2075-76 relative to the current
                        triple lock uprating. However, alongside this announcement the Government also set out an
                        ambition to expand public provision of social care. The overall impact of these
                        announcements on the outlook for long-term fiscal sustainability therefore depends on the
                        costs of the reforms to social care. These issues are discussed further in paragraphs 3.48 to
                        3.54.

Fiscal targets and wider indicators of fiscal sustainability
7.14                    The Budget Responsibility and National Audit Act 2011 requires the OBR to assess at least
                        once per financial year the extent to which the Government has achieved or is likely to
                        achieve its fiscal mandate under current policy. The Charter for Budget Responsibility states
                        that the fiscal mandate is:




                                                                        159                             Economic and fiscal outlook
Fiscal context, targets and uncertainty




        •     to have the current budget in surplus in 2029-30, until 2029-30 becomes the third
              year of the forecast period. From that point, the current budget must then remain in
              balance or in surplus from the third year of the rolling forecast periods.

7.15    There are also two supplementary targets in the Charter:

        •     to have debt, defined as public sector net financial liabilities (PSNFL), falling as a share
              of the economy in 2029-30, until 2029-30 becomes the third year of the forecast
              period. Debt should then fall by the third year of the rolling forecast period; and

        •     to ensure that expenditure on welfare (excluding the state pension and payments
              closely linked to the economic cycle) is contained within a predetermined cap and
              margin. The Charter sets out that the OBR will assess spending against the welfare cap
              and margin at the first Budget of each new Parliament.

7.16    The Charter also requires the OBR to provide an analysis of the risks surrounding the fiscal
        outlook, and to consider a wide range of factors and dimensions relating to the
        sustainability of the public finances. The Charter further states that the Treasury will consider
        a wide range of indicators in its management of fiscal policy.

Performance against the Government’s fiscal targets in the central forecast
7.17    Based on the central forecast and as shown in Table 7.1, the centres of the distribution of
        forecast outcomes for the current budget and the PSNFL-to-GDP ratio are such that:

        •     The fiscal mandate for the current budget to be in surplus in 2029-30, the third year of
              the forecast period, is met by a margin of 0.4 per cent of GDP (£15 billion). This
              compares to a margin of 0.6 per cent of GDP (£22 billion) when performance against
              the rules was last assessed alongside the November 2025 Budget.

        •     The supplementary target, for public sector net financial liabilities (PSNFL) to be falling
              as a per cent of GDP in 2029-30, is forecast to be met by a margin of 0.7 per cent of
              GDP (£24 billion), unchanged from a margin of 0.7 per cent of GDP (also £24 billion)
              at the November 2025 Budget.

        •     The welfare cap plus margin, set for 2029-30, is on course to not be met by a margin
              of £3 billion, as opposed to last November when it was forecast to be met by a margin
              of £2 billion.

Probability of meeting the fiscal targets
7.18       Chart 7.5 presents fan charts, based on stochastic simulations and historic deviations from
           central forecasts, of the probability distribution around the forecast for the current budget
           and PSNFL.2 These distributions enable us to assess the probability of the Government’s
           fiscal targets being met based on current policies. They show that currently:

           •      The probability of meeting the fiscal mandate to have the current budget in surplus in
                  2029-30 is now 56 per cent in the post-measures forecast. This is a slight reduction
                  from last November’s 59 per cent. With the shorter horizon to the target year
                  compared to last November, a smaller margin is required to have the same chance of
                  meeting the rules. Despite this, the probability has decreased overall because the
                  reduction in the margin more than offsets the effect of the shorter horizon. For the
                  probability of meeting the fiscal mandate to be increased to reach 60 or 70 per cent, it
                  would require the current budget forecast to be in surplus by around £25 and £50
                  billion, respectively, in 2029-30, instead of the current forecast margin of £15 billion.

           •      The probability of meeting the supplementary target, PSNFL falling as a share of GDP,
                  is now 54 per cent. The estimated likelihood has increased slightly from 52 per cent in

2
  The probability distributions for the current budget deficit are based on stochastic simulations, which generate thousands of potential
outcomes whose chances of occurring are based on randomly selected shocks drawn historical outturn. We use historical deviations from
previous forecasts to generate the probability distributions for PSNFL. We only began forecasting PSNFL in 2016 so there would be a
limited sample from which to generate stochastic simulations, and from a period which has seen relatively high variation in borrowing
and debt. More detail can be found in the October 2024 EFO and in Steel, D., OBR Working Paper No. 17: Evaluating forecast
uncertainty with stochastic simulations, December 2021.


                                                                   161                               Economic and fiscal outlook
Fiscal context, targets and uncertainty




                              November 2025, primarily due to a reduction in the uncertainty around the central
                              forecast for the target year having rolled forward from the fourth to the third year of
                              the forecast. For the probability of the supplementary target to be met by a 60 or 70
                              per cent chance, it would require PSNFL to be falling by 1.8 or 3.9 per cent of GDP in
                              2029-30, respectively, instead of the current forecast margin of a fall of 0.7 per cent
                              of GDP.

7.19                   Chart 7.5 illustrates the very wide range of possible outcomes around the central forecast
                       for the current deficit and PSNFL. In this forecast, the probability distribution suggests that
                       there is a 60 per cent chance that the current budget deficit falls between a deficit of 2.0 per
                       cent of GDP (around £70 billion) and a surplus of 2.8 per cent of GDP in the target year
                       2029-30 (around £100 billion). For PSNFL, there is a 60 per cent chance of it lying between
                       78 per cent of GDP and 90 per cent of GDP in 2029-30. This reflects the uncertainty
                       inherent in any medium-term fiscal forecast given the multiple economic factors that can
                       affect the public finances and the scale of the shocks that can hit the economy over such a
                       period. This is illustrated by the frequency with which large adverse shocks have hit the
                       economy over the last two decades, as outlined earlier in the chapter. These types of
                       downside risks mean that the probability distribution is weighted towards higher levels of
                       borrowing and debt. While risks are skewed to the downside, outcomes above and below
                       the median forecast remain broadly equally likely, based on current stated Government
                       policy.

Change in the margins against fiscal targets
7.20                   Chart 7.6 decomposes the change in margins for the current budget and PSNFL targets in
                       the central forecast compared to last November. The current budget has a reduced margin
                       of £7 billion, while PSNFL is unchanged:




Economic and fiscal outlook                                         162

                  •          Underlying pre-measures forecast changes between the Budget 2025 forecast and the
                             March 2026 forecast increased the margin by £8 billion for both the current budget
                             and the net liabilities target.3 This was largely driven by upward revisions in March to
                             the receipts forecast due to higher forecast equity prices, and a lower debt interest
                             spending forecast as a result of lower gilt rates and RPI inflation at that time.

                  •          The effects of policy measures announced between Budget 2025 and the March 2026
                             forecast lowered the current budget and PSNFL margins by £6 billion.

                  •          Underlying pre-measures forecast changes since the March forecast have reduced the
                             current budget margin by £11 billion and the PSNFL margin by £5 billion.3 This largely
                             reflects higher debt interest spending, mainly as a result of increased borrowing costs
                             and inflation, and higher welfare spending due to inflation, described further in
                             Chapter 6. The impact of pre-measures forecast changes on PSNFL is lower because
                             of a higher forecast for nominal GDP growth in 2029-30, which lowers PSNFL as a
                             share of GDP.4

                  •          The effect of policy included in this Budget, as set out in Chapter 3, increases both the
                             current budget and PSNFL margins by £2 billion and £3 billion respectively.

Margins against successive fiscal mandates
7.21               The margin against the Government’s fiscal mandate of £15 billion is around half of the
                   average margin that previous Chancellors have left against their fiscal mandate since 2010.
                   Chart 7.7 shows that at 56 per cent, the probability of meeting the target is judged to be
                   broadly in line with the mean and median since the pandemic. The £15 billion margin is
                   only slightly lower than the average revision to pre-measures borrowing in the third year of
                   the forecast since the pandemic, which is £18 billion.5 It is under half of the median three-
                   year-ahead difference between forecast and outturn, which is £34 billion.6

7.22               The margins shown in Chart 7.7 have often been against rules that targeted the fourth or
                   fifth year of the forecast period. At this forecast, the current Government’s fiscal mandate is
                   now assessed at the third year of the forecast. The Charter also requires the Government to
                   set detailed spending plans for three years ahead at spending reviews every two years. This
                   means that, from the next spending review, the Government will be required to have
                   detailed spending allocations for the year in which the fiscal targets bind. This should
                   reduce the uncertainty, for a given expected margin, around meeting these targets.

Welfare cap
7.24                      The welfare cap is a limit for government spending on certain social security benefits and
                          tax credits in the final year of a given Parliament. Since its introduction in 2014, the welfare
                          cap has been revised upwards frequently to account for inflation and changes in welfare
                          demand. The Charter states that the OBR will make a formal assessment of spending
                          against the welfare cap and margin at the first Budget of each new Parliament, and will
                          monitor welfare spending against the pathway and margin at each Budget before the
                          formal assessment.

7.25                      The Government set a welfare cap of £194.5 billion in 2029-30 at the October 2024
                          forecast, and a margin for the cap that rises each year to reach 5 per cent in 2029-30.
                          Spending against the welfare cap in 2029-30 has risen by £5 billion since the November
                          2025 forecast, largely due to higher in-work and health-related universal credit caseloads,
                          along with higher spending forecasts for supported housing. This results in the welfare cap
                          being on course not to be met by £3 billion once the margin is included.

Balance sheet and fiscal affordability metrics
7.26      The assessment of the Government’s performance against its medium-term fiscal targets
          provides limited information on the overall sustainability of the fiscal position. In recognition
          of this, the Charter sets out that the “OBR will consider a wide range of factors and
          dimensions relating to the sustainability of the public finances”. In this chapter, we have
          included analysis of the UK’s overall fiscal position compared to the past, provided
          comparisons of key fiscal indicators in the UK compared to other advanced economies, and
          summarised some of the key conclusions from our most recent analysis of long-term fiscal
          sustainability in the 2026 FRS. This section provides a summary assessment of a range of
          balance sheet, debt affordability and debt stabilisation metrics, which can also be used as
          indicators of fiscal sustainability.

7.27      Table 7.3 presents this dashboard of metrics that shows: first (top two panels), their levels
          and how these compare with the medians from 1967-68 to 2006-07 (the four decades
          preceding the financial crisis) and from 2007-08 to 2018-19; and second (lowest panel),
          whether they are improving or deteriorating in each year of the forecast. It shows that:

          •      Balance sheet stock measures all remain in a significantly worse position than both the
                 pre-2007 and the 2007-2019 medians across the forecast period.

          •      The financing and cost of debt measures are also generally in a worse position than
                 the pre-crisis median. The central government net cash requirement remains above the
                 pre-2007 median for the majority of the forecast period. Net interest costs are also
                 above the pre-2007 median as a share of GDP across the forecast period, and close
                 to the pre-2007 median as a share of revenue.

Economic and fiscal outlook                             166
                                                                                         Fiscal context, targets and uncertainty



           •      The primary balance (non-interest revenue minus non-interest spending) is expected to
                  be in a better position than the pre-financial crisis median from 2028-29 onwards. It is
                  forecast to improve from a 1.5 per cent of GDP deficit (£47 billion) in 2025-26 to a
                  1.7 per cent of GDP (£65 billion) surplus in 2031-32.

           •      However, the debt-stabilising primary balance, which is the level of the primary
                  balance at which debt remains constant as a share of GDP, is forecast to be a surplus
                  which rises across the forecast period. By contrast, in both the pre-financial crisis
                  period and the 2007-2019 period, governments could run deficits of 0.4 per cent of
                  GDP and 1.5 per cent of GDP respectively, and still stabilise debt.

           •      The difference between the primary balance and the debt-stabilising primary balance
                  gives the debt stabilisation gap. This gap is positive until 2028-29, which means that
                  debt is forecast to rise up to this year, but becomes negative from 2029-30 onwards
                  due to the forecast improvement in the primary balance, which means that debt is
                  forecast to be marginally falling in this period. The long-term trends and outlook for
                  the debt-stabilising primary balance are discussed further in Box 7.1.

 Box 7.1: Long-term trends in the growth-corrected interest rate, “R-G”
 A key indicator of fiscal sustainability is the growth-corrected interest rate, often referred to as ‘R-
 G’, which is the difference between the cost of servicing debt and the growth rate of the
 economy.a If the effective nominal interest rate (R) exceeds nominal GDP growth (G), so R-G is
 positive, then a primary surplus is required to stabilise debt as a share of GDP, all else equal.
 Conversely, when G exceeds R, and so R-G is negative, governments can, in principle, run a
 primary deficit without debt as a share of GDP rising. Together with the level of the debt stock
 and financial transactions, R-G determines the debt-stabilising primary balance.b When debt
 levels are high, a given positive level of R-G implies a larger debt-stabilising primary balance.

 Chart A shows trends in R-G and the debt-stabilising primary balance since the Second World
 War. Up to the end of the 1970s, growth was consistently higher than the effective interest rate
 on government debt, and so R-G was negative, which meant that the debt-stabilising primary
 balance was very low at, on average, -5 per cent. This facilitated the rapid reduction in debt as a
 share of GDP seen over this period. The 1980s saw nominal growth rates fall below nominal
 interest rates, and so R-G turned positive. It remained positive for most years up to the financial
 crisis in 2008, with an average value of 1 per cent over the period. However, a low stock of debt
 meant the debt-stabilising primary balance averaged close to zero per cent of GDP.

 Following the global financial crisis (GFC), the effective interest rate fell to an average of 3 per
 cent between 2009-10 and 2018-19, the lowest rate since the 1950s. While growth was also
 weak over much of this period, these very low interest rates meant that R-G turned negative,
 reaching -1.3 per cent in 2018-19. Despite this, debt levels rose, largely because the primary
 deficit averaged 2.1 per cent of GDP higher than the level required to keep debt stable.

 Since 2022, interest rates have risen sharply and economic growth has remained subdued. As a
 result, R-G has increased from -3.5 per cent in 2022-23 to -1.1 per cent in 2025-26. Market
 interest rates are currently higher than the effective interest rate on the stock of debt, so as the
 debt stock is refinanced over time the effective interest rate will rise towards the market rate. This
 contributes to the forecast increase in R-G from -0.2 per cent in 2027-28 to 0.4 per cent in
 2031-32.

 This would be a return to the generally positive levels of R-G seen in the three decades leading
 up to the financial crisis, even though market interest rates are currently around 1.7 per cent
 lower than the post-war average. However, the level of debt is now much higher than in the pre-
 financial crisis period which, along with an increased use of financial transactions, increases the
 debt-stabilising primary balance for a given level of R-G. The debt-stabilising primary balance is
 expected to rise from 0.4 per cent of GDP in 2025-26 to 1.5 per cent by 2031-32, 3 percentage
 points higher than the 2007-2019 median (Table 7.3).

Risk and uncertainty
7.28                         All economic and fiscal forecasts are inherently subject to significant risk and uncertainty. It
                             is inevitable that unforeseen events will drive economic and fiscal outcomes away from any
                             central forecast. In recent years, large negative shocks, consistently weak GDP growth and


                                                                        169                          Economic and fiscal outlook
Fiscal context, targets and uncertainty




        significant policy changes have generated substantial revisions to forecasts from one fiscal
        event to the next. We use a range of tools to highlight this uncertainty and to meet the
        requirement in the Charter that we provide analysis of the risks surrounding the fiscal
        outlook. These include:

        •     detailing specific risks to our economic and fiscal forecasts, focusing on those which
              appear most material at the time;

        •     fan charts that reflect the chances of shocks of different sizes – through stochastic
              simulations and past central forecast deviations from outturns – to illustrate the
              uncertainty around key economic and fiscal variables, including, as set out in
              paragraphs 7.18 and 7.19, the fiscal aggregates targeted in the Government’s fiscal
              rules;

        •     sensitivity analysis that shows the responsiveness of the path of the current budget
              deficit to changes in key forecast variables, including gilt yields and nominal GDP
              growth; and

        •     scenarios that illustrate the potential channels through which changes in energy prices
              could affect the public finances.

Specific risks
7.29    The most important specific sources of risk to the economy forecast include:

        •     The evolution of the Middle East conflict, and its implications for interest rates and
              inflation, which has sharply pushed up energy prices this year and led to disruption to
              wider international trade flows. Energy markets remain volatile – at the time of
              finalising this report, oil and gas futures pricing was close to the top of the range
              observed since the beginning of the Middle East conflict. From paragraph 7.33
              onwards, we explain the results of a detailed set of scenarios that illustrate the
              potential economic and fiscal implications of alternative paths for energy prices. This
              highlights that the fiscal impact is particularly dependent on changes in financial
              conditions and the extent of the pass-through of an inflation shock to wages, which
              would serve to boost nominal tax bases. This is particularly important given that
              personal tax thresholds are currently frozen. In addition, any inflationary shock would
              create further pressure on departmental spending by eroding real-terms Spending
              Review settlements.

        •     The outlook for productivity growth, a key driver of the economic and fiscal forecasts,
              is highly uncertain. The development and adoption of artificial intelligence could have
              a significant positive effect – our central forecast is for it to boost productivity growth by
              0.2 percentage points a year by the medium term – but the scale and timing of any
              effect is highly uncertain. An additional uncertainty relates to challenges in accurately
              measuring historical productivity. As discussed in Box 2.2, ONS revisions to historical
              average hours suggest that UK productivity growth slowed after the financial crisis, but


Economic and fiscal outlook                        170
                                                                 Fiscal context, targets and uncertainty




            that the magnitude of the slowdown was closer to international comparators than
            previously suggested. This was driven by average hours growth being materially lower
            than previously estimated. Any change to the outlook for productivity could have a
            significant impact on the public finances. In the November 2025 EFO, we illustrated
            that if annual productivity growth were 0.5 percentage points higher or lower in the
            medium term than our central forecast, borrowing would be around £50 billion lower
            or £40 billion higher by 2030-31.

       •    The outlook for the labour market, including from the effects of an ageing population
            and from migration, provides several sources of uncertainty. We assume that the
            ageing population will have a moderate adverse impact on average hours worked and
            labour market participation. However, the forecast for average hours worked is
            particularly uncertain due to recent historical revisions, as outlined above. There are
            also significant risks around the size and composition of future migration flows, given
            the post-Brexit change in migration regime and the recent spike and subsequent fall in
            net migration. In addition, there are risks around the outlook for unemployment. In the
            March 2026 EFO, we produced scenarios on the fiscal implications of alternative paths
            for the labour market. These suggested that if unemployment returned to its estimated
            equilibrium rate two years earlier than the central forecast, borrowing could be £16
            billion a year lower on average, or if it instead peaked at a higher 6.8 per cent,
            borrowing could be an average of £20 billion a year higher than our central forecast.

7.30   There are also an additional set of specific fiscal and policy-related risks:

       •    The cost of servicing government debt – which had already increased sharply since
            2021 before rising further following the onset of the conflict in Iran. As explained in
            Chapter 2, UK government borrowing costs have been volatile recently with the 10-
            year yield ranging from around 4.5 to 5.5 per cent since March. But yields within this
            range are not unusual by historical standards. As a result of the recent rise in yields,
            debt interest spending is now forecast to rise to 4.2 per cent of GDP (£160 billion) in
            2031-32, compared to 1.7 per cent of GDP (£39 billion) in 2019-20. Paragraph 7.32
            sets out that a 1 percentage point change in gilt yields and Bank Rate expectations
            across the forecast in either direction could lead to an increase or decrease of £16
            billion in the current budget deficit in the medium term.

       •    There continue to be a wide range of pressures on departmental spending. These
            include higher inflation since March, which reduces real-terms departmental spending
            within fixed nominal spending limits, and the risk of higher-than-expected pay
            settlements. Beyond the current spending review, after accounting for spending
            commitments in areas including the NHS, defence and schools, the total spending
            assumptions currently set by the Government for the likely next spending review period
            imply ‘unprotected’ areas of day-to-day spending could face a 2.6 per cent a year
            real-terms reduction in the final three years of the forecast. Spending would have to be
            £11 billion higher in 2031-32 for ‘unprotected’ spending not to fall in real terms from
            2028-29. The Government has set out a longer-term ambition to reform social care,
            which the Health Foundation previously estimated could cost between £3.5 billion and


                                                 171                       Economic and fiscal outlook
Fiscal context, targets and uncertainty




                   £17 billion a year.7 The commitment to increase defence spending to 3.5 per cent of
                   GDP by 2035 would cost around £35 billion in 2026-27 terms.

           •       There is significant uncertainty around the outlook for welfare spending, especially on
                   disability- and health-related benefits, where caseloads have increased sharply since
                   the pandemic. We continue to assume that the caseloads will rise in the forecast, but at
                   a slower rate than that seen to date. If caseloads were to continue to grow at their
                   post-pandemic rate, spending in 2031-32 would be roughly £25 billion higher than in
                   our central forecast in 2031-32, but if pre-pandemic rates were restored, then
                   spending could be around £13 billion lower in 2031-32.

           •       The forecast rise in the tax-to-GDP ratio accounts for around three-fifths of the current
                   planned medium-term consolidation of the public finances. However, there are several
                   risks around this forecast. Much of the increase is from rising income tax and national
                   insurance contributions which is highly sensitive to growth in nominal earnings,
                   especially with personal tax thresholds frozen until 2030-31. Capital taxes also
                   account for a significant portion of the forecast rise, but they are highly dependent on
                   future equity prices and are sensitive to behavioural responses from affected taxpayers
                   who are more typically a more mobile part of the tax base. Further, the forecast
                   assumes that the indexation of fuel duty and reversal of the 5p cut will be implemented
                   in July 2027. The forecast also assumes the tax gap – a measure of tax compliance –
                   will decline over the forecast period following the recent period where it has remained
                   broadly flat after rising in the 2010s. More broadly, a higher level of tax take increases
                   the risk that incentives within the tax system distort or constrain economic activity by
                   more or less than expected.

7.31       Some steps have been taken to address previously identified fiscal risks. As discussed in
           more detail in Chapter 3, the Government has announced it will replace the state pension
           triple lock with an adjusted triple lock from April 2030 which should significantly reduce the
           projected increase in the cost of the state pension over the long term. Additionally, as set out
           above, the fiscal mandate now targets the third year of the forecast. This means that, from
           the next spending review, the Government will be required to have detailed spending
           allocations for the year in which the fiscal targets bind which should reduce the risks around
           meeting these targets.

Sensitivities
7.32       Sensitivity analysis estimates how unit changes to key forecast variables would, in isolation,
           affect the current budget deficit relative to our central forecast.8 Chart 7.9 shows that:

           •       A 0.1 percentage point reduction in nominal GDP growth from 2027-28 is estimated to
                   increase the current budget deficit by £5 billion in 2029-30 and £9 billion in 2031-32.

Scenarios
7.33                       Energy prices have been volatile recently and their future evolution will be important to the
                           economic and fiscal outlook, with significant implications for inflation, interest rates and
                           GDP growth. To explore these factors, we present two alternative scenarios for energy
                           prices, shown in Chart 7.10. The energy price paths are calibrated using an option-implied
                           distribution of market pricing, taking one standard deviation above or below the central
                           path implied by futures in the near term:

                           •     In our severe shock scenario, oil prices peak at around $115 a barrel in the first half
                                 of 2027 and the shock is more persistent than the market pricing in our central
                                 forecast, with prices remaining at or above $100 a barrel throughout 2027 and
                                 staying elevated until 2031. Gas prices reach a peak of 275 pence a therm, with
                                 prices remaining over 150 pence a therm throughout 2027 before gradually returning
                                 to the central forecast path. In both cases, the peak is near the highs observed
                                 following the 2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine.

                           •     In our orderly recovery scenario, oil and gas prices fall back more quickly than
                                 assumed in our central forecast, and converge on our March 2026 forecast path in the
                                 medium term. Oil prices reach a trough of around $55 a barrel in the second quarter
                                 of 2027 and gas prices a trough of around 55 pence a therm during the third quarter,
                                 and both return to their March 2026 forecast path in 2029.

7.34                       We take a layered approach to illustrate the potential channels through which these
                           alternative paths may affect the economic and fiscal outlook, rather than providing a single
                           estimate of the possible impact. In both scenarios, we first set out the potential direct impact
                           on the economy and public finances, and then layer on the potential implications for interest
                           rates and gilt yields. In the severe shock scenario, we set out further potential implications


Economic and fiscal outlook                                                 174
                                                                                     Fiscal context, targets and uncertainty




           arising from second-round effects in inflation and increased pressures on departmental
           spending plans arising from higher inflation. The scenarios were informed by the Oxford
           Economics Global Economic Model, alongside our own judgement.

         Severe shock
7.35       We set out four channels of potential impacts on the economy and public finances in the
           severe shock scenario (for which the key economic implications are shown in Chart 7.11):

           •      The pure energy shock leads to a peak in CPI inflation of around 6 per cent next year
                  – about 2¼ percentage points above our central forecast – and a reduction in GDP
                  growth in 2027 to below ½ a per cent – ¾ of a percentage point below our central
                  forecast. In 2031, the level of real GDP is ¼ per cent lower than in our central
                  forecast, though the level of nominal GDP is slightly higher. RHDI per person is
                  persistently lower than our central forecast, and only recovers to its pre-pandemic level
                  after a decade, in 2029-30. By assumption, there is no adjustment in interest rates.

           •      We then illustrate a potential tightening in financial conditions resulting from the
                  shock. Relative to our central forecast, we assume an additional upward adjustment to
                  Bank Rate of around 1 percentage point by the second half of 2027, and a persistent
                  increase in gilt yields of around 1 percentage point.10 This leads to a further reduction
                  in real GDP growth in 2027.

           •      Possible second-round effects increase nominal earnings growth by an average of ½
                  of a percentage point each year from 2027 to 2029, and CPI inflation by an average
                  ¼ of a percentage point. This results in a further upward adjustment in Bank Rate and
                  gilt yields and increases the size of the nominal economy and key tax bases. But it has
                  little impact on the path of real GDP growth or RHDI per person. Both whether second-
                  round effects emerge and their potential scale are highly uncertain.

           •      We also illustrate the implied erosion in real departmental spending power due to the
                  higher inflation. This is the additional spending that would be required to maintain the
                  same real-terms value of departmental spending as in our central forecast. Any
                  adjustment would not happen automatically as departmental spending limits are set in
                  nominal terms and are a discretionary policy choice for the Government.




10
   In these scenarios, we model the Bank Rate adjustment using the “contemporaneous Taylor-type rule (headline inflation)” from the Bank
of England April MPR, and assume this converges to the Bank Rate path in our central forecast in the medium term. In practice, changes
to Bank Rate are based on the collective judgement of the Monetary Policy Committee. We model the adjustment in gilt yields assuming a
one percentage point increase in the risk premium alongside an estimate of the present value of changes in the Bank Rate, and use the
10-year mark as a proxy for the curve given the average maturity of UK public debt.

7.36                       Chart 7.12 shows the potential implications for borrowing from each of these different
                           channels in the severe shock scenario relative to our central forecast:

                           •      The pure energy shock is estimated to add £7 billion to borrowing in 2027-28, as
                                  increased spending on index-linked gilts more than offsets higher oil and gas
                                  revenues. It adds £4 billion in 2031-32 as higher welfare spending more than offsets
                                  a small increase in receipts arising from a larger nominal economy.11

                           •      Tightening financial conditions could add a further £11 billion, on average, to
                                  borrowing over the forecast, due to higher debt interest costs.

                           •      Second-round effects could reduce borrowing by increasing amounts, reaching £13
                                  billion in 2031-32, as domestically generated inflation from wages pushes up receipts
                                  by more than spending, particularly due to frozen personal tax thresholds.

                           •      Taken together, all these factors could increase borrowing by around £6 billion in
                                  2031-32 relative to the central forecast. The pressures on the public finances would be
                                  compounded by a £13 billion erosion in real-terms departmental spending power.12

7.37              We set out two channels of impact for the economy and public finances in the orderly
                  recovery scenario.13 The implications for CPI inflation and real GDP growth are shown in
                  Chart 7.13:

                  •       The pure energy price fall results in more rapid disinflation than in our central forecast,
                          with CPI inflation falling to 2¼ per cent in 2027 (1¼ percentage points lower than the
                          central forecast). There is higher real GDP growth of 1½ per cent in 2027 (½ of a
                          percentage point above our central forecast) while the lower inflation largely reverses
                          the near-term decline in RHDI per person seen in our central forecast. By 2031, the
                          level of real GDP is ¼ of a per cent higher than in our central forecast, while the level
                          of nominal GDP is similar. As in the severe shock scenario, there is no adjustment in
                          interest rates by assumption.

                          halfway back to the March EFO forecast levels. This has only modest implications for
                          real GDP growth.

7.38              Chart 7.14 shows the implications for PSNB of the orderly recovery scenario relative to our
                  central forecast. These are modest compared to the severe shock scenario, as the near-term
                  change in energy prices is smaller. The fiscal implications of the pure fall in energy prices
                  largely track inflation. Lower inflation reduces debt interest spending on index-linked gilts in
                  2027-28, but then debt interest spending is marginally higher in the two subsequent years
                  because inflation is slightly higher than in the central forecast. By 2031-32, borrowing is
                  broadly unchanged, as lower welfare spending due to lower inflation offsets lower receipts
                  from a smaller nominal economy. Financial easing further reduces borrowing by £5 billion
                  on average over the forecast period, due to the lower path for Bank Rate and gilt yields.

      our March 2026 Economic and fiscal outlook. These tables include:

           forecast;

      •    public sector current receipts and individual taxes;

      •    contributions to total managed expenditure;

      •    the main fiscal aggregates; and

      •    sources of year-on-year changes in balance sheet aggregates.
